ODE/IQFT correspondence for the generalized affine 𝔰 ​ 𝔩 ​ ( 2 ) \mathfrak{sl}(2) Gaudin model arXiv: 2106.01238
Gleb A. Kotousov, Sergei L. Lukyanov

Abstract
An integrable system is introduced, which is a generalization of the 𝔰 ​ 𝔩 ​ ( 2 ) \mathfrak{sl}(2) quantum affine Gaudin model. Among other things, the Hamiltonians are constructed and their spectrum is calculated using the ODE/IQFT approach. The model fits into the framework of Yang-Baxter integrability. This opens a way for the systematic quantization of a large class of integrable non-linear sigma models. There may also be some interest in terms of Condensed Matter applications, as the theory can be thought of as a multiparametric generalization of the Kondo model.



1
Introduction

Suppose we are a given a set of quantum spins
\vec{S}^{(a)}=\big(S^{(a)}_{1},S^{(a)}_{2},S^{(a)}_{3}\big)
:

\displaystyle\big[S^{(a)}_{A},S^{(b)}_{B}\,\big]={\rm i}\,\delta_{ab}\ {\varepsilon_{ABC}}\ S^{(a)}_{C}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \big(\vec{S}^{(a)}\big)^{2}={\mathfrak{j}}_{a}({\mathfrak{j}}_{a}+1)

(1.1)

with
a=1,\ldots,r
.
As was pointed out by Gaudin
Gaudin
;
Gaudin1
,
the operators

\displaystyle{\bf{H}}^{(a)}=2\ \sum_{b=1\atop b\not=a}^{r}\frac{\vec{S}^{(a)}\cdot\vec{S}^{(b)}}{z_{a}-z_{b}}\ :\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \sum_{a=1}^{r}{\bf H}^{(a)}=0

(1.2)

mutually commute for an arbitrary choice of the parameters

\{z_{a}\}_{a=1}^{r}
. They
also commute with any projection
of the total spin operator
{\vec{S}}=\sum_{a=1}^{r}{\vec{S}}^{(a)}
and, furthermore,

{\vec{S}}^{2}
is linearly expressed through the Hamiltonians:

\displaystyle{\vec{S}}^{\,2}=\sum_{a=1}^{r}z_{a}{\bf H}^{(a)}+\sum_{a=1}^{r}{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}({\mathfrak{j}}_{a}+1)\ .

(1.3)

It turns out that
the problem of the simultaneous diagonalization of
{\bf H}^{(a)}

can be “solved” within the framework of the
Bethe ansatz
Gaudin
;
Gaudin1
. In this approach the
energy
E_{a}
of the
a
-th Hamiltonian is expressed as

E_{a}=\sum_{b=1\atop b\not=a}^{r}\frac{2{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}\mathfrak{j}_{b}}{z_{a}-z_{b}}\,-\,\sum_{m=1}^{\tt{M}_{+}}\frac{2{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}}{z_{a}-x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}_{m}}\ ,

(1.4)

where the set of auxiliary parameters
\{x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}_{m}\}_{m=1}^{{\tt M}_{+}}

is determined through
the solution of the system of algebraic equations

\sum_{a=1}^{r}\frac{\mathfrak{j}_{a}}{z_{a}-x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}_{m}}\,-\,\sum_{n=1\atop n\not=m}^{{\tt M}_{+}}\frac{1}{x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}_{n}-x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}_{m}}=0\qquad\qquad(m=1,2,\ldots,\tt{M}_{+})\ .

(1.5)

The integer
{\tt M}_{+}
takes all possible values from
0
to
2\sum_{a=1}^{r}\mathfrak{j}_{a}
.

The Gaudin model admits an almost straightforward generalization to any
simple Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
(see sec.13.2.2 in
Gaudin1
and ref.
Gurco
).
The development of the mathematical apparatus of
2D
Conformal Field Theory
led to the idea that there should be a
meaningful generalization to the case when the finite-dimensional Lie algebra
is replaced by an affine Kac-Moody algebra
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
.
Then the Hilbert space would be built out of
Verma modules for an algebra of extended conformal symmetry.
According to general principles of integrability in CFT
Zam
,
the diagonalization problem would be
formulated for an infinite set
\{{\bf I}_{s}\}
of so-called local
Integrals of Motion (IM), which
depend on the arbitrary parameters
\{z_{a}\}_{a=1}^{r}
.
These would mutually commute, while by local what is meant is that

{\bf{I}}_{s}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\mbox{d}u}{2\pi}\ T_{s+1}(u)\,,

(1.6)

where
T_{s+1}
is a chiral local field of Lorentz spin
s+1
.

An important step in exploring the affine Gaudin model was made by Feigin and Frenkel in ref.
Feigin:2007mr
.
The key idea came from an interesting link between the original Gaudin model described above
and a certain class of linear
differential equations of the form
Gaudin1
;
Feigin:1994in
;
Frenkel:2004qy

\displaystyle\big(-\partial_{z}^{2}+t_{0}(z)\big)\,\Psi=0\ \ \ \ \qquad{\rm with}\ \ \ \qquad t_{0}(z)=\sum_{a=1}^{r}\bigg(\frac{\mathfrak{j}_{a}\,(\mathfrak{j}_{a}+1)}{(z-z_{a})^{2}}+\frac{E_{a}}{z-z_{a}}\bigg)\ .

(1.7)

The ODE possesses
r
regular singular points at
z=z_{a}
.
If we further assume that

\displaystyle\sum_{a=1}^{r}E_{a}=0

(1.8)

then
z=\infty
is also a regular singularity so that (
1.7
)
is a Fuchsian differential equation.
A remarkable phenomenon occurs when the residues

\{E_{a}\}_{a=1}^{r}
in the potential
t_{0}(z)

coincide with the set of energies corresponding to some common eigenvector of the
Hamiltonians
{\bf H}^{(a)}
.
In this case all the singular points at
z=z_{a}

turn out to be apparent.
1
1

1

Recall that a singularity
z_{a}
is called apparent
if the ratio of any two solutions
of the ODE is single valued in the vicinity of that point.

The roots
x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}_{m}
of the algebraic system (
1.5
),
such that (
1.4
) is satisfied for the given set

\{E_{a}\}_{a=1}^{r}
, have a simple interpretation.
They are the zeroes of the function

\Psi_{+}(z)=\frac{\prod_{m=1}^{{\tt M}_{+}}(z-x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}_{m})}{\prod_{a=1}^{r}(z-z_{a})^{\mathfrak{j}_{a}}}\,,

(1.9)

which is a solution of the ODE (
1.7
).
There is another linearly independent solution of the form

\Psi_{-}(z)=z\ \frac{\prod_{m=1}^{\tt{M}_{-}}(z-{x}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(-)}}_{m})}{\prod_{a=1}^{r}(z-z_{a})^{\mathfrak{j}_{a}}}\ \qquad\qquad\ \qquad\ \ \Big(\,{\tt M}_{+}+{\tt M}_{-}=2\sum_{a=1}^{r}\mathfrak{j}_{a}\,\Big)\,.

(1.10)

Here the set
\{x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(-)}}_{m}\}_{m=1}^{{\tt M}_{-}}
also solves the Bethe ansatz like equations,

\frac{1}{{x}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(-)}}_{m}}+\sum_{a=1}^{r}\frac{\mathfrak{j}_{a}}{z_{a}-{x}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(-)}}_{m}}-\sum_{n=1\atop n\not=m}^{{\tt M}_{-}}\frac{1}{{x}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(-)}}_{n}-{x}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(-)}}_{m}}=0\qquad\qquad(m=1,2,\ldots,\tt{M}_{-})\ ,

(1.11)

while

E_{a}=-\frac{2{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}}{z_{a}}+\sum_{b=1\atop b\not=a}^{r}\frac{2{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}\mathfrak{j}_{b}}{z_{a}-z_{b}}\,-\,\sum_{m=1}^{{\tt M}_{-}}\frac{2{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}}{z_{a}-{x}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(-)}}_{m}}\ .

(1.12)

Thus there is a link between the spectrum of the Gaudin Hamiltonians and a class of
differential equations possessing certain monodromy properties.
This provides, perhaps, one of the simplest illustrations of a broad phenomena,
known as the ODE/IQFT correspondence
Voros:1994
;
Dorey:1998pt
;
Bazhanov:1998wj
;
Bazhanov:2003ni
.
2
2

2

The term ODE/IM correspondence, where
IM stands for integrable model, is also used
in the literature. However, we find that the latter
tends to be heavily focused on
formal properties of ODEs at the expense
of a clear study of the quantum theory which, in our opinion,
is the most interesting part of the relation. Recently, there has been a great
flux of works that introduce and discuss different sorts of
“correspondences”, which are essentially ODE/IM.
In order to emphasize that our main motivation is the
study of integrable quantum field theory, rather than
abstract “integrable models”, we use the term ODE/IQFT.

In ref.
Feigin:2007mr
Feigin and Frenkel
introduce the Hamiltonians, which can be interpreted as an “affinization”
of
{\bf H}^{(a)}
(
1.2
).
They are built from
r
independent
copies of the affine Kac-Moody
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{k_{a}}(2)
algebra
at levels
k_{a}=1,2,\ldots\
. The currents would obey the operator
product expansions of the form

\displaystyle J^{(a)}_{A}(u)J^{(b)}_{B}(0)=-\delta_{ab}\ \bigg(\,\frac{k_{a}}{2u^{2}}\ \eta_{AB}+\frac{{\rm i}}{u}\ {f_{AB}}^{C}\,J_{C}^{(a)}\,\bigg)+O(1)\ .

(1.13)

To each copy one can associate the Virasoro field,

\displaystyle G^{(a)}=\frac{\eta^{AB}J_{A}^{(a)}J_{B}^{(a)}}{k_{a}+2}=\frac{1}{4\,(k_{a}+2)}\ \Big(J_{0}^{(a)}J_{0}^{(a)}+2\,J_{+}^{(a)}J_{-}^{(a)}+2\,J_{-}^{(a)}J_{+}^{(a)}\Big)

(1.14)

(here
\eta^{AB}
stands for the Killing form, while
\eta^{AC}\,\eta_{CB}=\delta^{A}_{B}
).
Then the Hamiltonians of the affine Gaudin model are given by

{\bf H}_{{\rm\scriptscriptstyle G}}^{(a)}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\mbox{d}u}{2\pi}\ \sum_{b=1\atop b\neq a}^{r}\,\frac{k_{b}\,G^{(a)}+k_{a}\,G^{(b)}-2\eta^{AB}J_{A}^{(a)}J_{B}^{(b)}}{z_{a}-z_{b}}\ .

(1.15)

Feigin and Frenkel put forward the
conjecture that the spectrum of these operators would be encoded in
a class of differential equations that generalizes (
1.7
),
though they did not explain exactly how the spectrum would be extracted from the ODEs.
The last point was clarified in refs.
Lacroix:2018fhf
;
Lacroix:2018itd
.

In this work we introduce and study the model, which possesses
an infinite set of mutually commuting local integrals of motion. The simplest ones,
the “Hamiltonians”, are expressed in terms of the
\mathfrak{sl}(2)

Kac-Moody currents and Virasoro field (
1.14
) as

\displaystyle{\bf{H}}^{(a)}_{\rm gen}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\mbox{d}u}{2\pi}\,\bigg[\,\frac{\beta^{2}K}{1-\beta^{2}}\ G^{(a)}+\frac{1}{4K}\ \frac{1-\beta}{1+\beta}\ \Big(\,k_{a}\,\big(J_{0}^{(\rm tot)}\big)^{2}-K\,J_{0}^{(a)}J_{0}^{(\rm tot)}\,\Big)

(1.16)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\sum_{b=1\atop b\neq a}^{r}\frac{1}{z_{a}-z_{b}}\,\Big(\,\frac{1}{4}\,(z_{a}+z_{b})\,J^{(a)}_{0}J^{(b)}_{0}+z_{a}\,J_{+}^{(b)}J_{-}^{(a)}+z_{b}\,J_{+}^{(a)}J_{-}^{(b)}-k_{a}z_{b}\,G^{(b)}-k_{b}z_{a}\,G^{(a)}\Big)\,\bigg],

where

J_{0}^{({\rm tot})}=\sum_{a=1}^{r}J_{0}^{(a)}\qquad\qquad{\rm and}\qquad\qquad K=\sum_{a=1}^{r}k_{a}\ .

(1.17)

Notice that

\frac{1-\beta^{2}}{\beta^{2}K}\ \sum_{a=1}^{r}\,{\bf H}^{(a)}_{\rm gen}\,=\,\sum_{a=1}^{r}\,\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\mbox{d}u}{2\pi}\ G^{(a)}\,,

(1.18)

which can be thought of as the affine counterpart of eq. (
1.3
).

The operators (
1.16
) depend on the parameter
\beta
.
The Hamiltonians of the affine Gaudin model (
1.15
) are obtained
through a certain limiting procedure, which includes taking
\beta\to 1^{-}
.
We formulate the ODE/IQFT correspondence for the model and explain
how the spectrum
of
{\bf H}^{(a)}_{\rm gen}
for arbitrary
\beta\in(0,1)
can be extracted from
the differential equations.
The theory will be referred to as the
Generalized Affine Gaudin Model (GAGM).

The GAGM fits within the framework of the standard
Yang-Baxter integrability. In particular,
the Hamiltonians
{\bf H}^{(a)}_{\rm gen}
are part of a large commuting family
which, as usual, involves the quantum transfer-matrices and Baxter

Q
-operators. These are explicitly constructed along the lines
of the BLZ approach
Bazhanov:1994ft
;
Bazhanov:1996dr
;
Bazhanov:1998dq
.
It is proposed that the GAGM governs the critical
behaviour of a lattice system, which in the simplest case coincides with the
inhomogeneous six-vertex model introduced by Baxter
Baxter:1971cs
. The
local Boltzmann weights are contained in the
R
-matrix that is
the trigonometric solution of the Yang-Baxter equation.
The anisotropy parameter entering into the
R
-matrix,
commonly referred to as
q
, is related to
the parameter
\beta
in (
1.16
) as

q=-\,\mbox{e}^{\frac{{\rm i}\pi}{K}(\beta^{2}-1)}\,.

(1.19)

In the limit
\beta\to 1^{-}
the trigonometric
R
-matrix becomes the rational one.

The paper is organized as follows.
Sections
2
-
5
contain no
new material and their purpose is to illustrate
the ideas of the BLZ approach. Its main ingredient is
a realization of the Borel subalgebra of the quantum algebra

U_{q}\big(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)\big)
in terms of the
vertex operators.
We use the example from ref.
Lukyanov:2006gv
as it
contains the essential blueprints for the construction
of the commuting family of operators in the GAGM
as well as the ODE/IQFT correspondence.
The realization of the
Borel subalgebra of
U_{q}\big(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)\big)
,
which gives rise to the commuting family for the GAGM,
is presented in sec.
6
. The next section is
a central one and contains a detailed discussion of
the ODE/IQFT correspondence for the model. Some comments
concerning the literature are also presented therein.
The way the spectrum of the local and non-local integrals of motion
are extracted from the ODE is described in sec.
8
.
Sections
9
and
10
deal with some
specific cases and provide an illustration of
the rather abstract ideas that preceded them.
The Hamiltonians
{\bf{H}}^{(a)}_{\rm gen}

(
1.16
)
are deduced in sec.
11
.
Also considered are certain limits of the model such as the isotropic, classical
as well as the limit,
which yields the Hamiltonians of the affine Gaudin model (
1.15
).
Finally, we sketch how the GAGM appears in the scaling
limit of the Baxter-type statistical systems in sec.
12
.

2
Quantum transfer-matrices

The algebraic structure underlying
the Yang-Baxter relation
was clarified within the theory of quasi-triangular Hopf algebras by Drinfeld
Drinfeld1986
.
A basic example
is when the Hopf algebra is
U_{q}(\widehat{\mathfrak{g}})

– the
quantum deformation of the universal enveloping algebra of
the affine algebra
Drinfeld1986
;
Jimbo:1985zk
.
The central rôle is played by the universal
R
-matrix, which lies in the
tensor product
U_{q}(\widehat{\mathfrak{g}})\otimes U_{q}(\widehat{\mathfrak{g}})

and satisfies the relation

{\cal R}^{12}\,{\cal R}^{13}\,{\cal R}^{23}\,=\,{\cal R}^{23}\,{\cal R}^{13}\,{\cal R}^{12}\ .

(2.1)

An important feature of
{\cal R}
is that it is decomposed as

{\cal R}\in U_{q}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{b}}}_{+})\otimes U_{q}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{b}}}_{-})
, where

U_{q}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{b}}}_{\pm})
stand for the Borel subalgebras of
U_{q}(\widehat{\mathfrak{g}})
.
In this paper we restrict to the case

{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{sl}(2)
.

Let us consider the evaluation homomorphism of
U_{q}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{g}}})
to the loop algebra

U_{q}({\mathfrak{g}})[\lambda,\lambda^{-1}]
and specify a finite dimensional matrix representation
\pi
of

U_{q}(\mathfrak{g})
.
In the case under consideration the Borel subalgebra
U_{q}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{b}}}_{+})
is generated by four elements,

\{y_{0},y_{1},h_{0},h_{1}\}
,
and its evaluation homomorphism is defined by

y_{0}\mapsto\lambda q^{\frac{{\tt h}}{2}}\,{\tt e}_{+}\ ,\qquad y_{1}\mapsto\lambda\,q^{-\frac{{\tt h}}{2}}\,{\tt e}_{-}\ ,\qquad h_{0}\mapsto{\tt h}\ ,\qquad h_{1}\mapsto-{\tt h}\ .

(2.2)

Here
{\tt h},{\tt e}_{\pm}
are the generators of
U_{q}\big({\mathfrak{sl}}(2)\big)
,
subject to the commutation relations

\displaystyle[\,{\tt h},{\tt e}_{\pm}\,]=\pm 2\,{\tt e}_{\pm}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ [\,{\tt e}_{+},{\tt e}_{-}\,]=\frac{q^{\tt h}-q^{-{\tt h}}}{q-q^{-1}}\ .

(2.3)

Then

\displaystyle{\bm{L}}_{\ell}(\lambda)=\big(\pi_{\ell}(\lambda)\otimes 1\big)[{\cal R}]\qquad\qquad\qquad\big(\ell=\tfrac{1}{2},1,\tfrac{3}{2},\ldots\big)

(2.4)

is a

U_{q}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{b}}}_{-})
-valued

(2{\ell}+1)\times(2{\ell}+1)
matrix
whose entries depend on an auxiliary parameter
\lambda
.
In turn
the formal algebraic relation (
2.1
) becomes the Yang-Baxter algebra

R_{{\ell},{\ell}^{\prime}}\big(\lambda_{1}/\lambda_{2}\,\big)\big({\bm{L}}_{{\ell}}(\lambda_{1})\otimes{\bm{1}}\big)\big({\bm{1}}\otimes{\bm{L}}_{{\ell}^{\prime}}(\lambda_{2})\big)=\big({\bm{1}}\otimes{\bm{L}}_{{\ell}^{\prime}}(\lambda_{2})\big)\big({\bm{L}}_{{\ell}}(\lambda_{1})\otimes{\bm{1}}\,\big)R_{{\ell},{\ell}^{\prime}}\big(\lambda_{1}/\lambda_{2}\,\big)

(2.5)

with

\displaystyle{R}_{{\ell}_{1},{\ell}_{2}}(\lambda_{1}/\lambda_{2})=\big(\pi_{{\ell}_{1}}(\lambda_{1})\otimes\pi_{{\ell}_{2}}(\lambda_{2})\big)[{\cal R}]\,.

As an immediate consequence the operators

\displaystyle{\bm{\tau}}_{\ell}(\lambda)={\rm Tr}_{\ell}\Big[q^{\frac{1}{2}{\tt h}\,h_{0}}\,{\bm{L}}_{{\ell}}(\lambda)\Big]\,,

(2.6)

usually referred to as the transfer-matrices,
obey the commutativity condition

\big[{\bm{\tau}}_{\ell}(\lambda),\,{\bm{\tau}}_{{\ell}^{\prime}}(\lambda^{\prime})\big]=0\ .

(2.7)

With the expression for the universal
R
-matrix
given in
Khoroshkin:1994um
,
one can obtain
{\bm{L}}_{\ell}(\lambda)
as a formal series expansion in
powers of the spectral parameter
\lambda
. The first few terms read as

\displaystyle{\bm{L}}_{\ell}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{\frac{1}{2}{\tt h}\,h_{0}}\ \bigg[1+\lambda\,(q-q^{-1})\,(x_{0}\,q^{\frac{{\tt h}}{2}}\,{\tt e}_{+}+x_{1}\,q^{-\frac{{\tt h}}{2}}\,{\tt e}_{-})

(2.8)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}\ \frac{q-q^{-1}}{q^{2}\,[2]_{q}}\,\bigg(\,(q^{2}-1)\,x_{0}^{2}\ (q^{\frac{{\tt h}}{2}}\,{\tt e}_{+})^{2}+\,(q^{2}-1)\,x_{1}^{2}\ (q^{-\frac{{\tt h}}{2}}\,{\tt e}_{-})^{2}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\,(q^{2}\,x_{1}x_{0}-x_{0}x_{1})\ (q^{\frac{{\tt h}}{2}}\,{\tt e}_{+})(q^{-\frac{{\tt h}}{2}}\,{\tt e}_{-})+(q^{2}\,x_{0}x_{1}-x_{1}x_{0})\,(q^{-\frac{{\tt h}}{2}}\,{\tt e}_{-})(q^{\frac{{\tt h}}{2}}\,{\tt e}_{+})\bigg)+\ldots\bigg]\,.

Here and below, abusing notation,
we do not distinguish between the formal generators of
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sl}}_{2})
and their

(2{\ell}+1)\times(2{\ell}+1)
matrices in a finite
dimensional representation
\pi_{\ell}
. Also,

[n]_{q}\equiv(q^{n}-q^{-n})/(q-q^{-1})
.
The expression in the square brackets contains the elements

x_{0},x_{1}\in U_{q}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{b}}}_{-})
,
which obey the quantum
Serre relations

x_{a}^{3}x_{b}-[3]_{q}\,x_{a}^{2}x_{b}x_{a}+[3]_{q}\,x_{a}x_{b}x_{a}^{2}-x_{b}x_{a}^{3}=0\qquad(a,b=0,1)\,.

(2.9)

There are
two remaining generators
h_{0},h_{1}
satisfying

\begin{array}[]{lll}[h_{0},x_{0}]=-[h_{1},x_{0}]=-2x_{0}\,,&[h_{0},x_{1}]=-[h_{1},x_{1}]=2x_{1}\,,&[h_{0},h_{1}]=0\,.\end{array}

(2.10)

Since
h_{0}+h_{1}

is a central element, for our purposes and
without loss of generality we have set it to be zero.

Up to this point,
there was no need to specify a representation of
U_{q}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{b}}}_{-})
–
the Yang-Baxter relation (
2.5
) holds identically
provided (
2.9
), (
2.10
) are true. An important case is when
the generators
x_{0}
,
x_{1}
are realized as integrals over the vertex operators

x_{0}=\frac{1}{q-q^{-1}}\,\int_{0}^{2\pi}{\rm d}u\,V_{+}(u)\ ,\qquad x_{1}=\frac{1}{q-q^{-1}}\,\int_{0}^{2\pi}{\rm d}u\,V_{-}(u)\ .

(2.11)

The latter are required to satisfy the braiding relation

\displaystyle V_{\sigma_{1}}(u_{1})V_{\sigma_{2}}(u_{2})=q^{2\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}\ V_{\sigma_{2}}(u_{2})V_{\sigma_{1}}(u_{1})\ ,\ \ \qquad u_{1}>u_{2}

(2.12)

along with the quasiperiodicity condition

\displaystyle V_{\pm}(u+2\pi)=q^{-2}\ \Omega^{\pm 1}\ V_{\pm}(u)\ .

(2.13)

Here the operator
\Omega
obeys

\displaystyle\Omega\,V_{\pm}(u)\,\Omega^{-1}=q^{\pm 4}\ V_{\pm}(u)

(2.14)

and can be identified with

\displaystyle\Omega=q^{2h_{0}}\ .

(2.15)

As was pointed out in the work
Bazhanov:1998dq
, using the braiding relations (
2.12
)
it is possible to express monomials built from the generators
x_{0}
and
x_{1}

in terms of the ordered integrals

J(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{m})=\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{2\pi>u_{1}>u_{2}>\ldots>u_{m}>0}\hskip-28.45274pt{\rm d}u_{1}\ldots{\rm d}u_{m}\,V_{\sigma_{1}}(u_{1})\ldots V_{\sigma_{m}}(u_{m})\ .

(2.16)

This way, the formal power series (
2.8
) can be brought to the form

{\bm{L}}_{\ell}(\lambda)=\,\Omega^{\frac{1}{4}{\tt h}}\,\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\infty}\ \lambda^{m}\!\!\!\sum\limits_{\sigma_{1}\ldots\sigma_{m}=\pm}\big(q^{\frac{{\tt h}}{2}\sigma_{1}}{\tt e}_{\,\sigma_{1}}\big)\ldots\big(q^{\frac{{\tt h}}{2}\sigma_{m}}{\tt e}_{\,\sigma_{m}}\big)\ J(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{m})\ .

(2.17)

The latter is recognized as the path ordered exponent

\displaystyle\bm{{L}}_{\ell}(\lambda)=\Omega^{\frac{1}{4}{\tt h}}\ \overset{\leftarrow}{{\cal P}}\exp\bigg(\lambda\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mbox{d}u\,\Big(V_{-}(u)\ q^{+\frac{{\tt h}}{2}}\,{\tt e}_{+}+V_{+}(u)\ q^{-\frac{{\tt h}}{2}}\,{\tt e}_{-}\Big)\bigg)\ .

(2.18)

The first realization of the
generators
x_{0}
,
x_{1}

in terms of the vertex operators was proposed in refs.
Bazhanov:1994ft
;
Bazhanov:1996dr
;
Bazhanov:1998dq
.
It reads as

\displaystyle V_{\pm}=\mbox{e}^{\pm 2{\rm i}\beta\phi}\,,

(2.19)

where

\phi=\phi(u)
stands for the chiral Bose field satisfying the Operator
Product Expansion (OPE)

\displaystyle\phi(u_{1})\phi(u_{2})=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\log(u_{1}-u_{2})+O(1)\ .

(2.20)

The parameter
\beta
is related to
q
, entering into the
braiding relation (
2.12
), as
q=\mbox{e}^{{\rm i}\pi\beta^{2}}
.
It is taken to lie in the domain

\displaystyle 0<\beta<1\ .

(2.21)

If the field
\phi
is assumed to be quasiperiodic
it can be expanded in a Fourier series
of the form
3
3

3

We usually employ the hat notation for
the oscillator modes to emphasize that they are operators
rather than
c
- numbers.
However in some cases, when the formulae become too cluttered,
the “hat” will be omitted (see, e.g., eq. (
9.47
)).

\displaystyle\phi(u)=\hat{\phi}_{0}+\hat{a}_{0}u+{\rm i}\ \sum_{m\not=0}\frac{\hat{a}_{m}}{m}\ \mbox{e}^{-{\rm i}mu}

(2.22)

so that

\displaystyle\phi(u+2\pi)=\phi(u)+2\pi\hat{a}_{0}\ .

(2.23)

In turn the vertices satisfy the quasiperiodicity condition (
2.13
) with

\Omega=\mbox{e}^{4\pi{\rm i}\beta\hat{a}_{0}}\,.

It is easy to see that the operators
{\bm{\tau}}_{\ell}(\lambda)
(
2.6
)
commute with the zero mode momenta,

\displaystyle\hat{a}_{0}\ :\ \ \ \ \ [\hat{\phi}_{0},\hat{a}_{0}]=\tfrac{{\rm i}}{2}\ ,

(2.24)

and hence
act invariantly in the Fock space
{\cal F}_{P}
.
The latter is generated by the action of the creation operators
\hat{a}_{n}\ (n=-1,-2,\ldots)
on the Fock
vacuum

\displaystyle|\,P\,\rangle\ :\ \ \ \ \ \hat{a}_{n}\,|\,P\,\rangle=0\ \ \ \qquad(n=1,2,\ldots)\ ,\ \ \ \qquad\hat{a}_{0}\,|\,P\,\rangle={P}\,|\,P\,\rangle\ .

(2.25)

3
Parafermionic realization of
U_{q}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{b}}}_{-})

In ref.
Lukyanov:2006gv
a
generalization of (
2.19
) was proposed,
which goes along the following lines.
Suppose we are given the algebra
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{k}(2)
with
central charge
k=1,2,\ldots\
.
The Kac-Moody currents obey the OPEs

\displaystyle J_{+}(u)J_{-}(0)=-\frac{k}{u^{2}}-\frac{{\rm i}}{u}\ J_{0}(0)+O(1)\ ,\ \ \ J_{0}(u)J_{\pm}(0)=\mp\frac{2{\rm i}}{u}\ J_{\pm}(0)+O(1)

\displaystyle J_{0}(u)J_{0}(0)=-\frac{2k}{u^{2}}+O(1)\ .

(3.1)

As is well known
Fateev:1985mm
, they admit a realization in terms of the
\mathbb{Z}_{k}

parafermionic fields
\psi_{\pm}
and the chiral Bose field
\phi
(
2.20
):

\displaystyle J_{\pm}=\sqrt{k}\ \psi_{\pm}\ \mbox{e}^{\pm\frac{2{\rm i}\phi}{\sqrt{k}}}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ J_{0}=2\sqrt{k}\ \partial\phi\ .

(3.2)

The parafermionic fields are quasiperiodic

\displaystyle\psi_{\pm}(u+2\pi)=\mbox{e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{k}}\ \big(\hat{\Omega}_{k}\big)^{\pm 1}\,\psi_{\pm}(u)

(3.3)

with
\hat{\Omega}_{k}
being the operator of the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{k}
charge:

\displaystyle\hat{\Omega}_{k}\,\psi_{\pm}\,\big(\hat{\Omega}_{k}\big)^{-1}=\omega^{\pm 2}\,\psi_{\pm}\,,\qquad\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \omega=\mbox{e}^{-\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{k}}\ .

(3.4)

Then

\displaystyle V_{\pm}=\sqrt{k}\,\psi_{\pm}\ \mbox{e}^{\pm\frac{2{\rm i}\beta\phi}{\sqrt{k}}}\,,

(3.5)

where again
\beta\in(0,1)
.
Note that the vertex operators
can be interpreted as a one-parameter deformation of the currents
J_{\pm}

from the case
\beta=1
.
The braiding relations (
2.12
) and quasiperiodicity condition (
2.13
)
are satisfied with

\displaystyle q=-\mbox{e}^{\frac{{\rm i}\pi}{k}(\beta^{2}-1)}\,,\qquad\qquad\Omega=\mbox{e}^{\frac{4\pi{\rm i}\beta\hat{a}_{0}}{\sqrt{k}}}\ \hat{\Omega}_{k}\ .

(3.6)

Let us now briefly discuss the diagonalization problem
for the transfer matrices
{\bm{\tau}}_{\ell}(\lambda)
(
2.6
)
with the vertex operators as in (
3.5
).
To this end some basic facts concerning
representations of the algebra of parafermionic currents are needed.
The theory was developed by Fateev and Zamolodchikov in ref.
Fateev:1985mm
,
in the construction of the
CFTs
describing the multicritical points of the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{k}
statistical systems
Fateev:1982wi
(certain
generalizations
of the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
invariant Ising model).
The algebra
contains a set of non-local currents
\{\psi_{n}\}_{n=1}^{k-1}

with conformal dimensions

\displaystyle\Delta_{n}=\frac{n\,(k-n)}{k}\ .

(3.7)

Their defining OPEs are invariant w.r.t.
{\mathbb{Z}}_{k}
transformations

\psi_{m}\mapsto\omega^{2ma}\,\psi_{m}
with
a=0,1,\ldots,k-1
and
\omega=\mbox{e}^{-\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{k}}
.
All the parafermionic currents can be generated through the OPE of the
currents with the lowest conformal dimensions

\displaystyle\psi_{+}\equiv\psi_{1}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \psi_{-}\equiv\psi_{k-1}\ ,

(3.8)

which carry the

{\mathbb{Z}}_{k}
-charges
+2
and
-2
, respectively (see eq.(
3.4
)).

The OPE of the fundamental parafermions is of special interest. It has the form

\displaystyle\psi_{+}(u)\psi_{-}(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mbox{e}^{\frac{{\rm i}\pi}{k}}\ (u-v)^{-2+\frac{2}{k}}\,\Big[1+\tfrac{k+2}{2k}\ (u-v)^{2}\ \big(W_{2}(u)+W_{2}(v)\big)

(3.9)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\,k^{-\frac{3}{2}}\ (u-v)^{3}\ \big(W_{3}(u)+W_{3}(v)\big)+\ldots\Big]\ .

All the fields occuring in the expansion are
{\mathbb{Z}}_{k}
neutral.
The field
W_{2}
, having Lorentz spin
2
, generates the Virasoro algebra with central charge

\displaystyle c_{k}=\frac{2(k-1)}{k+2}\ .

(3.10)

Further terms in the OPE (
3.9
)
involve a set of local fields
W_{3},\,W_{4},\ldots\,
, labeled by
their value of the Lorentz spin.
They form the

W\!A_{k-1}
algebra introduced in
refs.
Zamolodchikov:1985wn
;
Fateev:1987zh
with the special value of the
central charge
c=c_{k}
(
3.10
).

The chiral component of the Hilbert space
of the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{k}
CFT
can be decomposed into irreps
{\cal V}^{(k)}_{\mathfrak{j}}
of the chiral algebra. Here the subscript
{\mathfrak{j}}
stands
for the highest weight of the irrep with highest weight vector
\big|\,\sigma^{(k)}_{\mathfrak{j}}\,\big\rangle

having
conformal dimension

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathfrak{j}}=\frac{{\mathfrak{j}}\,(k-2{\mathfrak{j}})}{k\,(k+2)}\ ,

(3.11)

while

\displaystyle\hat{\Omega}_{k}\ \big|\,\sigma^{(k)}_{\mathfrak{j}}\,\big\rangle=\omega^{2{\mathfrak{j}}}\,\big|\,\sigma^{(k)}_{\mathfrak{j}}\,\big\rangle\ .

(3.12)

It will be further assumed that

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{j}}=0,\tfrac{1}{2},1,\ldots,\tfrac{k}{2}\ .

(3.13)

If
{\mathfrak{j}}\not=0,\tfrac{k}{2}

the states
|\,\sigma^{(k)}_{\mathfrak{j}}\,\rangle
and
|\,\sigma^{(k)}_{\frac{k}{2}-{\mathfrak{j}}}\,\rangle

possess the same non-vanishing
conformal dimension. However they are distinguished by their value of the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{k}
charge.
In fact the whole space

{\cal V}_{\mathfrak{j}}
is naturally splitted on the invariant subspaces of the operator
\hat{\Omega}_{k}
Fateev:1985mm
:

\displaystyle{\cal V}_{\mathfrak{j}}=\Big[\oplus_{s=0}^{2{\mathfrak{j}}}\,{\cal V}^{(k)}_{{\mathfrak{j}},2{\mathfrak{j}}-2s}\,\Big]\oplus\Big[\oplus_{s=1}^{k-2{\mathfrak{j}}-1}\,{\cal V}^{(k)}_{{\mathfrak{j}},2{\mathfrak{j}}+2s}\Big]\ :\ \ \ \quad\hat{\Omega}_{k}\,{\cal V}_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}}}^{(k)}=\omega^{{\mathfrak{m}}}\ {\cal V}_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}}}^{(k)}\ .

(3.14)

The lowest possible conformal dimension in the subspace
{\cal V}_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}}}^{(k)}
is given
by

\Delta_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}}}=\frac{{\mathfrak{j}}({\mathfrak{j}}+1)}{k+2}-\frac{{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}}{4k}

(3.15)

for
{\mathfrak{m}}=-2{\mathfrak{j}},\,-2{\mathfrak{j}}+2,\,\ldots,2{\mathfrak{j}}
, and

\Delta_{\frac{k}{2}-{\mathfrak{j}},k-{\mathfrak{m}}}

when
{\mathfrak{m}}=2{\mathfrak{j}}+2,\ldots,2k-2{\mathfrak{j}}-2
.

Each of the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{k}
-invariant subspaces

{\cal V}_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}}}^{(k)}\subset{\cal V}_{\mathfrak{j}}^{(k)}
possess a structure
of the highest weight irrep of the
W\!A_{k-1}
algebra.
From the formal algebraic point of view such
an irrep is obtained by factorizing a highest weight module over submodules of
“null-vectors”. In the case under consideration the highest weight can be
thought of as a pair of eigenvalues
(\Delta,w)
of the mutually commuting operators

\displaystyle\frac{c_{k}}{24}+\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{{\mbox{d}u}}{2\pi}\ W_{2}(u),\ \ \ \ \ \ \int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{{\mbox{d}u}}{2\pi}\ W_{3}(u)

(3.16)

corresponding to the highest weight vector. In the case of
{\cal V}_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}}}^{(k)}

with
{\mathfrak{m}}=2{\mathfrak{j}},\,2{\mathfrak{j}}-2,\,\ldots,-2{\mathfrak{j}}

the conformal dimension
\Delta
is given by (
3.15
),
while
w=w_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}}}
with

\displaystyle w_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}}}=\frac{\mathfrak{m}}{6\sqrt{k}}\ \bigg(\,\frac{3k+4}{2k}\,{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}-6\,{\mathfrak{j}}({\mathfrak{j}}+1)+k\,\bigg)\ .

(3.17)

For
{\mathfrak{m}}=2{\mathfrak{j}}+2,\ldots,2k-2{\mathfrak{j}}-2
,
the highest weight is

\big(\Delta_{\frac{k}{2}-{\mathfrak{j}},k-{\mathfrak{m}}},w_{\frac{k}{2}-{\mathfrak{j}},k-{\mathfrak{m}}}\big)
.
The primary state (the highest weight vector) w.r.t. the
W\!A_{k-1}
algebra will be
denoted by
\big|\,\sigma^{(k)}_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}}}\,\big\rangle\in{\cal V}_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}}}^{(k)}
.

Let us return to the transfer-matrices
{\bm{\tau}}_{\ell}(\lambda)

built from the vertex operators
V_{\pm}=\sqrt{k}\,\psi_{\pm}\ \mbox{e}^{\pm\frac{2{\rm i}\beta\phi}{\sqrt{k}}}
.
First of all it is straightforward to show that they commute with
\hat{a}_{0}
and
\hat{\Omega}_{k}
:

\displaystyle[{\bm{\tau}}_{\ell}(\lambda),\hat{a}_{0}]=[{\bm{\tau}}_{\ell}(\lambda),\hat{\Omega}_{k}]=0\ .

(3.18)

This implies that
{\bm{\tau}}_{\ell}(\lambda)
acts invariantly
in the space
{\cal V}_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}}}^{(k)}\otimes{\cal F}_{P}
.
Furthermore, it turns out that

\displaystyle[{\bm{\tau}}_{\ell}(\lambda),{\bf I}_{1}]=0\ ,

(3.19)

where

\displaystyle{\bf I}_{1}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\mbox{d}u}{2\pi}\ \big(\,(\partial\phi)^{2}+W_{2}\,\big)\ .

(3.20)

The eigenvalues of this
operator
are given by

\displaystyle P^{2}+\Delta_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}}}-\frac{c_{k}+1}{24}+{\tt L}

(3.21)

with
{\tt L}
a non-negative integer.
As such
{\bf I}_{1}
naturally introduces a grading in

{\cal V}_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}}}^{(k)}\otimes{\cal F}_{P}
.
The dimensions of each level eigenspaces characterized by given
{\tt L}=0,1,\ldots

is finite. Due to the commutativity condition (
3.19
) the transfer-matrix acts invariantly in
each level subspace
of
{\cal V}_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}}}^{(k)}\otimes{\cal F}_{P}
. An immediate consequence is that
the primary state
\big|\,\sigma^{(k)}_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}}}\,\big\rangle\otimes|P\rangle

is an eigenvector of the transfer-matrix.

One can address the diagonalization problem
of
{\bm{\tau}}_{\ell}(\lambda)
in the level subspaces of

{\cal V}_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}}}^{(k)}\otimes{\cal F}_{P}
.
It turns out that the transfer-matrices are part
of a larger commuting family. The latter includes the operators

{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{\pm}(\lambda)\in{\rm End}\big({\cal V}_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}}}^{(k)}\otimes{\cal F}_{P}\big)\ :\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \big[\bm{\tau}_{\ell}(\lambda),{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{\pm}(\lambda)\big]=\big[{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{+}(\lambda),{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{-}(\lambda)\big]=0\,,

(3.22)

which
satisfy the Baxter type relation

\displaystyle{\bm{\tau}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\lambda)\,{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{\pm}(\lambda)=\Omega^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}\,{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{\pm}(q^{+1}\lambda)+\Omega^{\mp\frac{1}{2}}\,{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{\pm}(q^{-1}\lambda)\ .

(3.23)

The definition of
{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{\pm}(\lambda)

is similar to that given by eqs. (
2.18
), (
2.6
)
and involves the vertex operators
V_{\pm}
(
3.5
) and
\Omega
(
3.6
).
However, the path-ordered exponent now
contains the generators of the

q
-oscillator algebra
{\cal E}_{\pm}
and
{\cal H}
:

[{\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{E}}_{\pm}]=\pm 2\,{\mathcal{E}}_{\pm}\,,\qquad\qquad q\,{\mathcal{E}}_{+}{\mathcal{E}}_{-}-q^{-1}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{-}{\mathcal{E}}_{+}=\frac{1}{q-q^{-1}}\ .

(3.24)

Let
\rho_{\pm}
be representations of this algebra such that the traces

{{\rm Tr}}_{\rho_{\pm}}\big[\mbox{e}^{\pm 2{\rm i}\pi\beta P{\cal H}}\big]\neq 0,\,\infty\qquad{\rm with}\qquad{\Im}m(P)<0

(3.25)

exist and are non-vanishing.
Then
one may introduce the operators
{{{{{\bm{a}}}}}}_{\pm}(\lambda)

as

\displaystyle{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{\pm}(\lambda)=\frac{{\rm Tr}_{\rho_{\pm}}\big[\,\mbox{e}^{\pm{\rm i}\pi\beta\hat{a}_{0}{\cal H}}{{\bm{L}}}_{\pm}(\lambda)\,\big]}{{\rm Tr}_{\rho_{\pm}}\big[\,\mbox{e}^{\pm 2{\rm i}\pi\beta\hat{a}_{0}{\cal H}}\,\big]}\,,

(3.26)

where

\displaystyle{\bm{L}}_{\pm}(\lambda)=\Omega^{\pm\frac{1}{4}{\cal H}}\ \overset{\leftarrow}{{\cal P}}\exp\bigg(\lambda\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mbox{d}u\,\Big(V_{-}(u)\ q^{\pm\frac{\cal H}{2}}\,{\cal E}_{\pm}+V_{+}(u)\ q^{\mp\frac{\cal H}{2}}\,{\cal E}_{\mp}\Big)\bigg)\ .

(3.27)

It turns out
Bazhanov:1998dq
that all the transfer-matrices are expressed
through
{{{{{\bm{a}}}}}}_{\pm}(\lambda)
as

\big(\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}-\Omega^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big)\ {{\bm{\tau}}}_{\ell}(\lambda)=\Omega^{\frac{2\ell+1}{2}}\,{{{{{\bm{a}}}}}}_{+}\big(q^{\ell+\frac{1}{2}}\lambda\big){{{{{\bm{a}}}}}}_{-}\big(q^{-\ell-\frac{1}{2}}\lambda\big)-\Omega^{-\frac{2\ell+1}{2}}\,{{{{{\bm{a}}}}}}_{+}\big(q^{-\ell-\frac{1}{2}}\lambda\big){{{{{\bm{a}}}}}}_{-}\big(q^{\ell+\frac{1}{2}}\lambda\big)

(3.28)

with
\ell=0,\frac{1}{2},1\ldots
and
{\bm{\tau}}_{0}(\lambda)\equiv\bf{1}
.

4
Local IM

Among the operators which commute with the transfer-matrix, a special r
{\hat{\rm o}}
le belongs to the
local Integrals of Motion (IM)
Zam
.
These are a set of mutually commuting operators which
can be written in the form

\displaystyle{\bf I}_{s}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\mbox{d}u}{2\pi}\ T_{s+1}(u)

(4.1)

with
T_{s+1}
being a chiral local density of integer Lorentz spin
s+1
.
Remarkably, for a given choice of vertex operators, there exists a purely algebraic procedure
which, in principle, allows one
to explicitly build the local IM.
Later, a generalization of the vertex operators

V_{\pm}=\sqrt{k}\,\psi_{\pm}\,\mbox{e}^{\pm\frac{2{\rm i}\beta\phi}{\sqrt{k}}}

will be proposed, which
gives rise to new commuting families involving
{\bm{\tau}}_{\ell}(\lambda)
,

{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{\pm}(\lambda)
as well as the corresponding sets of local IM.
Here, for future references, we illustrate the construction of
\{{\bf I}_{s}\}

for the basic case.

Let
{\cal L}^{(s)}
be the linear space of chiral local fields of
Lorentz spin
s
built out of the Bose field
\phi
and the fundamental parafermions
\psi_{\pm}
.
For given
positive integer
s
it is a finite dimensional space.
We choose one of the vertices, say
V_{+}
, and consider the linear subspace

{\cal W}^{(s)}\subset{\cal L}^{(s)}
made up of local fields
X_{s}

such that
the singular part of the OPE
X_{s}(u)V_{+}(v)
is a total derivative in
v
:

\displaystyle X_{s}(u)V_{+}(v)=\partial_{v}(\ldots)+O(1)\ .

(4.2)

In the physical slang, one says that the fields
X_{s}
commute
with the “screening charge”

\displaystyle Q=\oint\mbox{d}v\ V_{+}(v)\ .

(4.3)

Suppose
X_{s}
and
Y_{s^{\prime}}
both commute with
Q
.
By construction any local field which appears in the OPE

X_{s}(u)Y_{s^{\prime}}(v)
also commutes with the screening charge.
Hence the direct sum
\oplus_{s\geq 1}{\cal W}^{(s)}
possesses
the structure of an operator algebra.
Starting from the seminal work of A.B. Zamolodchikov
Zamolodchikov:1985wn
,
algebras of such type are referred to as
the
W
-algebras.

In the case under consideration
the space
{\cal L}^{(1)}
contains only the field
\partial\phi
, while
{\cal W}^{(1)}=\emptyset
.
The space
{\cal L}^{(2)}
is spanned by
(\partial\phi)^{2},\,\partial^{2}\phi
and the

W_{2}
current occuring in the OPE of the fundamental parafermionic fields
\psi_{\pm}
(
3.9
).
It is easy to check that, up to an overall multiplicative factor,
there is a single spin 2 field commuting with the screening charge,

\displaystyle X_{2}=(\partial\phi)^{2}+\tfrac{{{\rm i}}}{\sqrt{k}}\ \big(\beta^{-1}-\beta\big)\,\partial^{2}\phi+W_{2}\ .

(4.4)

Further,
{\cal L}^{(3)}={\rm span}\big((\partial\phi)^{3},\,\partial^{2}\phi\partial\phi,\ \partial^{3}\phi,\,W_{3},\,\partial\phi W_{2},\,\partial W_{2}\big)
while
any local field from
{\cal W}^{(3)}
is proportional to the derivative
\partial X_{2}
.
The space
{\cal W}^{(4)}
contains
\partial^{2}X_{2}
and
(X_{2})^{2}
. The latter is a spin 4 local field
that is the first regular term in the OPE:

\displaystyle X_{2}(u)X_{2}(v)=\frac{c}{2(u-v)^{2}}-\frac{X_{2}(u)+X_{2}(v)}{(u-v)^{2}}+(X_{2})^{2}(v)+O(u-v)\ ,

(4.5)

where
c=\frac{3k}{k+2}-\frac{6}{k}(\beta^{-1}-\beta)^{2}
.
It turns out that
\dim\big({\cal W}^{(4)}\big)=3
, i.e., together with the descendants of
X_{2}
there is
an extra field
X_{4}
. Its construction can be simplified if one takes advantage of the
bosonization formulae for the parafermion currents
Zam1986
;
Wakimoto
;
Gerasimov:1989mz

\displaystyle\psi_{\pm}=\Big(\partial\alpha\pm{\rm i}\,\sqrt{\tfrac{k+2}{k}}\ \partial\gamma\Big)\ \mbox{e}^{\pm\frac{2\alpha}{\sqrt{k}}}\,.

(4.6)

This involves two chiral Bose fields with

\alpha(u)\alpha(v)=-\tfrac{1}{2}\log(u-v)+O(1)
and similarly for
\gamma
.
Then the field
X_{4}
is a certain differential polynomial built from

\partial\phi
,
\partial\alpha
,
\partial\gamma
.
In principal one can proceed further and explicitly describe the higher spin components
{\cal W}^{(s)}
.
The original definition of the parafermion algebra requires that

k
is a positive integer.
Nevertheless it can be treated as an
arbitrary complex number
in the bosonization formulae (
4.6
).
This makes it possible to introduce
the
W
-algebra associated with the screening charge
Q
(
4.3
),
for arbitrary
k
as was done in the unpublished work of V.A. Fateev as well as
in refs.
Feigin:2001yq
;
Lukyanov:2012wq
. In the last paper
\oplus_{s=1}^{\infty}{\cal W}^{(s)}

was referred to as the corner-brane
W
-algebra.

The second vertex operator
V_{-}
comes in to play when
obtaining the local IM from the fields
in the
W
-algebra. One searches for local
fields
T_{s+1}\in{\cal W}^{(s+1)}
such that the OPE of
T_{s+1}
and
V_{-}
possesses the following structure

\displaystyle T_{s+1}(u)V_{-}(v)=\sum_{m=2}^{s+1}\frac{{R}_{-m}(v)}{(u-v)^{m}}+\frac{{R}_{-1}(v)}{u-v}+O(1)\ \ \ \ \ {\rm with}\ \ \ \ \ {R}_{-1}=\partial{\cal O}(v)\ .

(4.7)

Note that, if
T_{s+1}
exists, it is defined up to the addition of a total derivative

\partial X_{s}

(\forall X_{s}\in{\cal W}^{(s)})
.
This ambiguity has no affect on
{\bf I}_{s}
, which is expressed through an integral as in (
4.1
).
There is also an ambiguity in the overall multiplicative
normalization of
T_{s+1}
,
which is carried over to the local IM.
Following the arguments of ref.
Bazhanov:1998dq
, it is expected that
{\bf I}_{s}

commutes with the transfer-matrices and
{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{\pm}(\lambda)
:

\displaystyle\big[\bm{\tau}_{\ell}(\lambda),{\bf I}_{s}\big]=\big[{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{\pm}(\lambda),{\bf I}_{s}\big]=0\ .

(4.8)

Also, assuming that the transfer-matrices resolve all the degeneracies in

{\cal V}_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}}}^{(k)}\otimes{\cal F}_{P}
, one arrives at
the mutual commutativity condition

\displaystyle\big[\,{\bf I}_{s},{\bf I}_{s^{\prime}}\big]=0\ .

(4.9)

In the case under consideration
the operator
{\bf I}_{s}
exists and is unique (up to overall normalization)
only for odd
s=1,3,5,\ldots\
. For example, for
s=1
the density
T_{2}=X_{2}
from (
4.4
), while
the explicit formula for
{\bf I}_{3}
was originally obtained in refs.
Fateev:1995ht
;
Fateev:1996ea
.
Further facts on this commuting family
of local IM can be found in
Bazhanov:2013cua
.

5
Prototype example of the ODE/IQFT correspondence

The most effective way for computing the spectrum of
the operators
{\bm{\tau}}_{\ell}(\lambda)
,

{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{\pm}(\lambda)
and
{\bf I}_{2n-1}

in the space
{\cal V}_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}}}^{(k)}\otimes{\cal F}_{P}

is provided
by the ODE/IQFT correspondence.
As part of our study of the new commuting family, a class of ODEs
for the eigenstates will be proposed.
In order to prepare for that discussion it would be useful to
demonstrate the approach on the
eigenvalues of
{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{+}(\lambda)

corresponding to the primary state

|\,\sigma^{(k)}_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}}}\,\big\rangle\otimes\big|P\rangle
.

Let us start with the simplest case when
{\mathfrak{j}}={\mathfrak{m}}=0
.
According to the work
Lukyanov:2006gv
, one should consider the linear differential equation

\displaystyle\bigg[-\partial_{x}^{2}+\kappa^{2}\ \big(\,\mbox{e}^{\xi x}+\mbox{e}^{(1+\xi)x}\,\big)^{k}-A^{2}\ \bigg]\ \Theta=0\,.

(5.1)

Here
\xi>0
while
k
is assumed to be a positive integer.
For
\Im m(A)\geq 0
one can introduce the Jost
solution, which asymptotically approaches to a plane wave

\displaystyle{\Theta}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\leftarrow)}}_{A}(x)\asymp\mbox{e}^{-{\rm i}Ax}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\rm as}\ \ \ \ x\to-\infty\ .

(5.2)

It turns out that as a function of
A
it is meromorphic.
This allows one to
unambiguously define
\Theta^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\leftarrow)}}_{A}(x)

for any complex
A
except for a discrete, pure imaginary set of values,
where it develops simple poles.
The function

\Theta^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\leftarrow)}}_{-A}(x)

is another solution to the ODE (
5.1
),
which is linearly independent from
\Theta^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\leftarrow)}}_{A}(x)
when
A\neq 0
.

Since the potential in (
5.1
) grows rapidly for large positive
x
, the ODE admits a solution which
decays at
x\to+\infty
.
We denote it by
\Theta^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\rightarrow)}}
and specify its overall normalization through the asymptotic condition

\displaystyle\Theta^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\rightarrow)}}\asymp\mbox{e}^{-F(y)+o(1)}\ \ \ \ \ {\rm as}\ \ \ y=x+\log(\mu)\to+\infty\ ,

(5.3)

where

F(y)=\tfrac{1}{4}\,(1+\xi)k\,y+\tfrac{2}{(1+\xi)k}\ \mbox{e}^{\frac{1}{2}(1+\xi)k\,y}\,{}_{2}F_{1}\big(-\tfrac{1}{2}\,(1+\xi)k,-\tfrac{1}{2}\,k,1-\tfrac{1}{2}\,(1+\xi)k\,;\,-\mu\mbox{e}^{-y}\,\big)

(5.4)

(
{}_{2}F_{1}
stands for the Gauss hypergeometric function)
and

\displaystyle\mu=\kappa^{\frac{2}{(1+\xi)k}}\ .

(5.5)

The solution
\Theta^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\rightarrow)}}

may be introduced for any complex value of
A^{2}
and is an entire function
of this parameter.

Consider the connection coefficients for the ODE (
5.1
), which are expressed
via the Wronskian,
{\rm Wron}[f,g]=f\partial_{x}g-g\partial_{x}f
, as

\displaystyle W(\mu)=\mu^{-{\rm i}A}\ {\rm Wron}\big[\,\Theta^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\rightarrow)}},\Theta^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\leftarrow)}}_{A}\,\big]\ .

(5.6)

Here the overall factor has been chosen to ensure
the existence of the limit

\displaystyle\lim_{\mu\to 0}W(\mu)=W(0)\ .

(5.7)

The connection coefficient
W
is a meromorphic function of
A
.
For given
A
it
turns out to be an entire function of
\mu

and, therefore, admits a convergent Taylor series expansion.
This is related to the fact that
in the variable
y=x+\log(\mu)
,
the ODE takes the form

\displaystyle\big(-\partial_{y}^{2}+U(y)-A^{2}\,\big)\Theta=0\ \ \ \ \ \qquad{\rm with}\ \ \ \ \ \ \qquad U(y)=\mbox{e}^{(1+\xi)ky}+\delta U\ .

(5.8)

The term
\delta U=(\mbox{e}^{(1+\xi)y}+\mu\,\mbox{e}^{\xi y})^{k}-\mbox{e}^{(1+\xi)ky}

analytically depends on
\mu
and can be treated perturbatively so long as
\xi>0
,
k=1,2,\ldots\
.
In ref.
Lukyanov:2006gv
it was shown that the ratio
W(\mu)/W(0)
coincides with the eigenvalue
of

{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{+}(\lambda)

corresponding to the primary state
\big|\,\sigma^{(k)}_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}}}\,\big\rangle\otimes|P\rangle
with
{\mathfrak{j}}={\mathfrak{m}}=0
,
provided the following identification of the parameters is made:

\displaystyle\xi=\tfrac{\beta^{2}}{1-\beta^{2}}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \mu=-\lambda^{2}\ \Gamma^{2}\big(\tfrac{1-\beta^{2}}{k}\big)\big(\tfrac{k}{1-\beta^{2}}\big)^{\frac{2}{k}(1-\beta^{2})}

(5.9)

and also

\displaystyle A=\tfrac{{\rm i}\sqrt{k}}{\beta^{-1}-\beta}\ P\ .

(5.10)

Recall that
the vertices
V_{\pm}
are non-local fields with fractional conformal dimensions.
In writing the
\mu-\lambda
relation (
5.9
) we assume that the phase ambiguity of
V_{\pm}

is specified through the following condition imposed on the OPE

\displaystyle q\ V_{\pm}(u_{1})V_{\mp}(u_{2})\big|_{u_{1}-u_{2}\to 0^{+}}\to k\times(u_{1}-u_{2})^{-\frac{2}{k}(k-1+\beta^{2})}\,\big(1+o(1)\,\big)>0\ .

(5.11)

We now turn to the eigenvalues corresponding to the states

\big|\,\sigma^{(k)}_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}}}\,\big\rangle\otimes|P\rangle

with
{\mathfrak{j}}
and
{\mathfrak{m}}
not necessarily zero.
To the best of our knowledge the ODE/IQFT correspondence for this case
has not yet been discussed in the literature
despite that it follows immediately from the results of the works

Lukyanov:2012wq
;
Bazhanov:2013cua
.
The generalization of the ODE (
5.1
) reads as

\displaystyle\bigg[-\partial_{x}^{2}+\kappa^{2}\ \big(\mbox{e}^{(1+\xi)x}+\mbox{e}^{\xi x}\,\big)^{k}-\frac{A^{2}+B^{2}\mbox{e}^{x}}{1+\mbox{e}^{x}}-(C^{2}-\tfrac{1}{4}\big)\ \frac{\mbox{e}^{x}}{(1+\mbox{e}^{x})^{2}}\bigg]\ \Theta=0\,.

(5.12)

Together with
\xi
and
k
this equation involves three extra parameters. With the specialization

B^{2}=A^{2}
and
C^{2}=\tfrac{1}{4}
, it boils down to (
5.1
).
As with the previous case one can consider the Wronskian
W(\mu)
(
5.6
).
However an important difference now is that
W(\mu)

turns out to be an entire function of
\log(\mu)
rather than
\mu
.
This is related to the fact that when the ODE is rewritten in the form (
5.8
),
the term
\delta U
is no longer an entire function of
\mu
.

For the analysis of eq.(
5.12
) it is convenient to perform the change of variables

\displaystyle z=\mbox{e}^{x}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\Psi}(z)=\mbox{e}^{\frac{x}{2}}\,\Theta(x)\ ,

(5.13)

bringing the ODE to the form

\displaystyle\bigg[\!\!\!

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\partial_{z}^{2}+\kappa^{2}\ z^{-2+\xi k}\,(1+z)^{k}

(5.14)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{A^{2}+\frac{1}{4}}{z^{2}}+\frac{A^{2}-B^{2}-C^{2}+\frac{1}{4}}{z}+\frac{{B}^{2}+{C}^{2}-{A}^{2}-\frac{1}{4}}{1+z}+\frac{{C}^{2}-\frac{1}{4}}{(1+z)^{2}}\,\bigg]\ {\Psi}=0\,.

For generic values of the parameters it possesses three singular points at
z=0,-1,\infty
.
Let us explore the condition when the singularity
at
z=-1
becomes apparent.
First we consider the case
\kappa=0
when (
5.14
) belongs to the Fuchsian class. The test for the apparent
singularity is well known and can be found, for example, in ref.
Gao
.
Suppose we are given the equation

\displaystyle\big(-\partial_{z}^{2}+V(z)\big)\,\Psi=0

(5.15)

and
V(z)
admits a Laurent expansion of the form

\displaystyle V(z)=\frac{1}{(z-w)^{2}}\ \bigg(v_{0}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}v_{m}(z-w)^{m}\bigg)\,.

(5.16)

The singularity at
z=w
is apparent if and only if

\displaystyle v_{0}={\mathfrak{j}}({\mathfrak{j}}+1)\ \ \ \ \ \ {\rm with}\ \ \ \ {\mathfrak{j}}=0,\tfrac{1}{2},1,\ldots

(5.17)

and

\displaystyle F_{{\mathfrak{j}}}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{2{\mathfrak{j}}+1})=0\ .

(5.18)

Here the polynomials
F_{{\mathfrak{j}}}
are defined through the determinant

\displaystyle F_{{\mathfrak{j}}}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{2{\mathfrak{j}}+1})=\det\begin{pmatrix}&v_{1}&1\cdot(2{\mathfrak{j}})&0&\ldots&0\\
&v_{2}&v_{1}&2\cdot(2{\mathfrak{j}}-1)&\ldots&0\\
&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
&v_{2{\mathfrak{j}}}&v_{2{\mathfrak{j}}-1}&v_{2{\mathfrak{j}}-2}&\ldots&2{\mathfrak{j}}\cdot(1)\\
&v_{2{\mathfrak{j}}+1}&v_{2{\mathfrak{j}}}&v_{2{\mathfrak{j}}-1}&\ldots&v_{1}\end{pmatrix}.

(5.19)

In the simplest cases (
5.18
) reads explicitly as

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{j}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\ :\ \ \ \ \ v_{1}^{2}-v_{2}=0

(5.20)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{j}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1\ :\ \ \ \ \ v_{1}\,\big(v_{1}^{2}-4v_{2}\,\big)+4v_{3}=0\ .

Applying the above condition to (
5.14
) with
\kappa=0

one concludes that the singularity at
z=-1
is apparent provided the parameters

A
,
B
and
C
satisfy the conditions

\begin{array}[]{lll}C^{2}=\big({\mathfrak{j}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\big)^{2}&{\rm with}&{\mathfrak{j}}=0,\tfrac{1}{2},1,\ldots\\[8.5359pt]
B^{2}=\big(A-\tfrac{{\rm i}}{2}\,{\mathfrak{m}}\big)^{2}&{\rm with}&{\mathfrak{m}}=-2{\mathfrak{j}},\,-2{\mathfrak{j}}+2,\ldots,{\mathfrak{j}}-2,2{\mathfrak{j}}\,.\end{array}

(5.21)

Suppose now that
\kappa\not=0
.
For

\displaystyle k=1,2,3,\ldots

(5.22)

the term
\propto\kappa
has a zero of order
k
at
z=-1
.
Hence its presence will not affect the conditions (
5.17
) and (
5.18
)
as long as

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{j}}=0,\tfrac{1}{2},1,\ldots,\tfrac{k}{2}\ .

(5.23)

When
z=-1
is an apparent singularity
we define
the normalized connection coefficients in the frame
(z,\Psi)
(
5.13
) as

D_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}},A}(\mu)=\frac{W_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}},A}(\mu)}{W_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}},A}(0)}\ ,\ \ \ \ \quad{\rm where}\ \ \ W_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}},A}(\mu)=\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{m}-{\rm i}A}\ {\rm Wron}\big[\,\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\rightarrow)}},\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\leftarrow)}}_{A}\,\big]\,.

(5.24)

Then

W_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}},A}
admits a power series expansion in
\mu
.
4
4

4

In fact the phenomena
was already discussed in ref.
Bazhanov:2017nzh
for the case
\xi=0
.

It is possible to show that

\displaystyle W_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}},A}(0)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\ \big({(1+\xi)k}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\mathfrak{m}+2{\rm i}A}{(1+\xi)k}}\ \frac{\Gamma\big(1-\tfrac{\mathfrak{m}+2{\rm i}A}{(1+\xi)k}\big)\Gamma(1-2{\rm i}A)\,\Gamma(-\mathfrak{m}-2{\rm i}A)}{\Gamma\big(\mathfrak{j}+1-\tfrac{\mathfrak{m}}{2}-2{\rm i}A\big)\,\Gamma\big(-\mathfrak{j}-\tfrac{\mathfrak{m}}{2}-2{\rm i}A\big)}\ ,

(5.25)

while

\displaystyle D_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}},A}(\mu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+\bigg(\frac{2}{(1+\xi)k}\bigg)^{\frac{2}{(1+\xi)k}}\ \frac{\Gamma\big(-\frac{1}{(1+\xi)k}\big)\Gamma\big(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{(1+\xi)k}\big)}{4\sqrt{\pi}}\ \frac{\Gamma\big(1-\frac{1+\mathfrak{m}+2{\rm i}A}{(1+\xi)k}\big)}{\Gamma\big(\frac{1-\mathfrak{m}-2{\rm i}A}{(1+\xi)k}\big)}

(5.26)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\bigg(\,\frac{4\mathfrak{j}(\mathfrak{j}+1)+{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}+4{\rm i}\mathfrak{m}A}{(1-{\mathfrak{m}}-2{\rm i}A)(1+{\mathfrak{m}}+2{\rm i}A)}+\frac{2k}{(1+\xi)k-2}\,\bigg)\ \mu+O(\mu^{2})\ .

A comparison of the first nontrivial expansion coefficient of
the eigenvalue of
{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{+}(\lambda)

corresponding to the state

\big|\,\sigma^{(k)}_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}}}\,\big\rangle\otimes|P\rangle

with formula (
5.26
) results in the relations

\begin{array}[]{ll}A={\rm i}\,\big(\,\sqrt{\xi(1+\xi)k}\,P-\frac{1}{2}\,\xi\,{\mathfrak{m}}\big)\,,&{C}^{2}=\big({\mathfrak{j}}+\frac{1}{2}\big)^{2}\\[5.69046pt]
\!\!\!{B}^{2}=-\big(\,\sqrt{\xi(1+\xi)k}\,P-\frac{1}{2}(1+\xi)\,{\mathfrak{m}}\big)^{2}\ .&\end{array}

(5.27)

It also confirms eq. (
5.9
).
This way one identifies

D_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}},A}(\mu)
with the eigenvalue of
{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{+}(\lambda)

corresponding to the state

\big|\,\sigma^{(k)}_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}}}\,\big\rangle\otimes|P\rangle
in the case

{\mathfrak{m}}=-2{\mathfrak{j}},\,-2{\mathfrak{j}}+2,\ldots,2{\mathfrak{j}}-2,2{\mathfrak{j}}
.

Regarding the other members of the commuting family, the
eigenvalue of
{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{-}(\lambda)

for the state
\big|\,\sigma^{(k)}_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}}}\,\big\rangle\otimes|P\rangle

coincides with
D_{{\mathfrak{j}},-{\mathfrak{m}},-A}(\mu)
.
The eigenvalues of the transfer-matrices
{\bm{\tau}}_{\ell}(\lambda)

are also given by certain connection
coefficients for the ODE (
5.14
). Those of the
local IM can be extracted from the
large
\mu
behaviour of

D_{{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}},A}
within the standard WKB technique.
All this is well known and widely discussed in the literature and will
not be elaborated on here.

6
New realization of
U_{q}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{b}}}_{-})

Introduce the
r
non-local fields

\displaystyle V_{\pm}^{(a)}=\sqrt{k_{a}}\ \psi_{\pm}^{(a)}\ \exp\bigg[\pm 2{\rm i}\,\Big(\,\tfrac{\beta-1}{K}\sum_{b=1\atop b\not=a}^{r}\sqrt{k_{b}}\ \phi_{b}+\big(\tfrac{\beta-1}{K}\ \sqrt{k_{a}}+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{k_{a}}}\big)\,\phi_{a}\,\bigg)\bigg]\,,

(6.1)

where
\phi_{a}
and
\psi_{\pm}^{(a)}
are

r
independent copies of the chiral Bose
and
{\mathbb{Z}}_{k_{a}}
parafermionic fields, respectively.
Also the positive integer
K
stands for

\displaystyle K=\sum_{a=1}^{r}k_{a}\,.

(6.2)

The definition (
6.1
) has been arranged so that
the braiding relations

\displaystyle V^{(a)}_{\sigma_{1}}(u_{1})V^{(b)}_{\sigma_{2}}(u_{2})=q^{2\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}\ V^{(b)}_{\sigma_{2}}(u_{2})V^{(a)}_{\sigma_{1}}(u_{1})\ ,\ \ \ u_{1}>u_{2}

(6.3)

with
q=-\mbox{e}^{\frac{{\rm i}\pi}{K}(\beta^{2}-1)}
are satisfied.
A simple way to see this is to rewrite
V^{(a)}_{\pm}
in the form

\displaystyle V_{\pm}^{(a)}=J^{(a)}_{\pm}\ \mbox{e}^{\pm\frac{2{\rm i}(\beta-1)}{\sqrt{K}}\varphi}\ ,

(6.4)

where
J^{(a)}_{\pm}
is given by eq. (
3.2
) with

k
,
\psi_{\pm}
,
\phi
swapped by
k_{a}
,
\psi_{\pm}^{(a)}
and
\phi_{a}
, respectively,
while

\displaystyle\varphi=\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\sum_{a=1}^{r}\sqrt{k_{a}}\,\phi_{a}\ :\ \ \ \ J_{0}^{\rm(tot)}\equiv\sum_{a=1}^{r}J_{0}^{(a)}=2\sqrt{K}\,\partial\varphi\ .

(6.5)

Then the braiding relations follow from the fact that the
Kac-Moody currents
J^{(a)}_{\pm}
,
J^{(a)}_{0}
are mutually local fields.
Notice that the field
\varphi
satisfies the
OPEs

\varphi(u_{1})\varphi(u_{2})=-\tfrac{1}{2}\ \log(u_{1}-u_{2})+O(1)\ ,\ \ \ \ \phi_{a}(u_{1})\varphi(u_{2})=-\sqrt{\tfrac{k_{a}}{4K}}\ \log(u_{1}-u_{2})+O(1)

(6.6)

We will assume that the non-local fields
V^{(a)}_{\pm}

are normalized by means of the condition

\displaystyle q\ V^{(a)}_{\pm}(u)\,V^{(b)}_{\mp}(0)\big|_{u\to 0^{+}}\to k_{a}\,u^{-\frac{2}{k_{a}}(k_{a}-1+\beta^{2})}\,\big(\delta_{ab}+o(1)\big)>0\ ,

(6.7)

which is similar to (
5.11
).

Let
{\cal V}_{{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}}
be the space of representation of the
parafermionic algebra generated by the fundamental parafermions
\psi_{\pm}^{(a)}
.
As before the subspace denoted by
{\cal V}_{{\mathfrak{j}}_{a},m_{a}}^{(k_{a})}
is the one
with fixed
{\mathbb{Z}}_{k_{a}}
charge
m_{a}
, so that

\displaystyle{\cal V}_{{\mathfrak{j}}_{a},m_{a}}^{(k_{a})}=\emptyset\ \ \ \ \ \ {\rm as}\ \ \ \ m_{a}-2{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}\not=2\,{\mathbb{Z}}

(6.8)

and

\displaystyle{\cal V}_{{\mathfrak{j}}_{a},m_{a}}^{(k_{a})}={\cal V}_{{\mathfrak{j}}_{a},m^{\prime}_{a}}^{(k_{a})}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\rm for}\ \ \ \ m_{a}=m_{a}^{\prime}\ \ ({\rm mod}\ k_{a})\,.

(6.9)

Also
{\cal F}^{(a)}_{P_{a}}
is the space of representation for the Heisenberg algebra
generated of the Fourier modes of the
field
\partial\phi_{a}
with
P_{a}
being the value of the corresponding zero mode momentum.
Introduce the space

\displaystyle{\cal H}\Big[\small{\begin{matrix}m_{1},\ldots m_{r}\\
{\mathfrak{j}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathfrak{j}}_{r}\end{matrix}}\Big|\,s\,\Big]=\bigotimes_{b=1}^{r}\Big(\,{\cal V}_{{\mathfrak{j}}_{b},{m}_{b}}^{(k_{b})}\otimes{\cal F}^{(b)}_{P_{b}({m}_{b}|s)}\,\Big)\ ,

(6.10)

where

\displaystyle P_{b}({m}_{b}|s)=\tfrac{1}{2}\ \big(\,\tfrac{{m}_{b}}{\sqrt{k_{b}}}+{s}\,\sqrt{k_{b}}\,\big)

(6.11)

and

s
is an arbitrary number.
The operators
V^{(a)}_{\pm}
act as the intertwiners

\displaystyle V^{(a)}_{\pm}\ :\ \ \ \ {\cal H}\bigg[\begin{matrix}m_{1},\ldots,m_{r}\\
{\mathfrak{j}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathfrak{j}}_{r}\end{matrix}}\bigg|\,s\,\bigg]\mapsto{\cal H}\bigg[\small{\begin{matrix}m_{1},\ldots,m_{a}\pm 2,\ldots,m_{r}\\
{\mathfrak{j}}_{1},\ldots,\phantom{\pm 2}{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}\phantom{\pm 2},\ldots,{\mathfrak{j}}_{r}\end{matrix}\bigg|\,s\pm\tfrac{2}{K}\,(\beta-1)\,\bigg]$\cr\lx@close@alignment andhenceinvariantlywithinthedirectsum\@@eqnarray Inotherwords,foranygiven
