7
ODE/IQFT correspondence

7.1
ODE for the
W
-primary states

Provided that the integer
\mathfrak{m}
is restricted as in (
),
the spectrum of the operator
{\bf I}_{1}
in
{\cal H}_{\mathfrak{j},\mathfrak{m},P}

is given by

I_{1}=P^{2}-\frac{{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}}{4K}+\sum_{a=1}^{r}\frac{{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}({\mathfrak{j}}_{a}+1)}{k_{a}+2}-\frac{1}{24}\,\sum_{a=1}^{r}\frac{3k_{a}}{k_{a}+2}+{\tt L}\qquad\qquad({\tt L}=0,1,2,\ldots)\,.

(7.1)

The dimensions of the subspace of primary states,
{\cal H}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}},P}^{(0)}
,
can be read off from the formula

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathfrak{m}}}\dim\big[{\cal H}^{(0)}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}},P}\big]\ {\tt q}^{\mathfrak{m}}=\prod_{a=1}^{r}\,[2\mathfrak{j}_{a}+1]_{{\tt q}}

(7.2)

with
[n]_{{\tt q}}=({\tt q}^{n}-{\tt q}^{-n})/({\tt q}-{\tt q}^{-1})
.
When
{\mathfrak{m}}=\pm 2\sum_{a=1}^{r}{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}

the corresponding eigenspaces
are one-dimensional and spanned by the ground states

{\bm{e}}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\pm 2{\mathfrak{J}},P}=\bigotimes_{a=1}^{r}\Big(\,\big|\,\sigma^{(k_{a})}_{{\mathfrak{j}}_{a},\pm 2{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}}\,\big\rangle\otimes\big|P^{({\rm vac},\pm)}_{a}\big\rangle\Big)\,,\qquad P^{({\rm vac},\pm)}_{a}=P\,\sqrt{\frac{k_{a}}{K}}\pm\sum_{b=1}^{r}\frac{{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}k_{b}-{\mathfrak{j}}_{b}k_{a}}{K\sqrt{k_{a}}}

(7.3)

The path-ordered integral formulae for

{\bm{\tau}}_{\ell}(\lambda)
and

{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{\pm}(\lambda)

allow one to express their matrix elements
as a convergent power series in
\lambda^{2}
. The
corresponding expansion coefficients are given by
the well defined multifold contour integrals (for further details see
Bazhanov:1998dq
).
In the simplest set-ups, it is possible to calculate them directly. For example
for the vacuum states (
7.3
), one can show that

\displaystyle{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{+}(\lambda)\,{\bm{e}}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\pm 2{\mathfrak{J}},P}={{{{\it a}}}}^{({\rm vac},\pm)}_{+}(\lambda)\,{\bm{e}}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\pm 2{\mathfrak{J}},P}\ :\ \ \ \ \log{{{{\it a}}}}^{({\rm vac},\pm)}_{+}(\lambda)=-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}H^{({\rm vac},\pm)}_{n}\,\lambda^{2n}

(7.4)

with

\displaystyle H_{1}^{({\rm vac},\pm)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\,\frac{\pi\Gamma\big(-1+\frac{2}{K}(1-\beta^{2})\big)}{\sin(\frac{\pi}{K}(1-\beta^{2}))}\ \frac{\Gamma\big(1-\frac{1}{K}(1-\beta^{2})+\frac{1}{K}\,\eta^{\rm(vac,\pm)}\big)}{\Gamma\big(\frac{1}{K}(1-\beta^{2})+\frac{1}{K}\,\eta^{\rm(vac,\pm)}\big)}\ \ \sum_{a=1}^{r}k_{a}z_{a}

(7.5)

\displaystyle\pm

\displaystyle\frac{\pi\Gamma\big(\frac{2}{K}(1-\beta^{2})\big)}{\sin(\frac{\pi}{K}(1-\beta^{2}))}\ \frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{1\pm 1}{2}-\frac{1}{K}(1-\beta^{2})+\frac{1}{K}\,\eta^{\rm(vac,\pm)}\big)}{\Gamma\big(\frac{1\pm 1}{2}+\frac{1}{K}(1-\beta^{2})+\frac{1}{K}\,\eta^{\rm(vac,\pm)}\big)}\ \ \sum_{a=1}^{r}2\mathfrak{j}_{a}z_{a}

and

\displaystyle\eta^{\rm(vac,\pm)}=2\sqrt{K}\beta P\mp\sum_{a=1}^{r}2\mathfrak{j}_{a}\ .

(7.6)

However, the computation of the higher order coefficients
H_{2},\,H_{3},\ldots
, even for the ground states,
turns out to be highly cumbersome.
A practical way of studying the spectrum of
{\bm{\tau}}_{\ell}(\lambda)
and

{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{\pm}(\lambda)
is through the ODE/IQFT correspondence
for the commuting family, which is proposed below.

Generalizing eq.(
5.14
) we consider the ODE

\displaystyle\bigg[\!\!\!

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\partial_{z}^{2}+\kappa^{2}\,z^{-2+\xi\,{\sum_{a=1}^{r}}k_{a}}\,\prod_{a=1}^{r}(z-z_{a})^{k_{a}}

(7.7)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{{A}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}}{z^{2}}+\sum_{a=1}^{r}\bigg(\,\frac{{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}({\mathfrak{j}}_{a}+1)}{(z-z_{a})^{2}}+\frac{z_{a}\gamma_{a}}{z(z-z_{a})}\,\bigg)\,\bigg]\ \Psi=0\,,

where
\xi>0
and
{\bm{k}}=(k_{1},\ldots k_{r})
is a set of positive integers.
As before the singularities at
z=z_{a}\ (a=1,\ldots,r)
are required to be apparent. Repeating the same
line of arguments as for the case
r=1
one arrives at the following conditions imposed on the parameters.
An immediate one is that

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}\in\big\{0,\tfrac{1}{2},1,\ldots,\tfrac{k_{a}}{2}\big\}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ (a=1,\ldots,r)\ .

(7.8)

Then for some given (half-)integers
({\mathfrak{j}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathfrak{j}}_{r})
and fixed value of
A
,
the set
{\bm{\gamma}}=(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{r})

should solve the system of algebraic equations

\displaystyle F_{{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}}\big(v^{(a)}_{1},\ldots,v^{(a)}_{2{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}+1}\big)=0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ (a=1,\ldots,r)\ .

(7.9)

Here
v^{(a)}_{m}=v^{(a)}_{m}({\bm{\gamma}})\ \ (m=1,\ldots,2{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}+1)
are defined though the Laurent expansion of

\displaystyle t_{0}(z)=-\frac{{A}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}}{z^{2}}+\sum_{a=1}^{r}\bigg(\,\frac{{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}({\mathfrak{j}}_{a}+1)}{(z-z_{a})^{2}}+\frac{z_{a}\gamma_{a}}{z(z-z_{a})}\,\bigg)

(7.10)

in the vicinity of
z=z_{a}
:

\displaystyle t_{0}(z)=\frac{1}{(z-z_{a})^{2}}\ \Big(v^{(a)}_{0}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}v_{m}^{(a)}\,(z-z_{a})^{m}\,\Big)\,.

(7.11)

The polynomials

F_{{\mathfrak{j}}}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{2{\mathfrak{j}}+1})
are given by the determinant (
5.19
).

Numerical work shows that, if
A
is generic,
the algebraic system (
7.9
) possesses
\prod_{a=1}^{r}(2{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}+1)
solutions.
These
are splitted on the classes labeled by an integer

{\mathfrak{m}}

such that

\displaystyle\sum_{a=1}^{r}z_{a}\gamma_{a}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,{\mathfrak{m}}\,\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\,{\mathfrak{m}}+2{\rm i}A\big)-\sum_{a=1}^{r}{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}({\mathfrak{j}}_{a}+1)\,,

(7.12)

where
{\mathfrak{m}}
is restricted as in eq.(
), i.e.,

\mathfrak{m}=-2\mathfrak{J},\,-2\mathfrak{J}-2,\ldots,\,2\mathfrak{J}-2,2\mathfrak{J}
.
The connection coefficients are defined similar to the case
r=1
. Namely,

D_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\pm\mathfrak{m},\pm A}=\frac{W_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\pm\mathfrak{m},\pm A}(\mu)}{W_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\pm\mathfrak{m},\pm A}(0)}\ \ \ \ \quad{\rm with}\ \ \ \ \ W_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\pm\mathfrak{m},\pm A}(\mu)=\mu^{\mp(\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{m}+{\rm i}A)}\ W\big[\,\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\rightarrow)}},\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\leftarrow)}}_{\pm A}\,\big]

(7.13)

They are treated as a function of

\mu\,:\ \ \ \kappa^{2}=\mu^{(1+\xi)K}\ .

(7.14)

Let
\bm{\gamma}
be a solution of (
7.9
).
We expect that there exists a
state labeled by this set

\displaystyle{\bm{e}}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},P}({\bm{\gamma}})\in{\cal H}^{(0)}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},P}

(7.15)

such that it is a simultaneous eigenstate for
the operators of the commuting family
with

\displaystyle{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{\pm}(\lambda)\,{\bm{e}}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}},P}({\bm{\gamma}})=D_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\pm\mathfrak{m},\pm A}(\mu|{\bm{\gamma}})\,{\bm{e}}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},P}({\bm{\gamma}})\ .

(7.16)

The parameters on the ODE and the field theory side are related as

\displaystyle\xi=\tfrac{\beta^{2}}{1-\beta^{2}}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \mu=-\lambda^{2}\ \Gamma^{2}\big(\tfrac{1-\beta^{2}}{K}\big)\,\big(\tfrac{K}{1-\beta^{2}}\big)^{\frac{2}{K}(1-\beta^{2})}\ ,

(7.17)

while

\displaystyle{A}=\tfrac{{\rm i}}{\beta^{-1}-\beta}\ \big(\,\sqrt{K}\,P-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\beta\,\mathfrak{m}\,\big)\ .

(7.18)

The states
{\bm{e}}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},P}({\bm{\gamma}})

form a basis in
{\cal H}^{(0)}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},P}
.

The support for the proposed ODE/IQFT correspondence
is based on the arguments that were
originally developed in the works

Bazhanov:1998wj
;
Bazhanov:2003ni
. Following ref.
Bazhanov:1998wj
, it is
straightforward to derive a
set of functional relations for various connection coefficients. The most fundamental of these is the
so-called quantum Wronskian relation

\big(q^{2}\big)^{2{\rm i}A+{\mathfrak{m}}}\,D_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},A}\big(q^{2}\mu\big)D_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},-\mathfrak{m},-A}(\mu)-D_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},A}(\mu)D_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},-\mathfrak{m},-A}\big(q^{2}\mu\big)=\big(q^{2}\big)^{2{\rm i}A+{\mathfrak{m}}}-1

(7.19)

with
q^{2}=\mbox{e}^{-\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{(1+\xi)K}}
. It holds true as long as all the
singularities
at
z=z_{a}
are apparent. Furthermore in this case
the connection
coefficients turn out to be
entire functions of
\mu
. On the other hand,
all the eigenvalues of
{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{\pm}(\lambda)

obey the quantum Wronskian relation,
which comes from the operator valued relation (
3.28
) with
\ell=0
.
This coincides with (
7.19
) upon making the identification
(
7.18
) as well as

\xi=\frac{\beta^{2}}{1-\beta^{2}}
and

\mu\propto\lambda^{2}
.
The precise
\mu-\lambda
relation (
7.17
) can be established via a first order
perturbative calculation in
\mu
of
D_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},A}
for (
7.7
)
with all
\mathfrak{j}_{a}=\mathfrak{m}=\gamma_{a}=0
and comparing the result with the corresponding
specialization of eq. (
7.5
).

For given
A
and
{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}}=({\mathfrak{j}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathfrak{j}}_{r})
,
there exists two
solutions of the algebraic system (
7.9
)
which are particularly simple:

\displaystyle\gamma^{{({\rm vac},\pm)}}_{a}=\frac{2{\mathfrak{j}}_{0}\mathfrak{j}_{a}}{z_{a}-z_{0}}+\sum_{b=1\atop b\not=a}^{r}\frac{2\mathfrak{j}_{a}\mathfrak{j}_{b}}{z_{a}-z_{b}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ (a=1,\ldots,r)\ ,

(7.20)

where

\displaystyle z_{0}=0\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \qquad{\mathfrak{j}}_{0}=\pm{\rm i}A-\tfrac{1}{2}\ .

(7.21)

The ODE (
7.7
) in this case takes the form

\bigg[\!-\partial_{z}^{2}+\kappa^{2}\prod_{a=0}^{r}(z-z_{a})^{k_{a}}+\sum_{a=0}^{r}\frac{{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}({\mathfrak{j}}_{a}+1)}{(z-z_{a})^{2}}+\sum_{0\leq a<b\leq r}\frac{2\,\mathfrak{j}_{a}\mathfrak{j}_{b}}{(z-z_{a})(z-z_{b})}\,\bigg]\Psi=0\ .

(7.22)

Here, together with the notations (
7.21
), we use

\displaystyle k_{0}=-2+\xi\,\sum_{a=1}^{r}k_{a}\ .

(7.23)

The integer
{\mathfrak{m}}
(
7.12
) for the solutions
\gamma^{{({\rm vac},\pm)}}_{a}

coincides with
\pm 2{\mathfrak{J}}
. Numerical work shows
that they correspond to the ground states

{\bm{e}}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\pm 2\mathfrak{J},P}

(
7.3
). Note that, in view of eq.(
7.18
),

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{j}}_{0}=\mp\frac{\sqrt{K}\,P}{\beta^{-1}-\beta}-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\beta}{\beta^{-1}-\beta}\ \sum_{a=1}^{r}{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}\ .

(7.24)

It is important to keep in mind that the connection coefficients

D_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\pm\mathfrak{m},\pm A}
are defined
with
\mathfrak{m}
ranging from
-2\mathfrak{J}
to
+2\mathfrak{J}

as in eq. (
). In order to cover the case (
),
we introduce
\check{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}
,
\check{\mathfrak{m}}
and
\check{A}
via the formulae

\check{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,k_{a}-\mathfrak{j}_{a}\,,\qquad\qquad\check{\mathfrak{m}}=\mathfrak{m}-K\,,\qquad\qquad\check{A}=\tfrac{{\rm i}}{\beta^{-1}-\beta}\ \big(\,\sqrt{K}\,P-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\beta\,(\mathfrak{m}-K)\,\big)

(7.25)

and consider the ODE

\displaystyle\bigg[\!

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\partial_{z}^{2}+\kappa^{2}\,z^{-2+\xi\,{\sum_{a=1}^{r}}k_{a}}\,\prod_{a=1}^{r}(z-z_{a})^{k_{a}}

(7.26)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{\check{{A}}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}}{z^{2}}+\sum_{a=1}^{r}\bigg(\,\frac{\check{{\mathfrak{j}}}_{a}\,(\,\check{{\mathfrak{j}}}_{a}+1)}{(z-z_{a})^{2}}+\frac{z_{a}\check{\gamma}_{a}}{z(z-z_{a})}\,\bigg)\,\bigg]\ \Psi=0\,.

Here the set
\{\check{\gamma}_{a}\}
solves the system of equations (
7.9
)
with
A
and
{{\mathfrak{j}}}_{a}
replaced by their “checked” counterparts.
In turn, eq. (
7.12
) is swapped for

\sum_{a=1}^{r}z_{a}\check{\gamma}_{a}=\tfrac{1}{4}\,\check{\mathfrak{m}}\,\big(\check{\mathfrak{m}}+2{\rm i}\check{A}\big)-\sum_{a=1}^{r}\check{{\mathfrak{j}}}_{a}(\check{{\mathfrak{j}}}_{a}+1)\,.

(7.27)

Then the ODE/IQFT correspondence for the primary states

\displaystyle{\bm{e}}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},P}(\check{\bm{\gamma}})\in{\cal H}^{(0)}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},P}\qquad\qquad{\rm with}\qquad\qquad\mathfrak{m}=2\mathfrak{J}+2,\,2\mathfrak{J}+4,\ldots,\,2K-2\mathfrak{J}-2

(7.28)

is formulated as

\displaystyle{{{{\bf\it a}}}}_{\pm}(\lambda)\,{\bm{e}}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}},P}(\check{\bm{\gamma}})=D_{\check{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\pm\check{\mathfrak{m}},\pm\check{A}}(\mu|\check{{\bm{\gamma}}})\,{\bm{e}}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},P}(\check{{\bm{\gamma}}})\ .

(7.29)

Notice that

\big(q^{2}\big)^{2{\rm i}A+{\mathfrak{m}}}\,=\,\mbox{e}^{-\frac{4\pi{\rm i}}{\sqrt{K}}\,\beta P-\frac{2\pi{\rm i}\mathfrak{m}}{K}}\,=\,\mbox{e}^{-\frac{4\pi{\rm i}}{\sqrt{K}}\,\beta P-\frac{2\pi{\rm i}\check{\mathfrak{m}}}{K}}\,=\,\big(q^{2}\big)^{2{\rm i}\check{A}+\check{\mathfrak{m}}}

(7.30)

and therefore the quantum Wronskian relation
for the
connection coefficients
D_{\check{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\pm\check{\mathfrak{m}},\pm\check{A}}

is identical to (
7.19
) as long as the parameter
P
is kept fixed.
This is the first piece of support for the isomorphism (
).

7.2
Relation to the Bethe ansatz equations for the
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
Gaudin model

There is an alternative description of the sets

\bm{\gamma}
, which solve the algebraic equations
(
7.9
) going back to the works
Gaudin1
;
Feigin:2007mr
;
Feigin:1994in
;
Frenkel:2004qy
.
Assuming that
t_{0}
(
7.10
) is given, consider the Riccati equation for the unknown function
f_{0}=f_{0}(z)
,

\displaystyle t_{0}=f^{2}_{0}-\partial_{z}f_{0}\,,

(7.31)

so that
t_{0}
is the Miura transform of
f_{0}
.
One can search for a solution using the ansatz

\displaystyle f_{0}(z)=\frac{{\rm i}A-\frac{1}{2}}{z}+\sum_{a=1}^{r}\frac{\mathfrak{j}_{a}}{z-z_{a}}-\sum_{m=1}^{{\tt M}_{+}}\frac{1}{z-x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}_{m}}\,,

(7.32)

which involves a set of parameters
\bm{x}=(x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}_{1},\ldots,x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}_{{\tt M}_{+}})
.
Substituting
f_{0}
into the Riccati equation gives

\displaystyle t_{0}(z)=-\frac{{A}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}}{z^{2}}+\sum_{a=1}^{r}\bigg(\,\frac{{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}({\mathfrak{j}}_{a}+1)}{(z-z_{a})^{2}}+\frac{\gamma_{a}}{z(z-z_{a})}\bigg)+\sum_{m=1}^{{\tt M}_{+}}\frac{r_{m}}{z-x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}_{m}}\ ,

(7.33)

where

\displaystyle\gamma_{a}=\mathfrak{j}_{a}\ \bigg(\,\frac{2{\rm i}A-1}{z_{a}}+\sum_{b=1\atop b\not=a}^{r}\frac{2\mathfrak{j}_{b}}{z_{a}-z_{b}}-\sum_{m=1}^{{\tt M}_{+}}\frac{2}{z_{a}-x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}_{m}}\,\bigg)

(7.34)

and

\displaystyle r_{m}=\sum_{n=1\atop n\not=m}^{{\tt M}_{+}}\frac{2}{x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}_{m}-x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}_{n}}-\frac{2{\rm i}A-1}{x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}_{m}}-\sum_{a=1}^{r}\frac{2\mathfrak{j}_{a}}{x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}_{m}-z_{a}}\,.

(7.35)

The requirement that
r_{m}=0
yields an algebraic system for the auxiliary parameters
x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}_{m}
:

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1\atop n\not=m}^{{\tt M}_{+}}\frac{2}{x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}_{m}-x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}_{n}}\,-\,\frac{2{\rm i}A-1}{x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}_{m}}\,-\,\sum_{a=1}^{r}\frac{2\mathfrak{j}_{a}}{x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}_{m}-z_{a}}=0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ (m=1,\ldots,{{\tt M}_{+}})\ .

(7.36)

Note that for
{\tt M}_{+}=0
, formula (
7.34
)
gives back the vacuum solution
\gamma_{a}^{({\rm vac},+)}
(
7.20
).
Moreover it is straightforward, using eqs. (
7.36
)
and
(
7.34
), to calculate the sum
\sum_{a=1}^{r}z_{a}\gamma_{a}
. This yields (
7.12
)
with
{\mathfrak{m}}
being related to
the non-negative integer
{\tt M}_{+}
as

\displaystyle{\tt{M}}_{+}=\sum_{a=1}^{r}{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathfrak{m}\ .

(7.37)

Having at hand a solution of eq. (
7.36
), the set

\bm{\gamma}
obtained via (
7.34
) would automatically
obey the conditions (
7.9
), which guarantee that the singularities
at
z=z_{a}
of the ODE are apparent. Our numerical work suggests that for
generic

A

there is a one-to-one correspondence between
\bm{x}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}
solving (
7.36
)
and
\bm{\gamma}
. However, for some specific values of
A
the correspondence breaks down.
For example upon taking

A=-\frac{{\rm i}}{2}
, eqs.(
7.34
), (
7.36
) imply that

\sum_{a=1}^{r}\gamma_{a}=0
. On the other hand, for
A^{2}=-\frac{1}{4}
,
the system (
7.9
) admits solutions with
\sum_{a=1}^{r}\gamma_{a}\neq 0
.

The ODE depends on
A^{2}
rather than
A
.
However eqs. (
7.34
) and (
7.36
)
are not invariant if the sign of
A
is flipped.
For this reason
the set
\bm{\gamma}
solving
(
7.9
) may be alternatively expressed as

\displaystyle\gamma_{a}=\mathfrak{j}_{a}\ \bigg(\!-\frac{2{\rm i}A+1}{z_{a}}+\sum_{b=0\atop b\not=a}^{r}\frac{2\mathfrak{j}_{b}}{z_{a}-z_{b}}-\sum_{m=1}^{{\tt M}_{-}}\frac{2}{z_{a}-{x}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(-)}}_{m}}\,\bigg)\,.

(7.38)

Here
{x}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(-)}}_{m}
are subject to the equations

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1\atop n\not=m}^{{\tt M}_{-}}\frac{2}{{x}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(-)}}_{m}-{x}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(-)}}_{n}}\,+\,\frac{2{\rm i}A+1}{{x}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(-)}}_{m}}\,-\,\sum_{a=1}^{r}\frac{2\mathfrak{j}_{a}}{{x}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(-)}}_{m}-z_{a}}=0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ (m=1,\ldots,{{\tt M}_{-}})\ ,

(7.39)

while

{\tt{M}}_{-}=\sum_{a=1}^{r}{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathfrak{m}\ .

(7.40)

When
A=-\frac{{\rm i}}{2}
the algebraic systems (
7.36
) and (
7.39
)
are identical to the
Bethe ansatz equations for the Gaudin model eqs. (
1.5
) and (
1.11
),
respectively.
As follows from a comparison of eqs. (
1.4
)
and (
7.34
), the set of energies
\{E_{a}\}_{a=1}^{r}

coincides with
\{\gamma_{a}\}_{a=1}^{r}
.
An immediate question arises as to whether there exists a generalization of the Hamiltonians
(
1.2
), which results in the Bethe ansatz equations (
7.36
)
with arbitrary
A
. An evident candidate is

{\bf H}^{(a)}=\frac{2}{z_{a}}\,\vec{S}^{(0)}\cdot\vec{S}^{(a)}+2\ \sum_{b=1\atop b\not=a}^{r}\frac{\vec{S}^{(a)}\cdot\vec{S}^{(b)}}{z_{a}-z_{b}}\ ,

(7.41)

where
\vec{S}^{(0)}
is a spin operator that acts in a highest weight
infinite dimensional representation (Verma module) of
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
.
The corresponding Casimir would be given by

\big(\vec{S}^{(0)}\big)^{2}=\mathfrak{j}_{0}\,(\mathfrak{j}_{0}+1)\ \qquad\qquad\qquad\big(2\mathfrak{j}_{0}\notin\mathbb{Z}\big)\,,

(7.42)

while the value of
S_{3}^{(0)}
on the highest weight is
\mathfrak{j}_{0}={\rm i}A-\frac{1}{2}
.
The diagonalization problem can be considered in the
finite dimensional eigenspaces of the operator
S_{3}^{(0)}+\sum_{a=1}^{r}S_{3}^{(a)}
,
which commutes with the Hamiltonians.
If instead of the highest weight, one takes the lowest weight
representation for
\vec{S}^{(0)}
with lowest weight
-1-\mathfrak{j}_{0}
,
the spectral problem for the Hamiltonians (
7.41
)
would lead to the Bethe ansatz equations (
7.39
).

7.3
Excited states ODE

In ref.
Bazhanov:2013cua
, developing the ideas from
Bazhanov:2003ni
,
the ODE/IQFT correspondence was proposed for the highest state irreps of the pillow brane
W
-algebra.
Here, following the construction from that work, we extend the
correspondence to
all the eigenstates of the commuting family of operators.
These form
a basis in the highest weight irrep of
{W}^{(c,r)}_{{\bm{k}}}
.
Such basic states

\displaystyle{\bm{e}}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},P}({\bm{\gamma}};\bm{w})\in{\cal H}^{({\tt L})}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},P}

(7.43)

would be
distinguished by the sets
(\bm{\gamma};\bm{w})=(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{r},w_{1},\ldots,w_{\tt L})
,
which are solutions of a certain algebraic system.
The latter
has already been discussed for
{\tt L}=0
.
The system of equations imposed on

({\bm{\gamma}};\bm{w})

for general
{\tt L}=0,1,2,\ldots
is described as follows.

Consider the meromorphic function

t_{\tt L}(z)=-\frac{{A}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}}{z^{2}}+\sum_{a=1}^{r}\bigg(\,\frac{{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}({\mathfrak{j}}_{a}+1)}{(z-z_{a})^{2}}+\frac{z_{a}\gamma_{a}}{z(z-z_{a})}\bigg)+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\tt L}\bigg(\,\frac{2}{(z-w_{\alpha})^{2}}+\frac{w_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\alpha}}{z(z-w_{\alpha})}\bigg)

(7.44)

which possesses second order poles at
z=z_{a}\ (a=1,\ldots,r)
and
z=w_{\alpha}\ (\alpha=1,\ldots,{\tt L})
.
The corresponding residues are given by the sets

{\bm{\gamma}}=(\gamma_{1},\ldots\gamma_{r})
and

{\bm{\Gamma}}=(\Gamma_{1},\ldots,\Gamma_{\tt L})
.
Introduce

\displaystyle v^{(a)}_{m}\ \ \ \ (m=1,\ldots,2{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}+1)\ \ \ \ \ {\rm as\ well\ as}\ \ \ \ \ t^{(\alpha)}_{1},\,t^{(\alpha)}_{2},\,t^{(\alpha)}_{3}\ \ \ \ \ \ (\alpha=1,\ldots,{\tt L})

(7.45)

through the Laurent expansion of
t_{{\tt L}}(z)

in the vicinity of
z=z_{a}
and
z=w_{\alpha}
, respectively:

\displaystyle t_{\tt L}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(z-z_{a})^{2}}\ \Big(v^{(a)}_{0}+\sum_{m=1}^{2{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}+1}v_{m}^{(a)}\,(z-z_{a})^{m}+\ldots\,\Big)

(7.46)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(z-w_{\alpha})^{2}}\ \Big(2+t_{1}^{(\alpha)}(z-w_{\alpha})+t_{2}^{(\alpha)}(z-w_{\alpha})^{2}+t_{3}^{(\alpha)}(z-w_{\alpha})^{3}+\ldots\,\Big)\ .

Assuming that
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{r})
and
(w_{1},\ldots,w_{\tt L})
are given, the residues

{\bm{\gamma}}

and

{\bm{\Gamma}}
are determined through the solution of the coupled system of
r+{\tt L}
equations:

\displaystyle F_{{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}}\big(v^{(a)}_{1},\ldots,v^{(a)}_{2{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}+1}\big)=0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ (a=1,\ldots,r)

(7.47)

\displaystyle t^{(\alpha)}_{1}\,\Big(\,\big(t^{(\alpha)}_{1}\big)^{2}-4t^{(\alpha)}_{2}\,\Big)+4t^{(\alpha)}_{3}=0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ (\alpha=1,\ldots,{\tt L})\ .

Here the polynomials

F_{{\mathfrak{j}}}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{2{\mathfrak{j}}+1})
are defined via the determinant (
5.19
).
The meaning of these equations should be obvious at this point: if
all
2{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}+1
are positive integers
they form the full set of conditions that all the singularities
of the Fuchsian differential equation
\big(-\partial_{z}^{2}+t_{{\tt L}}(z)\big)\Psi=0
are apparent
except for
z=0
and
z=\infty
.

Consider now the ODE

\displaystyle\big(\,-\partial_{z}^{2}+t_{\tt L}(z)+\kappa^{2}\,{\cal P}(z)\,\big)\ \Psi=0\,,

(7.48)

where
t_{\tt L}(z)
is given by (
7.44
), while

\displaystyle{\cal P}(z)=z^{-2+\xi\,{\sum_{a=1}^{r}}k_{a}}\,\prod_{a=1}^{r}(z-z_{a})^{k_{a}}\ .

(7.49)

We impose that all the singularities except
z=0,\infty

for
any
value of the parameter
\kappa
are apparent.
Eqs.(
7.47
) guarantee this property for
\kappa=0
so that
they are necessary conditions. Furthermore,
as was already discussed, for generic
\kappa
the admissible values of
{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}

must be restricted as in (
7.8
):

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}\in\big\{0,\tfrac{1}{2},1,\ldots,\tfrac{k_{a}}{2}\big\}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ (a=1,\ldots,r)\ .

(7.50)

Also it turns out that the positions
of the apparent singularities
w_{\alpha}

may not be chosen at will. Instead they
should satisfy
{\tt L}
extra conditions (for details see
Bazhanov:2013cua
)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\alpha}=\partial_{z}\log{\cal P}(z)|_{z=w_{\alpha}}\ ,

(7.51)

or explicitly

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\alpha}=-\Big(2-\xi\,{\sum_{b=1}^{r}}k_{b}\Big)\ \frac{1}{w_{\alpha}}+\sum_{b=1}^{r}\frac{k_{b}}{w_{\alpha}-z_{b}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ (\alpha=1,\ldots,{\tt L})\ .

(7.52)

This expresses the set of residues
\bm{\Gamma}
through
\bm{w}
.
Thus (
7.47
) becomes
a system of
r+{\tt L}
equations imposed on the
r+{\tt L}
variables

(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{r};w_{1},\ldots,w_{\tt L})
.
Similar to the case of
{\tt L}=0
,
its solutions
are splitted on the classes labeled by an integer

{\mathfrak{M}}

such that

\displaystyle\sum_{a=1}^{r}z_{a}\gamma_{a}+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\tt L}w_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\alpha}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,{\mathfrak{M}}\,\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\,{{\mathfrak{M}}}+2{\rm i}A\big)-2\,{\tt L}-\sum_{a=1}^{r}{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}({\mathfrak{j}}_{a}+1)\ .

(7.53)

This integer takes the values

\mathfrak{M}=-\mathfrak{M}_{\rm max},-\mathfrak{M}_{\rm max}+2,\ldots,\mathfrak{M}_{\rm max}-2,\mathfrak{M}_{\rm max}

(7.54)

with some
\mathfrak{M}_{\rm max}
. Numerical work suggests that

2\mathfrak{J}\leq\mathfrak{M}_{\rm max}\leq 2\mathfrak{J}+2{\tt L}\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\Big({\mathfrak{J}}=\sum_{a=1}^{r}{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}\Big)\,.

(7.55)

An alternative description is provided if one considers,
rather than
t_{\tt L}(z)
, the solution of the Riccati equation

t_{\tt L}=f_{\tt L}^{2}-\partial_{z}f_{\tt L}
.
As explained in sec.
7.2
, it leads one to introduce
the auxiliary sets
\bm{x}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\pm)}}=(x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\pm)}}_{1},\ldots,x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\pm)}}_{{\tt M}_{\pm}})
.
These would parameterize the residues
\gamma_{a}
as

\displaystyle\gamma_{a}=\mathfrak{j}_{a}\,\bigg(\,\frac{\pm 2{\rm i}A-1}{z_{a}}+\sum_{b=1\atop b\neq a}^{r}\frac{2\mathfrak{j}_{b}}{z_{a}-z_{b}}-\sum_{n=1}^{{\tt M}_{\pm}}\,\frac{2}{z_{a}-x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\pm)}}_{n}}+\sum_{\beta=1}^{{\tt L}}\frac{2}{z_{a}-w_{\beta}}\,\bigg)\ ,

(7.56)

where

\displaystyle{\tt M}_{\pm}=\mathfrak{J}+{\tt L}\mp\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathfrak{M}\ .

(7.57)

Then

\displaystyle\frac{\pm 2{\rm i}A-1}{x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\pm)}}_{m}}+\sum_{a=1}^{r}\frac{2\mathfrak{j}_{a}}{x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\pm)}}_{m}-z_{a}}-\sum_{n=1\atop n\neq m}^{{\tt M}_{\pm}}\frac{2}{x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\pm)}}_{m}-x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\pm)}}_{n}}+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\tt L}\frac{2}{x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\pm)}}_{m}-w_{\alpha}}=0\,\qquad\ \ (m=1,2,\ldots,{\tt M}_{\pm})

(7.58)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\alpha}-\frac{\pm 2{\rm i}A-1}{w_{\alpha}}-\sum_{b=1}^{r}\frac{2\mathfrak{j}_{b}}{w_{\alpha}-z_{b}}+\sum_{m=1}^{{\tt M}_{\pm}}\frac{2}{w_{\alpha}-x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\pm)}}_{m}}-\sum_{\beta=1\atop\beta\neq\alpha}^{{\tt L}}\frac{2}{w_{\alpha}-w_{\beta}}=0\,\ \ \ \ \quad(\alpha=1,2,\ldots,{\tt L})

together with (
7.52
) forms a closed system of
{\tt L}+{\tt M}_{\pm}

equations, which determine the sets

\bm{x}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\pm)}}=(x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\pm)}}_{1},\ldots,x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\pm)}}_{{\tt M}_{+}})
and
\bm{w}=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{\tt L})
. Finally
\bm{\gamma}=(\gamma_{1},\ldots\gamma_{r})
is obtained via eq. (
7.56
).

In order to formulate a precise conjecture regarding the ODE/IQFT correspondence,
there is an important issue that needs to be addressed. According to eqs. (
7.53
)-(
7.55
),
the algebraic system (
7.47
) admits
solutions
(\bm{\gamma};\bm{w})
with
|\mathfrak{M}|>2\mathfrak{J}
.
However, there exists a map

(\bm{\gamma};\bm{w})\mapsto(\tilde{\bm{\gamma}},\tilde{\bm{w}})
such that the
transformed set
satisfies the equations similar to (
7.47
) with the parameters
\mathfrak{j}_{a}
,
{\tt L}

and
A
replaced by

\tilde{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,k_{a}-\mathfrak{j}_{a}\,,\qquad\qquad\tilde{{\tt L}}={\tt L}-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(\,|\mathfrak{M}|-2\mathfrak{J}\,\big)\geq 0\,,\qquad\qquad\tilde{A}=A+\tfrac{\sigma{\rm i}}{2}\,\xi K\,,

(7.59)

\sigma={\rm sgn}(\mathfrak{M})
,
along with the conditions

\displaystyle\sum_{a=1}^{r}z_{a}\tilde{\gamma}_{a}+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\tilde{\tt L}}\tilde{w}_{\alpha}\tilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}\,\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\,{\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}}+2{\rm i}\tilde{A}\big)-2\,\tilde{{\tt L}}-\sum_{a=1}^{r}\tilde{{\mathfrak{j}}}_{a}(\tilde{{\mathfrak{j}}}_{a}+1)\,,

(7.60)

where

\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}=\begin{cases}\mathfrak{M}-K\,&\qquad{\rm for}\ \ \ \ \ \mathfrak{M}>+2\mathfrak{J}\\[5.69046pt]
\mathfrak{M}+K\,&\qquad{\rm for}\ \ \ \ \ \mathfrak{M}<-2\mathfrak{J}\end{cases}\ .

(7.61)

For
\mathfrak{M}>2\mathfrak{J}
the transformation is described as follows.
Suppose that
w_{\alpha}
and
\gamma_{a}
are given.
The set
\tilde{\bm{w}}_{\alpha}
is the solution of the
equations:

\frac{2{\rm i}A-1}{\tilde{w}_{\alpha}}+\sum_{b=1}^{r}\frac{2\mathfrak{j}_{b}}{\tilde{w}_{\alpha}-z_{b}}-\sum_{\beta=1\atop\beta\neq\alpha}^{\tilde{{\tt L}}}\frac{2}{\tilde{w}_{\alpha}-\tilde{w}_{\beta}}+\sum_{\beta=1}^{\tt L}\frac{2}{\tilde{w}_{\alpha}-w_{\beta}}=0\,\qquad\quad(\alpha=1,2,\ldots,\tilde{{\tt L}})\,,

(7.62)

which is uniquely specified by the extra conditions

\frac{2{\rm i}A-1}{z_{a}}+\sum_{b=1\atop b\neq a}^{r}\frac{2\mathfrak{j}_{b}}{z_{a}-z_{b}}-\sum_{\beta=1}^{\tilde{{\tt L}}}\,\frac{2}{z_{a}-\tilde{w}_{\beta}}+\sum_{\beta=1}^{{\tt L}}\frac{2}{z_{a}-w_{\beta}}=\frac{\gamma_{a}}{\mathfrak{j}_{a}}\qquad\qquad\ (a=1,2,\ldots,r)\,.

(7.63)

Once
\tilde{\bm{w}}
is found, the residues
\tilde{\bm{\gamma}}

are obtained from

\displaystyle\tilde{\gamma}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}\,\bigg(\frac{-2{\rm i}\tilde{A}-1}{z_{a}}+\sum_{b\neq a}\frac{2\tilde{\mathfrak{j}}_{b}}{z_{a}-z_{b}}-\sum_{\beta=1}^{{\tt L}}\frac{2}{z_{a}-w_{\beta}}+\sum_{\beta=1}^{\tilde{\tt L}}\frac{2}{z_{a}-\tilde{w}_{\beta}}\bigg)\,,

(7.64)

while
\tilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha}
is expressed in terms of
\tilde{w}_{\alpha}
as

\displaystyle\tilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha}=\partial_{z}\log{\cal P}(z)|_{z=\tilde{w}_{\alpha}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \qquad(\alpha=1,\ldots,\tilde{{\tt L}})\ .

(7.65)

If one now considers the change of variables

\widetilde{\Psi}=\big({\cal P}(z)\big)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,\bigg(\partial_{z}+\frac{2{\rm i}A-1}{2z}+\sum_{a=1}^{r}\frac{\mathfrak{j}_{a}}{z-z_{a}}+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{{\tt L}}\frac{1}{z-w_{\alpha}}-\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\tilde{\tt L}}\frac{1}{z-\tilde{w}_{\alpha}}\bigg)\,\Psi\,,

(7.66)

the ODE (
7.48
), (
7.44
)
becomes

\displaystyle\bigg[

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\partial_{z}^{2}+\kappa^{2}\,{\cal P}(z)-\frac{\tilde{A}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}}{z^{2}}+\sum_{a=1}^{r}\bigg(\,\frac{\tilde{{\mathfrak{j}}}_{a}(\tilde{{\mathfrak{j}}}_{a}+1)}{(z-z_{a})^{2}}+\frac{z_{a}\tilde{\gamma}_{a}}{z(z-z_{a})}\bigg)

(7.67)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\tilde{\tt L}}\bigg(\,\frac{2}{(z-\tilde{w}_{\alpha})^{2}}+\frac{\tilde{w}_{\alpha}\tilde{\Gamma}_{\alpha}}{z(z-\tilde{w}_{\alpha})}\bigg)\bigg]\widetilde{\Psi}=0\,.

For the other case with
\mathfrak{M}<-2\mathfrak{J}
,
the sign of
A
and
\tilde{A}
in eqs. (
7.62
)-(
7.66
)
needs to be flipped.

An important point is that
since
\Psi
and
\widetilde{\Psi}
are related through the action of a
first order differential operator, the monodromic properties of the ODEs
(
7.48
) and (
7.67
) are the same.
In consequence, the corresponding connections coefficients for these differential equations
coincide.
Thus, although there exists the solutions sets

(\bm{\gamma};\bm{w})
with
|\mathfrak{M}|>2\mathfrak{J}
,
by performing the above transformation,
the value of
|\mathfrak{M}|
can be reduced.
By successive applications if necessary, we expect that
the integer
\mathfrak{M}
may always be brought to the interval

\mathfrak{M}=-2\mathfrak{J},-2\mathfrak{J}+2,\ldots,2\mathfrak{J}\ .

(7.68)

Like for the primary states, it is necessary to distinguish the cases
(i)

and
(ii)
from (
).
For the former, one makes the identification

{\rm case}\ (i)\ \ \ \ \ \qquad\qquad\mathfrak{M}=\mathfrak{m}=-2\mathfrak{J},\,-2\mathfrak{J}+2,\,\ldots,2\mathfrak{J}\ .

(7.69)

The connection coefficients

D_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\pm{\mathfrak{m}},\pm A}(\mu\,|\,\bm{\gamma},\bm{w})

are introduced similarly as in eq. (
7.13
).
They turn out to be entire
functions of

\displaystyle\mu=\kappa^{\frac{2}{(1+\xi)K}}

(7.70)

for any given choice
of
({\bm{\gamma}},\bm{w})
.
Since the singularities
w_{\alpha}
that were introduced are apparent,
the quantum Wronskian
relation (
7.19
) is still satisfied.
Interpreting
D_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\pm\mathfrak{m},\pm A}(\mu\,|\,{\bm{\gamma}},\bm{w})

as an eigenvalue of the operator

{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{\pm}(\lambda)
for a certain state
in the level subspace
{\cal H}^{({\tt L})}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},P}
,
the zero-mode momentum
P
would be related to
A
as in eq. (
7.18
).
With this set-up, we conjecture that the connection coefficients
coincide with certain eigenvalues of
{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{\pm}(\lambda)
:

{\rm case}\ (i)\qquad\qquad{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{\pm}(\lambda)\,{\bm{e}}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},P}({\bm{\gamma}},\bm{w})=D_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\pm\mathfrak{m},\pm A}(\mu\,|\,{\bm{\gamma}},\bm{w})\,{\bm{e}}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},P}({\bm{\gamma}},\bm{w})\,\in\,{\cal H}^{({\tt L})}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},P}\ .

(7.71)

Moreover, all such eigenstates

{\bm{e}}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},P}({\bm{\gamma}},\bm{w})

form a basis in
{\cal H}^{({\tt L})}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},P}\,
.
Of course, the
\xi-\beta
and
\mu-\lambda
relations remain unchanged
and are given by (
7.17
).

For the second case in (
) we make the identification

{\rm case}\ (ii)\qquad\qquad\mathfrak{M}=\check{\mathfrak{m}}=-2\check{\mathfrak{J}},\,-2\check{\mathfrak{J}}+2,\,\ldots,2\check{\mathfrak{J}}

(7.72)

with
\check{\mathfrak{m}}=\mathfrak{m}-K
and

\check{\mathfrak{J}}=\frac{1}{2}\,K-\mathfrak{J}
.
The conjectured ODE/IQFT correspondence is described as

{\rm case}\ (ii)\qquad\qquad{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{\pm}(\lambda)\,{\bm{e}}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},P}({\check{\bm{\gamma}}},\check{\bm{w}})=D_{\check{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\pm\check{\mathfrak{m}},\pm\check{A}}(\mu\,|\,\check{{\bm{\gamma}}},\check{\bm{w}})\,{\bm{e}}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},P}(\check{{\bm{\gamma}}},\check{\bm{w}})\,\in\,{\cal H}^{({\tt L})}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},P}\ .

(7.73)

Here
\check{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\check{\mathfrak{m}}
and
\check{A}
are defined in
(
7.25
). It is important to keep in mind that
(\check{{\bm{\gamma}}},\check{\bm{w}})
,
which label the states in
{\cal H}^{({\tt L})}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},P}
, are solutions of the algebraic system
(
7.47
) and satisfy (
7.53
) with
A
,
\mathfrak{j}_{a}
replaced by their
“checked” counterparts and
\mathfrak{M}=\check{\mathfrak{m}}
.

It is expected that the eigenstates of
{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{+}(\lambda)

form a basis in the representation

{\cal H}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},P}
,
which is fully specified by its eigenvalues.
The conjecture (
7.73
) implies that the spectrum of

{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{+}(\lambda)
in the
space
{\cal H}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},P}
with

\mathfrak{m}=2\mathfrak{J},\ldots,2K-2\mathfrak{J}-2
coincides with its spectrum in

{\cal H}_{\check{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\check{\mathfrak{m}},P}
.
This leads us to propose the isomorphism
of these two representations of the
{W}^{(c,r)}_{{\bm{k}}}
algebra, see eq. (
).

7.4
Some comments concerning the literature

Isotropic limit

In ref.
Bazhanov:1994ft
an expression for the
vacuum eigenvalue of the transfer-matrix

{\bm{\tau}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\lambda)

was presented for the case
r=1
.
It coincides with the grand canonical partition function of a
1D plasma of alternating charges. The latter, since the seminal work of Anderson and Yuval
Anderson
, is well
known to be the partition function of the
one-channel anisotropic Kondo model, where the impurity has spin
\frac{1}{2}

Fendley:1995kj
.
This allowed the techniques of ref.
Bazhanov:1994ft
and its further developments, including the ODE/IQFT correspondence,
to be transferred to different variants of the
quantum impurity problem such as the quantum dot
Lukyanov:2003rt
and the Coqblin-Schrieffer
model
Bazhanov:2003ua
.
In the work
Lukyanov:2006gv
, the partition function
of the multichannel anisotropic Kondo model was expressed in terms of the solution of the ODE (
5.1
).
Renewed interest in the Kondo problem
came from refs.
Gaiotto:2020fdr
;
Gaiotto:2020dhf
, where
some previously obtained facts concerning
the isotropic case were rediscovered. Here we explain the relation
between the differential equation (
7.7
) and the one that was
considered in those works.

The isotropic limit corresponds to taking
\beta\to 1^{-}
. It is a complicated problem
to perform the limit starting with the path-ordered exponent (
2.18
),
which in the context of the impurity
problem is interpreted as an imaginary time evolution operator in the interaction picture.
One of the main advantages of the ODE/IQFT correspondence is that it is well adapted for
exploring such a limit.
The corresponding technique was developed in the works
Lukyanov:2003rt
;
Bazhanov:2003ua
and
can be straightforwardly applied to the ODE (
7.48
).

Let us make a uniform shift of the variable
z
and the positions of the apparent singularities

\displaystyle z\mapsto z+\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\ ,\ \ \ z_{a}\mapsto z_{a}+\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ w_{\alpha}\mapsto w_{\alpha}+\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}

(7.74)

and rewrite the ODE (
7.48
) using the parameters

\displaystyle\widetilde{\kappa}^{2}=\kappa^{2}\,\varepsilon^{2-\xi K}\ ,\ \ \qquad\widetilde{A}=\varepsilon\,A\,.

(7.75)

Of course such an innocent
change of variables and redefinition of the parameters should not affect the properties of the
ODE.
We set

\displaystyle\varepsilon=\tfrac{2}{\xi K}

(7.76)

and consider the limit
\xi\to+\infty
keeping
\widetilde{\kappa}
and
\widetilde{A}
fixed.
This corresponds to
\beta=\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{1+\xi}}\to 1^{-}
.
Taking the isotropic limit in the differential equation (
7.48
), one obtains

\displaystyle\Big(\,-\partial_{z}^{2}+\widetilde{\kappa}^{2}\,\mbox{e}^{2z}\ \prod_{a=1}^{r}(z-z_{a})^{k_{a}}+{t}_{\tt L}^{({\rm iso})}(z)\,\Big)\ \Psi=0\ ,

(7.77)

where

\displaystyle{t}_{\tt L}^{({\rm iso})}(z)=-\widetilde{A}^{2}+\sum_{a=1}^{r}\bigg(\,\frac{{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}({\mathfrak{j}}_{a}+1)}{(z-z_{a})^{2}}+\frac{\gamma_{a}}{z-z_{a}}\bigg)+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\tt L}\bigg(\,\frac{2}{(z-w_{\alpha})^{2}}+\frac{\Gamma_{\alpha}}{z-w_{\alpha}}\bigg)

(7.78)

and the limiting form of (
7.52
) reads as

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\alpha}=2+\sum_{b=1}^{r}\frac{k_{b}}{w_{\alpha}-z_{b}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ (\alpha=1,\ldots,{\tt L})\ .

(7.79)

The sets
\bm{\gamma}
and
\bm{w}
still satisfy the algebraic system (
7.47
).
Of course, now
v^{(a)}_{m}
and
t^{(\alpha)}_{i}
(
7.45
) are defined by
(
7.46
) with
{t}_{\tt L}(z)
substituted by
{t}_{\tt L}^{({\rm iso})}(z)
.
In the isotropic limit the relation (
7.18
) becomes

\widetilde{A}=\tfrac{{\rm i}}{K}\ \big(\,2\sqrt{K}\,P-\mathfrak{m}\,\big)\ .

(7.80)

For the “vacuum” case
corresponding to the states
{\bm{e}}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\pm 2{\mathfrak{J}},P}

(
7.3
), one should set
{\tt L}=0
and choose
\gamma_{a}
to be

\displaystyle\gamma^{{({\rm vac},\pm)}}_{a}=-{\rm i}\widetilde{A}^{{({\rm vac},\pm)}}\ \frac{2\mathfrak{j}_{a}}{z_{a}}+\sum_{b=1\atop b\not=a}^{r}\frac{2\mathfrak{j}_{a}\mathfrak{j}_{b}}{z_{a}-z_{b}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ (a=1,\ldots,r)\,,

(7.81)

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}^{{({\rm vac},\pm)}}=\frac{2{\rm i}}{K}\ \Big(\,\sqrt{K}\,P\mp\sum_{a=1}^{r}{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}\,\Big)\ .

(7.82)

The ODE (
7.77
) boils down to

\displaystyle\bigg(\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\!\partial_{z}^{2}+\tilde{\kappa}^{2}\,\mbox{e}^{2z}\ \prod_{a=1}^{r}(z-z_{a})^{k_{a}}-\big(\widetilde{A}^{{({\rm vac},\pm)}}\big)^{2}-{\rm i}\widetilde{A}^{{({\rm vac},\pm)}}\ \sum_{a=1}^{r}\frac{2\mathfrak{j}_{a}}{z_{a}(z-z_{a})}

(7.83)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\!\sum_{a=1}^{r}\frac{{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}({\mathfrak{j}}_{a}+1)}{(z-z_{a})^{2}}+\sum_{1\leq a<b\leq r}\frac{2\,\mathfrak{j}_{a}\mathfrak{j}_{b}}{(z-z_{a})(z-z_{b})}\,\bigg)\,\Psi=0\ .

This equation with
r=k=1
and
{\mathfrak{j}}_{1}=0
originally appeared in refs.
Lukyanov:2003rt
;
Bazhanov:2003ua
in the
description of the isotropic Kondo model.
It is worth mentioning that the parameter
2{\rm i}P
can be identified
with
H/T
, where
H
is an external local magnetic field acting on the impurity spin, while
T
stands for the temperature.
Also
\widetilde{\kappa}=T_{K}/T
with
T_{K}
being the Kondo temperature.

The ODE (
7.83
) with
r>1
and arbitrary
k_{a}

was introduced in the recent work
Gaiotto:2020fdr
.
Note that in the case
\widetilde{A}^{{({\rm vac},\pm)}}=0
it corresponds to
the chiral primary states for the
\otimes_{a=1}^{r}\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{k_{a}}(2)
WZW model.
Indeed, as follows
from (
7.1
) with
P=\frac{\mathfrak{m}}{2\sqrt{K}}=\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\sum_{a=1}^{r}{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}
,
the value of the local IM
{\bf I}_{1}
is given by

\displaystyle I_{1}=\sum_{a=1}^{r}\frac{{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}({\mathfrak{j}}_{a}+1)}{k_{a}+2}-\frac{c_{\tt wzw}}{24}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \qquad\ {\rm where}\ \ \ \ \ \ \qquad c_{\tt wzw}=\sum_{a=1}^{r}\frac{3k_{a}}{k_{a}+2}\ .

(7.84)

Gaudin limit

The ordinary differential equation for the isotropic case (
7.77
)-(
7.78
)
still admits an interesting limit, which can be interpreted as a double scaling limit
of the original system.
7
7

7

The authors thank S. Lacroix and B. Vicedo for pointing out this possibility.

One performs the change of variables

z=\delta\ y

(7.85)

and takes
\delta\to 0
, keeping fixed the combinations

z_{a}=\delta\ y_{a}\,,\qquad\qquad\kappa_{\rm\scriptscriptstyle G}=\delta^{1+\frac{K}{2}}\ \widetilde{\kappa}\ .

(7.86)

Further assuming that
\widetilde{A}=0
, i.e.,

P=\frac{\mathfrak{m}}{2\sqrt{K}}

(7.87)

the differential equation becomes

\bigg[\,-\partial_{y}^{2}+\kappa_{\rm\scriptscriptstyle G}^{2}\,\prod_{a=1}^{r}(y-y_{a})^{k_{a}}+\sum_{a=1}^{r}\bigg(\,\frac{{\mathfrak{j}}_{a}({\mathfrak{j}}_{a}+1)}{(y-y_{a})^{2}}+\frac{\gamma_{a}^{(\rm\scriptscriptstyle G)}}{y-y_{a}}\bigg)+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\tt L}\bigg(\,\frac{2}{(y-v_{\alpha})^{2}}+\frac{\Gamma_{\alpha}^{(\rm\scriptscriptstyle G)}}{y-v_{\alpha}}\bigg)\bigg]\ \Psi=0\ .

(7.88)

Here

\Gamma_{\alpha}^{(\rm\scriptscriptstyle G)}=\sum_{b=1}^{r}\frac{k_{b}}{v_{\alpha}-y_{b}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ (\alpha=1,\ldots,{\tt L})\ ,

(7.89)

while the value of
\gamma_{a}^{(\rm\scriptscriptstyle G)}
and

v_{\alpha}
are solutions of an algebraic system, which ensures that the points
y=y_{a},v_{\alpha}
are apparent
singularities of the ODE. Of course, this system can be obtained via a limit of (
7.47
).
The differential equation (
7.88
) was originally
proposed by Feigin and Frenkel
Feigin:2007mr
in their study of the affine Gaudin model.

Finally we note that the simplest variants
of the ODE (
7.44
)-(
7.52
) were studied in
refs.
Voros:1999
;
Ito:2020ueb
.

8

Large
\mu
asymptotic expansion of
D_{\bm{{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}

8.1
Leading behaviour

The operators
{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{\pm}(\lambda)
,
which depend continuously on the spectral parameter
\lambda
, are generating functions
of the family of commuting operators. In this regard, of special interest is the large
\lambda

expansion of
{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{\pm}(\lambda)

Bazhanov:1996dr
.
With the ODE/IQFT correspondence at hand, it can be explored
via the study of the connection coefficients
D_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\pm\mathfrak{m},\pm A}(\mu)

at large
\mu
. Below we restrict our attention to
D_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},A}
.

A brief inspection of the ODE shows that
the connection coefficients
depend on
\mu

and the parameters
\{z_{a}\}_{a=1}^{r}
only through the combinations
z_{a}\mu
.
Thus a multiplication of all the
z_{a}
by the same factor can be absorbed into a redefinition of the spectral
parameter. Focusing on the case when

z_{a}\not=0
for any
a
, without loss of generality, one can set

\displaystyle\prod_{a=1}^{r}(-z_{a})^{k_{a}}=1\ .

(8.1)

Some
details of the derivation of the
leading terms in the asymptotic expansion of
D_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},A}(\mu)

at
\mu\to+\infty
have been relegated to Appendix
A
.
Assuming the convention (
8.1
) and also that none of the

z_{a}
are positive real numbers,
the final result reads as

D_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},A}(\mu)\asymp R_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},A}\ \mu^{\frac{{\rm i}A}{\xi}-\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{m}}\ \ \exp\bigg(\mu^{\frac{1}{2}(1+\xi)K}\ q_{-1}+o(1)\bigg)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \qquad\big(\mu\to+\infty)\,.

(8.2)

The asymptotic coefficients are given by

\displaystyle q_{-1}=\frac{1}{(1-\mbox{e}^{-{\rm i}\pi(1+\xi)K})\ (1-\mbox{e}^{{\rm i}\pi\xi K})}\ \oint_{{\cal C}}\mbox{d}z\,\sqrt{{\cal P}(z)}

(8.3)

and

R_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},A}=(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{m}}\,\big((1+\xi)K\big)^{\frac{2{\rm i}A+\mathfrak{m}}{(1+\xi)K}-\frac{1}{2}}\ \big(\xi K)^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2{\rm i}A}{\xi K}}\ \frac{\Gamma\big(1-\tfrac{2{\rm i}A}{\xi K}\big)}{\Gamma\big(1-\tfrac{2{\rm i}A+\mathfrak{m}}{(1+\xi)K})}\ \frac{\prod_{m=1}^{{\tt M}_{+}}x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}_{m}}{\prod_{a=1}^{r}(z_{a})^{\mathfrak{j}_{a}}\prod_{\alpha=1}^{\tt L}w_{\alpha}}\ .

(8.4)

The branch of the multivalued function in the integrand
in (
8.3
) is chosen in such a way that

\displaystyle\Im m\big(\sqrt{{\cal P}(z)}\,\big)\to 0\,,\ \ \ \qquad\Re e\big(\sqrt{{\cal P}(z)}\,\big)>0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\rm as}\ \ \ z\to+\infty\ .

(8.5)

To describe the closed path of integration

{\cal C}
,
let us define
the domain of the complex plane

\displaystyle{\cal{D}}=\big\{z\,:\ -\delta<\arg(z)<+\delta\,\big\}\!\begin{array}[]{c}\\[-11.38092pt]
{\displaystyle{{\cup}}}\end{array}\!\big\{z\,:\ |z|<\varepsilon\,\big\}\!\begin{array}[]{c}\\[-11.38092pt]
{\displaystyle{{\cup}}}\end{array}\!\big\{z\,:\ |z|>\varepsilon^{-1}\,\big\}\ .

Here the parameters
\varepsilon
and
\delta
should be taken to be sufficiently
small, such that
z_{a}\notin{\cal D}
for all
a=1,\ldots,r
.
The contour
{\cal C}
lies inside
\cal{D}
and
is the image of
a Pochhammer loop on the Riemann sphere under stereographic projection.
The homotopy class of the loop is schematically shown in fig.
1
.
It winds around the south and north poles of the sphere,
which are mapped to
z=0
and
z=\infty
, respectively.

N
S

Figure 1
:
The Pochhammer loop on the Riemann sphere.

The asymptotic coefficient
R_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},A}

(
8.4
) is interpreted as an eigenvalue of the so-called
reflection operator.
8
8

8

The asymptotic formula (
8.2
),
when combined with the quantum Wronskian relation (
7.19
),
yields an interesting identity

\displaystyle\frac{\prod_{m=1}^{{\tt M}_{+}}x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}_{m}\,\prod_{m=1}^{{\tt M}_{-}}x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(-)}}_{m}}{\prod_{a=1}^{r}(z_{a})^{2\mathfrak{j}_{a}}\prod_{\alpha=1}^{\tt L}w^{2}_{\alpha}}=1+{\rm i}\ \frac{{\tt M}_{+}-{\tt M}_{-}}{2A}\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\big(\,{\tt M}_{\pm}=\mathfrak{J}+{\tt L}\mp\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathfrak{M}\,\big)\,.

For the case
r=1
and
k=1
it was
discussed in refs.
Zamolodchikov:1995aa
;
Kotousov:2019ygw
;
Kotousov:2019nvt
;
Litvinov:2020zeq
.
9
9

9

In ref.
Kotousov:2019nvt
,
the auxiliary variables
x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\pm)}}_{m}
are not employed and the
eigenvalues of the reflection operator are expressed solely in terms of
the apparent singularities
w_{\alpha}
. This results in
more cumbersome formulae.
It also appears
in various applications of the ODE/IQFT correspondence.

8.2
Eigenvalues of the local IM in the GAGM

The omitted terms in (
8.2
),
denoted by
o(1)
, encode the eigenvalues of the
local and so-called dual non-local integrals of motion.
It is expected that
D_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},A}(\mu)
admits
a systematic asymptotic expansion of the form

D_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}(\mu)\asymp R_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}\ \mu^{\frac{{\rm i}A}{\xi}-\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{m}}\ \ \exp\Big(\mu^{\frac{1}{2}(1+\xi)K}\ q_{-1}\Big)\ \ B(\mu)\ X(\mu)\ \ \ \qquad\big(\mu\to+\infty\big)\,,

(8.11)

where the eigenvalues of the local and dual non-local IM
appear in the coefficients
of the formal power series
\log(B)=o(1)
and
\log(X)=o(1)
, respectively.
The series
\log(B)
can be studied within the standard WKB technique.
Carrying over the analysis in refs.
Lukyanov:2013wra
;
Bazhanov:2013cua
, one finds

\displaystyle\log(B)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{n-1}\ \frac{\Gamma(n-\frac{1}{2})}{2n!\sqrt{\pi}}\ \ q_{2n-1}\ \mu^{\frac{1}{2}(1-2n)(1+\xi)K}

(8.12)

with

\displaystyle q_{2n-1}=\big[\,\big(1-\mbox{e}^{{\rm i}\pi(2n-1)(1+\xi)K}\big)\ \big(1-\mbox{e}^{-{\rm i}\pi(2n-1)\xi K}\big)\,\big]^{-1}\ \oint_{{\cal C}}\mbox{d}z\ U_{2n}\,.

(8.13)

There is a simple recursion procedure to generate the densities
U_{2n}
for any
n
.
For instance,

\displaystyle U_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big({\cal P}(z)\big)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,\bigg(\,t_{\tt L}+\frac{4\,{\cal P}\partial^{2}_{z}{\cal P}-5\,(\partial_{z}{\cal P})^{2}}{16\,{\cal P}^{2}}\,\bigg)

\displaystyle U_{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big({\cal P}(z)\big)^{-\frac{3}{2}}\,\bigg(\,t_{\tt L}+\frac{4\,{\cal P}\partial^{2}_{z}{\cal P}-5\,(\partial_{z}{\cal P})^{2}}{16\,{\cal P}^{2}}\,\bigg)^{2}\ .

(8.14)

Also
in writing the numerical coefficients in the sum (
8.12
),
it was assumed that the densities are normalized by the condition

\displaystyle U_{2n}=\big({\cal P}(z)\big)^{\frac{1}{2}-n}\ \big(\,(\,t_{\tt L}\,)^{n}+\ldots\,\big)\ ,

(8.15)

where the omitted terms contain derivatives and
lower powers of
t_{\tt L}
(
7.44
).

Let us focus on the first nontrivial integral
q_{1}
. It turns out
that the particular choice of the integration contour
{\cal C}
is not essential. For this reason we consider

\displaystyle q_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\cal C})}}=\oint_{{\cal C}}\frac{\mbox{d}z}{\sqrt{{\cal P}}}\,\ \bigg(\,t_{\tt L}+\frac{4\,{\cal P}\partial^{2}_{z}{\cal P}-5\,(\partial_{z}{\cal P})^{2}}{16\,{\cal P}^{2}}\,\bigg)\,,

(8.16)

where
{\cal C}
is an arbitrary
closed
contour.
Independently on the choice of
{\cal C}
,
the following relation holds true

\displaystyle q_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\cal C})}}=\Big(\sum_{a=1}^{r}I^{(a)}_{1}\,z_{a}\partial_{z_{a}}\,\Big)f^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\cal C})}}_{1}\qquad\qquad{\rm with}\qquad\qquad f^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\cal C})}}_{1}=\oint_{{\cal C}}\frac{\mbox{d}z}{\sqrt{\cal P}}\,\frac{1}{z^{2}}\ .

(8.17)

The coefficients in the sum read explicitly as

I^{(a)}_{1}=\frac{2z_{a}{\gamma}_{a}}{k_{a}}-2z_{a}\,\sum_{\beta=1}^{{\tt L}}\frac{1}{z_{a}-w_{\beta}}-\frac{2z_{a}}{k_{a}}\,\sum_{b=1\atop b\not=a}^{r}\frac{d_{a}k_{b}+d_{b}k_{a}}{z_{a}-z_{b}}-\frac{2d_{a}}{k_{a}}\,\big(\xi K-2\big)-2d_{0}\ .

(8.18)

Here we use the notations

\displaystyle d_{a}=\frac{\mathfrak{j_{a}}(\mathfrak{j_{a}}+1)}{k_{a}+2}-\frac{1}{24}\ \frac{3k_{a}}{k_{a}+2}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ K=\sum_{a=1}^{r}k_{a}\ ,

(8.19)

while

d_{0}=-\frac{1}{8}-{\tt L}-\sum_{a=1}^{r}d_{a}+\frac{1}{(1+\xi)K}\ \Big(2{\tt L}-A^{2}+\sum_{a=1}^{r}\big(\,\mathfrak{j_{a}}(\mathfrak{j_{a}}+1)+z_{a}{\gamma}_{a}\big)+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\tt L}w_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\alpha}\Big)

(8.20)

In spite of the somewhat cumbersome expressions, the proof of the identity (
8.17
) is elementary.
Namely, taking into account eq.(
7.51
), it is straightforward to show that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\cal P}}\,\bigg(\,t_{\tt L}+\frac{4\,{\cal P}\partial^{2}_{z}{\cal P}-5\,(\partial_{z}{\cal P})^{2}}{16\,{\cal P}^{2}}\,\bigg)=\partial_{z}V_{1}\,+\,\sum_{a=1}^{r}I^{(a)}_{1}\ z_{a}\partial_{z_{a}}\bigg(\frac{1}{z^{2}\sqrt{\cal P}}\bigg)

(8.21)

with

\displaystyle{V}_{1}(z)=-\frac{2}{\sqrt{\cal P}}\,\bigg(\,\frac{1}{z}\ \big(d_{0}-\tfrac{\xi K-2}{16}\,\big)+\sum_{a=1}^{r}\frac{d_{a}-\frac{k_{a}}{16}}{z-z_{a}}+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\tt L}\frac{1}{z-w_{\alpha}}\,\bigg)\ .

(8.22)

Integrating both sides of (
8.21
) over the closed contour yields (
8.17
).

In the context of the ODE/IQFT correspondence,
q_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\cal C})}}

(
8.16
) should be interpreted as the eigenvalue of a certain local IM.
Formula (
8.17
) shows that this quantity is a linear
combination of
I^{(a)}_{1}
with
a=1,2,\ldots r
, where the
coefficients

z_{a}\partial_{z_{a}}f^{\scriptscriptstyle{({\cal C})}}_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\ \oint_{{\cal C}}\frac{\mbox{d}z}{\sqrt{\cal P}}\,\frac{z_{a}k_{a}}{z^{2}\,(z-z_{a})}

(8.23)

encode all of the dependence on the integration contour. Thus
I^{(a)}_{1}
would be expected to coincide with
the eigenvalues of a suitably chosen
set of IM

{\bf{I}}^{(a)}_{1}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\mbox{d}u}{2\pi}\ T^{(a)}_{2}(u)\,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \qquad(a=1,\ldots,r)\ .

(8.24)

As follows from the expression (
8.18
) and (
7.53
), with
\mathfrak{M}=\mathfrak{m}
,
the sum
\sum_{a=1}^{r}k_{a}I^{(a)}_{1}
simplifies and can be brought
to the form

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\xi K}\,\sum_{a=1}^{r}k_{a}I^{(a)}_{1}=\sum_{a=1}^{r}d_{a}-\frac{\mathfrak{m}^{2}}{4K}+P^{2}+{\tt L}\,,

(8.25)

where
A
was swapped in favour of
P
and the integer
\mathfrak{m}
according to formula (
7.18
).
Keeping in mind the definition of
d_{a}
(
8.19
),
this coincides with the eigenvalue (
7.1
)
of the local IM
{\bf I}_{1}

(
).

For future references we include the expressions for the eigenvalues of the local IM through the sets

\bm{x}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\pm)}}=(x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\pm)}}_{1},\ldots,x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\pm)}}_{{\tt M}_{\pm}})
,

\bm{w}=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{\tt L})

with
{\tt M}_{\pm}=\mathfrak{J}+{\tt L}\mp\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathfrak{m}

solving the
algebraic system (
7.3
):

\frac{k_{a}}{2z_{a}}\,I^{(a)}_{1}=\sum_{b=0\atop b\neq a}^{r}\,\frac{1}{z_{a}-z_{b}}\,\big(2\mathfrak{j}_{a}\mathfrak{j}_{b}-d_{a}k_{b}-d_{b}k_{a}\big)\,-\,\sum_{m=1}^{{\tt M}_{\pm}}\,\frac{2\mathfrak{j}_{a}}{z_{a}-x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\pm)}}_{m}}\,+\,\sum_{\beta=1}^{{\tt L}}\frac{2\mathfrak{j}_{a}-k_{a}}{z_{a}-w_{\beta}}\ .

(8.26)

Here
a=1,\ldots,r
but the summation index “
b
” in the first sum runs from
0
to
r
and

\displaystyle k_{0}\equiv\xi K-2\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \qquad\ {\mathfrak{j}}_{0}\equiv\pm{\rm i}A-\tfrac{1}{2}\ ,\qquad\ \ \ \ \ z_{0}=0\,.

(8.27)

Also
d_{0}
, defined by eq.(
8.20
), can be rewritten as

\displaystyle d_{0}=-\sum_{a=1}^{r}d_{a}+\frac{1}{(1+\xi)K}\,\bigg(\sum_{a=0}^{r}\mathfrak{j}_{a}+\tfrac{1}{2}+{\tt L}-{\tt M}_{\pm}\bigg)^{2}-{\tt L}-\frac{1}{8}\ .

(8.28)

It is instructive to compare (
8.26
) with the expression
(
1.4
) for the energies
E_{a}
in the original Gaudin model.

In principle, similar computations can be performed for the higher local IM.
Upon obtaining the expression for the density
U_{2n}
, one should
establish the identity

\displaystyle U_{2n}=\partial_{z}V_{2n-1}+\sum_{a=1}^{r}I^{(a)}_{2n-1}\ z_{a}\partial_{z_{a}}F_{2n-1}

(8.29)

which is the analogue of (
8.21
). The eigenvalues of the local IM
would be read off from the expansion coefficients in the sum.
The calculations are purely algebraic and straightforward, but
rather cumbersome. For the integral
I_{3}^{(a)}
, they were performed
only for the case
r=1
in ref.
Bazhanov:2013cua
.

8.3
Some comments on the eigenvalues of the dual nonlocal IM

The factor
X
in the asymptotic expansion (
8.11
) is a
formal power series of the form

\displaystyle\log X(\mu)\,\asymp\,-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}X_{n}\,{\mu}^{-\frac{1+\xi}{\xi}n}\ .

(8.30)

The coefficients
X_{n}

are interpreted as the eigenvalues of certain integrals of motion. However, contrary to
{\bf{I}}_{2n-1}^{(a)}

the operators can not be represented as integrals over the local chiral densities.
Following the terminology of ref.
Bazhanov:1996dr
we refer to these operators as the “dual nonlocal IM”.

The appearance of
X(\mu)
in the large
\mu
expansion of the
connection coefficient is related to the fact that the applicability of the WKB approximation to the
ODE (
7.44
)-(
7.52
) breaks down
in the vicinity of
z=0
. This makes it difficult to provide a
mathematically rigorous justification of the
asymptotic series (
8.11
) and, in turn, to calculate the expansion coefficients

X_{n}
. Nevertheless, the presence of
X(\mu)

in (
8.11
) can be argued for similarly as it was done in ref.
Bazhanov:1998wj
for the case
r=k=1
.

Consider the simplest variant of the ODE (
7.48
), (
7.44
):

\displaystyle\bigg(-\partial_{z}^{2}+\kappa^{2}\,z^{-2+\xi K}\,\prod_{a=1}^{r}(z-z_{a})^{k_{a}}-\frac{{A}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}}{z^{2}}\,\bigg)\ \Psi=0\,.

(8.31)

Taking into account the convention (
8.1
),
the change of variables
z=\zeta^{-1},\ \Psi(z)=\zeta^{-1}\,\tilde{\Psi}(\zeta)
brings the
differential equation to the form

\displaystyle\bigg(-\partial_{\zeta}^{2}+\kappa^{2}\,\zeta^{-2-(1+\xi)K}\,\prod_{a=1}^{r}\big(\zeta-z_{a}^{-1}\big)^{k_{a}}-\frac{{A}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}}{\zeta^{2}}\,\bigg)\ \tilde{\Psi}=0\,.

(8.32)

The latter looks similar to the original ODE with the
parameters substituted as
\xi\mapsto-1-\xi
,
z_{a}\mapsto z^{-1}_{a}
.
In the parametric domain

-1<\xi<0
,
this formal substitution can be interpreted as a duality transformation,
which allows one to relate the properties of the solutions in the vicinity of
z=\infty
and
z=0
.

The subject of our current interest is the domain with
\xi>0
.
It is mapped to
\xi<-1
under the reflection

\xi\mapsto-1-\xi
, which thus
can not be interpreted as a duality transformation. However,
since it relates the basic solutions
\Theta^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\leftarrow)}}_{\pm A}
,
defined through their behaviour
at the regular singular point
z=0
,
and
\Theta^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\rightarrow)}}
, which is the fast decaying
solution at the irregular singularity
z=\infty
, it leads to an important property for
the connection coefficients.
Namely, while

D_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}(\mu)
possesses the convergent Taylor series expansion in

\mu
, its large
\mu
asymptotic involves the formal power series
X(\mu)
in the “dual” parameter
\widetilde{\mu}
:

\displaystyle\kappa^{2}=\mu^{(1+\xi)K}={\widetilde{\mu}}^{\,-\xi K}\ .

(8.33)

In this work, we forgo a systematic study of the eigenvalues of the dual nonlocal IM.
An illustration for a specific example with
r=2
and
k_{1}=k_{2}=1
is provided in sec.
9.3
.

Finally we note that the eigenvalues of the transfer-matrices
{\bm{\tau}}_{\ell}(\lambda)

can be expressed through
D_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}(\mu)
by using the
set of well known functional relations. Hence, provided that the large
\mu
asymptotic expansion of
the connection coefficients is available in the whole complex
\mu
plane, one can straightforwardly
derive the large
\lambda
expansion of the eigenvalues of
{\bm{\tau}}_{\ell}(\lambda)
.
The latter are of special interest in the context
of the quantum impurity problem, where it permits a systematic study of the infrared fixed points
in the theory.

9
Example with
r=2
and
k_{1}=k_{2}=1

The local IM for the GAGM
have not been sufficiently studied in the general setup.
The case
r=k=1
has been explored in detail in the context of the quantum KdV theory

Bazhanov:1994ft
;
Bazhanov:1996dr
;
Bazhanov:1998dq
, while
some results are known for
r=1
and
k>1
.
The simplest case beyond the
quantum KdV model is when
r
is a positive integer but with all
k_{a}=1
.
Then the representation of

U_{q}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{b}}}_{-})
discussed in sec.
6

does not involve the parafermionic fields, leading to significant simplifications.
This section is devoted to an analysis of the case
r=2
and
k_{1}=k_{2}=1
.

9.1
W
-algebra
and the first local IMs

Taking
r=2
and
k_{1}=k_{2}=1
in eqs.(
) and (
6.1
), the vertex operators
V_{\pm}

become

\displaystyle V_{+}=\big(\mbox{e}^{{\rm i}\sqrt{2}\theta}+\mbox{e}^{-{\rm i}\sqrt{2}\theta}\big)\ \mbox{e}^{+{\rm i}\sqrt{2}\beta\varphi}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ V_{-}=\big(z_{1}\,\mbox{e}^{-{\rm i}\sqrt{2}\theta}+z_{2}\,\mbox{e}^{+{\rm i}\sqrt{2}\theta}\big)\ \mbox{e}^{-{\rm i}\sqrt{2}\beta\varphi}

(9.1)

with

\displaystyle\varphi=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,\big(\phi_{1}+\phi_{2}\big)\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \theta=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,\big(\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}\big)\ .

(9.2)

The exponent involving the Bose field
\theta
can be “fermionized”
as

\mbox{e}^{\pm{\rm i}\sqrt{2}\theta}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,\big(\chi_{1}\pm{\rm i}\,\chi_{2}\big)\ ,

(9.3)

where
\chi_{1,2}
are a pair of chiral Majorana fermion fields,
satisfying the OPE

\displaystyle\chi_{a}(u)\chi_{b}(0)=-\frac{{\rm i}}{u}\ \delta_{ab}+O(1)\ .

(9.4)

Then formula (
9.1
) becomes

\displaystyle V_{+}=\sqrt{2}\ \chi_{1}\,\mbox{e}^{+{\rm i}\sqrt{2}\beta\varphi}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ V_{-}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\ \big(\,(z_{1}+z_{2})\,\chi_{1}-{\rm i}\,(z_{1}-z_{2})\,\chi_{2}\,\big)\mbox{e}^{-{\rm i}\sqrt{2}\beta\varphi}\ .

(9.5)

The construction of the local IM
follows the procedure outlined in sec.
4
. First
we note that the OPEs of
V_{+}
with the spin
\tfrac{3}{2}
and spin 2 fields,

\displaystyle{S}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\rho\partial-{\rm i}\partial\varphi\,\big)\,\chi_{1}

(9.6)

\displaystyle{G}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\partial\varphi)^{2}+{\rm i}\rho\ \partial^{2}\varphi+\tfrac{{\rm i}}{2}\,\chi_{1}\partial\chi_{1}\,,

obey the condition (
4.2
) provided the parameter
\rho
is taken to be

\displaystyle\rho=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,\big(\beta^{-1}-\beta\big)\ .

(9.7)

The fields
S
and
G
form the
{\cal N}=1
super Virasoro algebra,
whose commutation relations are encoded in the singular part of the operator product expansions

\displaystyle{{S}}(u){S}(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{{\rm i}\,c_{\scriptscriptstyle{sV}}}{3\,(u-v)^{3}}+\frac{{\rm i}\,({{G}}(u)+{{G}}(v)\big)}{2(u-v)}+(u-v)\,\partial{S}{S}(v)+O\big((u-v)^{2}\big)

\displaystyle{{G}}(u){G}(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{c_{\scriptscriptstyle{sV}}}{2\,(u-v)^{4}}-\frac{{G}(u)+{{G}}(v)}{(u-v)^{2}}+{G}^{2}(v)+O(u-v)

(9.8)

\displaystyle{{G}}(u){S}(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{2}\ \frac{{{S}}(v)}{(u-v)^{2}}-\frac{\partial{{S}}(v)}{u-v}+{{G}}{{S}}(v)+O(u-v)

with the central charge
c_{\scriptscriptstyle{sV}}=\tfrac{3}{2}-6\rho^{2}
.
The Majorana fermion
\chi_{2}
also obeys the condition (
4.2
) simply because the OPE

\chi_{2}(u)V_{+}(v)
is not singular at
u=v
.
The space of local fields satisfying (
4.2
) can be generated by the
spin
\frac{3}{2}
field
S
and spin
\frac{1}{2}
field
\chi_{2}
.
Among such fields we will focus on those having integer Lorentz spin, which form a closed operator algebra.
The latter is a
W
-algebra that will be denoted by

W^{(c,2)}_{\bm{1}}\equiv{W}^{(c,2)}_{\bm{k}}\big|_{\bm{k}=(1,1)}
.

In sec.
4
, the linear subspace of
chiral local fields of
Lorentz spin
s
commuting with the screening charge

\oint\mbox{d}u\,V_{+}
was denoted by
{\cal W}^{(s)}
. In the case at hand

{\cal W}^{(2)}
is a linear span of three fields
G,\,\chi_{2}S
and
\chi_{2}\partial\chi_{2}
.
Out of these

\displaystyle T^{(e)}_{2}={{G}}+\tfrac{{\rm i}}{2}\,\chi_{2}\partial\chi_{2}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ T^{(o)}_{2}=\chi_{2}{S}+{\rm i}\tau\,\chi_{2}\partial\chi_{2}

(9.9)

with

\displaystyle\tau=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\ \big(\beta^{-1}-\beta\big)\ \frac{z_{1}+z_{2}}{z_{1}-z_{2}}

(9.10)

satisfy the second requirement (
4.7
) involving the
vertex operator

V_{-}
.
There is a shortcut way of checking the last statement.
In the construction of the densities (
9.9
), one can
alternatively start by considering the
space of local fields satisfying the condition (
4.2
), where

V_{+}
is replaced by
V_{-}
.
Parameterizing
z_{1,2}
via
the complex numbers
s
and
\alpha
as

\displaystyle z_{1}={\rm i}s\ \mbox{e}^{-{\rm i}\alpha}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ z_{2}=-{\rm i}s\,\mbox{e}^{{\rm i}\alpha}

(9.11)

(if one adopts the convention (
8.1
), then
s^{2}=1
),
the vertices take the form

\displaystyle V_{+}=\sqrt{2}\,\chi_{1}\,\mbox{e}^{+{\rm i}\sqrt{2}\beta\varphi}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ V_{-}=\sqrt{2}\,s\,\tilde{\chi}_{2}\,\mbox{e}^{-{\rm i}\sqrt{2}\beta\varphi}\,,

(9.12)

where

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}_{2}=\cos(\alpha)\,\chi_{2}+\sin(\alpha)\,\chi_{1}\ .

(9.13)

Then such local fields would be generated by the

\frac{3}{2}
and
\frac{1}{2}
spin fields

\displaystyle\tilde{S}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\rho\partial+{\rm i}\partial\varphi\,\big)\,\tilde{\chi}_{2}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \tilde{\chi}_{1}=\cos(\alpha)\,\chi_{1}-\sin(\alpha)\,\chi_{2}\ .

(9.14)

Again we introduce the
W
-algebra,
\widetilde{W}^{(c,2)}_{\bm{1}}
,
formed by the integer Lorentz spin fields
that commute with the screening charge
\oint\mbox{d}u\,V_{-}
. From the formal
algebraic point of view, it is equivalent to
W^{(c,2)}_{\bm{1}}
,
but the fields are expressed differently in terms of the current
\partial\varphi

and the original Majorana fermions
\chi_{1,2}
.
The two
W
-algebras are related through a so-called reflection operator.
It acts on the fields as

\displaystyle{\bf R}\,S\,{\bf R}^{-1}={\tilde{S}}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\bf R}\,\chi_{2}\,{\bf R}^{-1}={\tilde{\chi}}_{1}\ .

(9.15)

The characteristic property of the densities entering into the local IM is that
they are invariant under the reflection, up to a total derivative:

\displaystyle{\bf R}\,T_{s}\,{\bf R}^{-1}=T_{s}+\partial{\cal O}_{s-1}\ .

(9.16)

Note that this immediately implies that the local IM
commute with the reflection operator:
10
10

10

The reflection operator is part of the commuting family
and its eigenvalue already appeared in eq. (
8.2
) as the subleading asymptotic
coefficient
R_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},A}
.

{\bf I}_{s}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\mbox{d}u}{2\pi}\,T_{s+1}(u)\,:\ \ \ \ \ \ \qquad[\,{\bf I}_{s},{\bf R}\,]=0\,.

(9.17)

It is easy to check that the fields (
9.9
) possess the property (
9.16
).

Below we’ll argue that the local IM

\displaystyle{\bf I}^{(e)}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\mbox{d}u}{2\pi}\,\big(\,{{G}}+\tfrac{{\rm i}}{2}\,\chi_{2}\partial\chi_{2}\,\big)=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\mbox{d}u}{2\pi}\,\big(\,{\tilde{G}}+\tfrac{{\rm i}}{2}\,\tilde{\chi}_{1}\partial\tilde{\chi}_{1}\,\big)

(9.18)

\displaystyle{\bf I}^{(o)}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\mbox{d}u}{2\pi}\ \Big(\chi_{2}{S}+{\rm i}\tau\,\chi_{2}\partial\chi_{2}\Big)=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\mbox{d}u}{2\pi}\,\Big(\tilde{\chi}_{1}\tilde{S}+{\rm i}\tau\,\tilde{\chi}_{1}\partial\tilde{\chi}_{1}\Big)

are
linearly expressed in terms of
{\bf I}^{(1)}_{1}
and
{\bf I}^{(2)}_{1}
, whose eigenvalues
are given by eq. (
8.18
) with
r=2
,
k_{1}=k_{2}=1
. Namely,

\displaystyle{\bf I}^{(e)}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{4\xi}\ \big({\bf I}^{(1)}_{1}+{\bf I}^{(2)}_{1}\big)

\displaystyle{\bf I}^{(o)}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{4\sqrt{2\xi(1+\xi)}}\,\big({\bf I}^{(1)}_{1}-{\bf I}^{(2)}_{1}\big)\ .

(9.19)

In fact the first relation follows immediately. It was already mentioned (see eq.(
8.25
) and below)
that its r.h.s. coincides with
the local IM
{\bf I}_{1}
defined by (
) with
the local density
T_{2}
in (
). The latter, specialized to the case
r=2,\ k_{1}=k_{2}=1
,
should be compared with the integrand in the first line of eq. (
9.18
),
taking into account that

\displaystyle(\partial\theta)^{2}=G_{{\chi_{1}}}+{G}_{{\chi_{2}}}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ {\rm where}\ \ \ \ G_{{\chi_{a}}}=\tfrac{{\rm i}}{2}\,\chi_{a}\partial\chi_{a}\ .

(9.20)

One can proceed further and construct the higher spin densities
T_{s+1}
.
It is easy to see that the linear subspace
{\cal W}^{(3)}\subset W^{(c,2)}_{\bm{1}}
includes, together with
the derivatives of the fields from
{\cal W}^{(2)}
, a single local field
\partial{\chi}_{2}S
. The latter
does not satisfy the condition (
9.16
). The space
{\cal W}^{(4)}
, apart from total derivatives,
contains five composite fields

\partial SS
,
G^{2}
,
\chi_{2}\,GS
,
\partial^{2}\chi_{2}\,S
and
{G}_{{\chi_{2}}}^{2}
.
The first two of them, together with the spin
\frac{7}{2}
field
GS
are
defined through the regular terms in the OPE (
9.1
).
The spin 4 local field
{G}_{{\chi_{2}}}^{2}
is
the first regular term in the OPE
{G}_{{\chi_{2}}}(u){G}_{{\chi_{2}}}(v)
as
u\to v
.
The explicit expressions in terms of the Bose field
\partial\varphi
and
the Majorana fermions are quoted in Appendix
B
(see (
B.22
)-(
B.27
)).
Using these formulae and the reflection condition (
9.16
),
one obtains the local IM

\displaystyle{\bf I}^{(e)}_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\mbox{d}u}{2\pi}\,\Big(G^{2}+2{\rm i}\,\partial SS+6\,{G}_{{\chi_{2}}}G+\tfrac{3}{7}\,\big(3-8\rho^{2}+24\tau^{2}\big)\,G_{\chi_{2}}^{2}-6\tau\,\partial^{2}\chi_{2}\,S\Big)

\displaystyle{\bf I}^{(o)}_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\mbox{d}u}{2\pi}\,\Big(\chi_{2}\,GS-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\tau\,G^{2}+\tau\,G_{\chi_{2}}G-\tfrac{3}{14}\,\tau\,\big(8\rho^{2}-8\tau^{2}-7\big)\,G_{\chi_{2}}^{2}

(9.21)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\big(\rho^{2}-\tau^{2}-\tfrac{3}{4}\big)\,\partial^{2}\chi_{2}\,S\Big)

Again, the eigenvalues of certain linear combinations of these operators are expected to
coincide with the coefficients
I_{3}^{(a)}\ (a=1,2)
from eq.(
8.29
) specialized to
n=2
.

9.2
Irreps of the
W
-algebra

The space
{\cal H}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}},P}
was introduced in sec.
6

and conjectured to be a highest weight representation of the algebra of local fields

{W}^{(c,r)}_{\bm{k}}
. It is labeled by
P
, which is the value of
\hat{a}_{0}
– the
zero mode momentum of
\varphi
(see eq. (
)),
the set
\bm{\mathfrak{j}}=(\mathfrak{j}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{j}_{r})

with
\mathfrak{j}_{a}=0,\frac{1}{2},\ldots,\frac{1}{2}\,k_{a}

and the integer
\mathfrak{m}\sim\mathfrak{m}+2K
such that

m
=-2
J
,-2
J
+2,…,2
J
-2,2
J

(
J
=∑_a=1^r
j
_a
)
.

Moreover, the representations

{\cal H}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}},P}
and

{\cal H}_{\check{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\check{{\mathfrak{m}}},P}

with

ˇ

j

_a=

1

2

k_a-
j
_a ,
ˇ

m

=
m
- K∼
m
+ K
(
K=∑_a=1^r k_a
)

are equivalent.
In the case when
r=2
,
k_{1}=k_{2}=1
we are left with four possibilities.
The irrep
{\cal H}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}},P}
corresponding to

\mathfrak{j}_{1}=\mathfrak{j}_{2}=\frac{1}{2}
and
\mathfrak{m}=\pm 2

is the same as the one with
\mathfrak{j}_{1}=\mathfrak{j}_{2}=\mathfrak{m}=0
.
As a shortcut, we’ll denote it by
{\cal H}_{0,0}
.
Similarly the case with
\bm{\mathfrak{j}}=(\frac{1}{2},0)
and
\mathfrak{m}=+1

is equivalent to the one where

\bm{\mathfrak{j}}=(0,\frac{1}{2})
and
\mathfrak{m}=-1
. The corresponding
space will be denoted by
{\cal H}_{1,-1}
. The notation
{\cal H}_{1,+1}

will stand for the irrep
{\cal H}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}},P}
with

\bm{\mathfrak{j}}=(\frac{1}{2},0)
and
\mathfrak{m}=-1
, which is the same
as
\bm{\mathfrak{j}}=(0,\frac{1}{2})
and
\mathfrak{m}=+1
.
Finally
{\cal H}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}},P}

with
\mathfrak{j}_{1}=\mathfrak{j}_{2}=\frac{1}{2}
and
\mathfrak{m}=0

will be referred to as
{\cal H}_{2,0}
.
Speaking in general, the
W
-primary states are the ones which are annihilated by

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mbox{d}u\,{\cal O}(u)\,\mbox{e}^{{\rm i}nu}
for any local field
{\cal O}\in{W}^{(c,r)}_{\bm{k}}
and any
positive integer
n=1,2,\ldots\
.
They can be chosen to be eigenstates of the mutually commuting operators

{\bf I}^{(a)}_{1}\ (a=1,\ldots,r)
.
As follows from eq. (
7.2
) the
representations
{\cal H}_{0,0}
,
{\cal H}_{1,-1}
and
{\cal H}_{1,+1}

all contain only one linearly independent state, which we denote by

{\bm{e}}_{0,0}
,
{\bm{e}}_{1,-1}
and
{\bm{e}}_{1,+1}
,
respectively.
As for
{\cal H}_{2,0}
, there are two
primary states
{\bm{e}}_{2,0}^{(\pm)}
.
A summary of the notation, together with the corresponding eigenvalues of the local IM
{\bf I}_{1}^{(1)}

and

)
are provided in tab.

The algebra
W^{(c,2)}_{\bm{1}}
was described in terms
of bosonic and fermionic fields.
In turn, the irreps

{\cal H}_{0,0}
,
{\cal H}_{1,\pm 1}
and
{\cal H}_{2,0}
can be identified
with certain subspaces of the bosonic and fermionic modules.
Taking into account that the eigenvalues of
\hat{U}^{2}
coincide with

\mbox{e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{K}{\mathfrak{m}}}
,
the quasiperiodicity condition (
) for the vertices
(
9.5
) implies that for the Majorana fermion fields

\chi_{a}(u+2\pi)=(-1)^{\mathfrak{m}+1}\,\chi_{a}(u)\,.

(9.43)

As a result, the spaces
{\cal H}_{0,0}
and

{\cal H}_{2,0}
would correspond to the anti-periodic boundary conditions,
while for
{\cal H}_{1,\pm 1}
the Majorana fermions would be periodic fields.

Neveu-Schwarz sector

In this sector the fermion fields are antiperiodic,
so that their Fourier mode expansions are given by

\displaystyle\chi_{a}=\sum_{\nu\in\frac{1}{2}+{\mathbb{Z}}}f^{(a)}_{\nu}\,\mbox{e}^{-{\rm i}\nu u}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \qquad\big\{f^{(a)}_{\nu},f_{\mu}^{(b)}\big\}=\delta_{ab}\ \delta_{\nu+\mu,0}\ .

(9.44)

The NS fermionic module (Fock space)
{\cal F}_{\rm NS}
is generated by the
action of the “creation” operators
f_{-\nu}^{(a)}

on the NS vacuum, which itself is defined by the condition

f_{\nu}^{(a)}\,|0\rangle_{\rm NS}=0
(here
\nu=\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{3}{2},\ldots
and
a=1,2
).
It is splitted onto the eigenspaces
{\cal F}_{\rm NS}^{(+)}
and

{\cal F}_{\rm NS}^{(-)}
of the fermion number operator

\displaystyle(-1)^{\tt F}=\exp\Big({\rm i}\pi\sum_{\nu>0}f^{{{(1)}}}_{{-\nu}}\,f_{{\vphantom{-}\nu}}^{{{(1)}}}+f^{{{(2)}}}_{{-\nu}}\,f_{{\vphantom{-}\nu}}^{{{(2)}}}\Big)\ .

(9.45)

Since all the fields of the
W
-algebra
W^{(c,2)}_{\bm{1}}

commute with
(-1)^{\tt F}

and the bosonic zero mode momentum
\hat{a}_{0}
,
the following identifications can be made

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{0,0}={\cal F}_{\rm NS}^{(+)}\otimes{\cal F}_{P}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\cal H}_{2,0}={\cal F}_{\rm NS}^{(-)}\otimes{\cal F}_{P}\ .

(9.46)

The primary state

{\bm{e}}_{0,0}
coincides with
|0\rangle_{\rm NS}\otimes|P\rangle
, while

{\bm{e}}^{(\pm)}_{2,0}
are certain linear combinations of

f^{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{-\frac{1}{2}}}}\,|0\rangle_{\rm NS}\otimes|P\rangle
and

f^{\scriptscriptstyle{(2)}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{-\frac{1}{2}}}}\,|0\rangle_{\rm NS}\otimes|P\rangle

that make them eigenstates of the local IM
{\bf I}^{(o)}_{1}
(
9.18
).

In terms of the oscillator modes
{\bf I}^{(e)}_{1}
(
9.18
) reads as

\displaystyle{\bf I}^{(e)}_{1}\,=\,-\tfrac{1}{12}+a_{0}^{2}+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}a_{-m}a_{m}+\sum_{\nu=\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2},+\ldots}^{\infty}\nu\,\big(\,f^{(1)}_{-\nu}f^{(1)}_{\nu}+f^{(2)}_{-\nu}f^{(2)}_{\nu}\,\big)\,,

(9.47)

while

\displaystyle{\bf I}^{(o)}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{24}\,\tau+\sum_{\nu=\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2},\ldots}\nu\,\big(\,2\tau\,f^{(2)}_{-\nu}f^{(2)}_{\nu}-{\rm i}\rho\,(f^{(1)}_{-\nu}f^{(2)}_{\nu}+f^{(2)}_{-\nu}f^{(1)}_{\nu})\big)

(9.48)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\,a_{0}b_{0}-\sqrt{2}\,\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\big(a_{-n}b_{n}+b_{-n}a_{n}\,\big)\,.

Here we use the notation

\displaystyle b_{n}=-\tfrac{{\rm i}}{\sqrt{2}}\sum_{\nu+\mu=n\atop\nu,\mu\in{\mathbb{Z}}+\frac{1}{2}}f^{(1)}_{\mu}f^{(2)}_{\nu}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ (n=\pm 1,\pm 2,\ldots)

\displaystyle b_{0}=-\tfrac{{\rm i}}{\sqrt{2}}\ \sum_{\nu=\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2},\ldots}\big(f^{(1)}_{-\nu}f^{(2)}_{\nu}-f^{(2)}_{-\nu}f^{(1)}_{\nu}\big)\ .

(9.49)

The latter coincide with the Fourier coefficients of the current

\displaystyle\partial\theta=-\tfrac{{\rm i}}{\sqrt{2}}\ \chi_{1}\chi_{2}=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}b_{n}\ \mbox{e}^{-{\rm i}nu}\ :\ \ \ \ \ \ [b_{m},b_{n}]=\tfrac{m}{2}\ \delta_{m+n,0}\ ,

(9.50)

where the bosonic field
\theta
was introduced in (
9.2
).
Then a simple calculation yields

\displaystyle{\bm{e}}_{0,0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|0\rangle_{\rm NS}\otimes|P\rangle\ :\ \ \ I_{1}^{(e)}=P^{2}-\tfrac{1}{12}\ ,\ \ \ I_{1}^{(o)}=-\tfrac{1}{24}\,\tau\,,

(9.51)

\displaystyle{\bm{e}}^{(\pm)}_{2,0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\alpha^{(\pm)}_{1}\,f^{{(1)}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{-\frac{1}{2}}}}+\alpha_{2}^{(\pm)}\,f^{{(2)}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{-\frac{1}{2}}}}\,\big)\,|0\rangle_{\rm NS}\otimes|P\rangle\ :\ \ \ \ I_{1}^{(e)}=P^{2}+\tfrac{5}{12}\ ,\ \ \ \ I_{1}^{(o)}=\tfrac{11}{24}\,\tau\mp\tfrac{1}{2}\,g

with

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}^{(\pm)}=\tau\pm g\,,\ \ \ \ \qquad\alpha_{2}^{(\pm)}={\rm i}(\rho+2P)\ ,\ \ \ \qquad g=\sqrt{4P^{2}-\rho^{2}+\tau^{2}}\ .

(9.52)

Ramond sector

In the Ramond sector the Majorana fermions are periodic fields,
so that

\displaystyle\chi_{a}=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}f^{(a)}_{n}\,\mbox{e}^{-{\rm i}nu}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \big\{f^{(a)}_{n},f^{(b)}_{m}\big\}=\delta_{ab}\ \delta_{n+m,0}\ .

(9.53)

The Ramond vacuum states
|\pm\rangle_{\rm R}
are annihilated by
f^{(a)}_{n}
with
n>0
and form a two
dimensional representation for
the algebra of zero modes

\displaystyle\big(f^{(1)}_{0}\big)^{2}=\big(f^{(2)}_{0}\big)^{2}=\tfrac{1}{2}\ ,\ \ \qquad\ \big\{f^{(1)}_{0},f^{(2)}_{0}\big\}=0\ .

(9.54)

Let
\sigma^{A}

(A=x,y,z)
be the Pauli matrices, such that
\sigma^{z}\,|\pm\rangle_{\rm R}=\pm\,|\pm\rangle_{\rm R}
.
One can set

\displaystyle f_{0}^{(1)}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\ \sigma^{y}\ (-1)^{\tt F}\ ,\ \ \ \ f_{0}^{(2)}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\ \sigma^{x}\ (-1)^{\tt F}\ ,\ \ \ \ -\tfrac{{\rm i}}{\sqrt{2}}\ f_{0}^{(1)}f_{0}^{(2)}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{8}}\ \sigma^{z}

(9.55)

with

\displaystyle(-1)^{\tt F}=\sigma^{z}\,\exp\Big({\rm i}\pi\sum_{n>0}f^{{{(1)}}}_{{-n}}\,f_{{\vphantom{-}n}}^{{{(1)}}}+f^{{{(2)}}}_{{-n}}\,f_{{\vphantom{-}n}}^{{{(2)}}}\Big)\,.

(9.56)

The Ramond fermionic module
{\cal F}_{\rm R}
, generated by the
action of the creation operators
f_{-n}^{(a)}

(n>0)
on the vacua,
can be splitted into
{\cal F}_{\rm R}^{(+)}
and

{\cal F}_{\rm R}^{(-)}
according to the value of
(-1)^{\tt F}
.
The fields from
W^{(c,2)}_{\bm{1}}
commute with

(-1)^{\tt F}
, which allows one to identify

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{1,\pm 1}={\cal F}_{\rm R}^{(\pm)}\otimes{\cal F}_{P}\ .

(9.57)

In turn, for the primary states

\displaystyle{\bm{e}}_{1,\pm 1}=|\pm\rangle_{\rm R}\otimes|P\,\rangle\,.

(9.58)

The local IM (
9.18
)
in the Ramond sector, written in terms of the creation/annihilation operators, read as

\displaystyle{\bf I}^{(e)}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{12}+a_{0}^{2}+2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a_{-n}a_{n}+\tfrac{1}{8}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n\,\big(\,f^{(1)}_{-n}f^{(1)}_{\vphantom{-}n}+f^{(2)}_{-n}f^{(2)}_{\vphantom{-}n}\,\big)

(9.59)

and

\displaystyle{\bf I}^{(o)}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{12}\,\tau+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n\,\big(\,2\tau\,f^{(2)}_{-n}f^{(2)}_{\vphantom{-}n}-{\rm i}\rho\,(f^{(1)}_{-n}f^{(2)}_{\vphantom{-}n}+f^{(2)}_{-n}f^{(1)}_{\vphantom{-}n})\big)

(9.60)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\,a_{0}b_{0}-\sqrt{2}\,\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\big(a_{-n}b_{n}+b_{-n}a_{n}\big)\,.

Now

\displaystyle b_{n}=-\tfrac{{\rm i}}{\sqrt{2}}\sum_{m+s=n\atop m,l\in{\mathbb{Z}}}f^{(1)}_{m}f^{(2)}_{s}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ (n=\pm 1,\pm 2,\ldots)

(9.61)

\displaystyle b_{0}=-\tfrac{{\rm i}}{\sqrt{2}}\ \Big(\,f^{(1)}_{0}f^{(2)}_{0}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\big(\,f^{(1)}_{-m}f^{(2)}_{\vphantom{-}m}-f^{(2)}_{-m}f^{(1)}_{\vphantom{-}m}\,\big)\,\Big)\ .

With these expressions it is easy to see that

\displaystyle{\bm{e}}_{1,\pm 1}=|\pm\rangle_{\rm R}\otimes|P\rangle\ :\ \ \ \qquad I_{1}^{(e)}=P^{2}+\tfrac{1}{24}\ ,\ \ \ \qquad I_{1}^{(o)}=\tfrac{1}{12}\,\tau\mp\tfrac{1}{2}P\ .

(9.62)

The relations (
9.1
) between the local IM

{\bf I}_{1}^{(e)}
,
{\bf I}_{1}^{(o)}
and
{\bf I}_{1}^{(1)},\,{\bf I}_{1}^{(2)}

come from a comparison of
the eigenvalues quoted in the above formula and eq.(
9.51
)
with the last two columns of tab.
1
.
One needs to also take into account that

\tau=-\rho\,\frac{z_{1}+z_{2}}{z_{1}-z_{2}}
and

\rho=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\xi(1+\xi)}}
.

The identifications
