\displaystyle{\rm{ch}}_{A,B}({\tt q})={\rm{Tr}}_{{\cal H}_{A,B}}\big({\tt q}^{{\bf I}_{1}^{(e)}}\big)\ .

(9.72)

Namely

\displaystyle{\rm{ch}}_{0,0}({\tt q})\pm{\rm{ch}}_{2,0}({\tt q})={\tt q}^{P^{2}-\frac{1}{12}}\ \frac{\prod_{\nu=\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2},\ldots}^{\infty}\big(1\pm{\tt q}^{\nu})^{2}}{\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-{\tt q}^{n})}\ ,

(9.73)

while

\displaystyle{\rm{ch}}_{1,\pm 1}({\tt q})={\tt q}^{P^{2}+\frac{1}{24}}\,\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(1+{\tt q}^{n})^{2}}{1-{\tt q}^{n}}\ .

(9.74)

The characters are the generating functions for the dimensions of the level subspaces.
In view of the ODE/IQFT correspondence discussed in sec.
7
,
these formulae provide highly nontrivial predictions
concerning the number of solutions of the algebraic system
(
7.47
)-(
7.51
)
on the apparent singularities of the ODE for
r=2
and
k_{1}=k_{2}=1
.

9.3
Dual nonlocal IM

In sec.
8.3
, we
briefly mentioned a certain “duality transformation”
and traced its appearance at the level of the ODE.
On the quantum field theory side, the simplest instance where it
was originally observed was in the work of Schmid
Schmid1
on the instanton calculus
in dissipative quantum mechanics. Around ten years later, the duality was
used in the computation of the conductance in a fractional quantum Hall system
Fendley

and also appeared in the study of the quantum KdV theory
Bazhanov:1996dr
. The latter
is the special case of the generalized affine Gaudin model with
r=k=1
.
For
r=2
and
k_{1}=k_{2}=1
the duality is manifest in essentially the same way,
as will be discussed below.
11
11

11

Some hints of the appearance of this duality were observed in the study
of the
{\cal Z}_{2}
invariant inhomogeneous six-vertex model in ref.
IJS2
.
The latter corresponds to the case
z_{1}=-z_{2}={\rm i}
.
The relation between the GAGM and the lattice system
is discussed in sec.
12
of this work.

An examination of the explicit formulae for the local IM (
9.18
) and (
9.1
)
shows that they remain unchanged under the transformation

\displaystyle\varphi\mapsto-\varphi\ ,\ \ \ \ \chi_{1}\mapsto-\chi_{1}\ ,\ \ \ \chi_{2}\mapsto\chi_{2}

(9.75)

so long as one simultaneously swaps the parameters

\displaystyle\beta\mapsto\beta^{-1}\ ,\ \ \ \ z_{a}\mapsto z_{a}^{-1}\ .

(9.76)

The defining property of the corresponding local densities
T_{2n}^{(a)}
is that they satisfy the OPEs
of the form

\displaystyle T_{2n}^{(a)}(u)\,V_{\pm}(v)=\sum_{m=2}^{2n+1}\frac{{R}^{(\pm)}_{-m}(v)}{(u-v)^{m}}+\frac{\partial{\cal O}_{\pm}(v)}{u-v}+O(1)

(9.77)

with the vertices from eq. (
9.5
). The invariance of the local IM
under the “duality” transformation immediately implies that
the similar OPEs hold true with
V_{\pm}
replaced by the “dual” vertices

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}_{+}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sqrt{2}\ \chi_{1}\,\mbox{e}^{-\frac{{\rm i}\sqrt{2}}{\beta}\varphi}

(9.78)

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}_{-}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\ \big(\,(z^{-1}_{1}+z^{-1}_{2})\,\chi_{1}+{\rm i}\,\,(z^{-1}_{1}-z^{-1}_{2})\,\chi_{2}\,\big)\ \mbox{e}^{+\frac{{\rm i}\sqrt{2}}{\beta}\varphi}\ ,

which are obtained from the original ones via the substitutions (
9.75
), (
9.76
).

Introduce the operators

\widetilde{x}_{0}=\frac{1}{\widetilde{q}-\widetilde{q}^{-1}}\,\int_{0}^{2\pi}{\rm d}u\,\widetilde{V}_{+}(u)\ ,\qquad\widetilde{x}_{1}=\frac{1}{\widetilde{q}-\widetilde{q}^{-1}}\,\int_{0}^{2\pi}{\rm d}u\,\widetilde{V}_{-}(u)\ .

(9.79)

These would satisfy the Serre relations (
2.9
), but with
q
replaced by

\displaystyle\widetilde{q}=-\mbox{e}^{-\frac{{\rm i}\pi}{2}(\beta^{-2}-1)}\,.

(9.80)

A realization of the Borel subalgebra
U_{\widetilde{q}}(\widehat{{\mathfrak{b}}}_{-})
is
provided by
\widetilde{x}_{0}
,
\widetilde{x}_{1}
, as well as
\widetilde{h}_{0}
,
which is defined such that

\displaystyle\tilde{q}^{\tilde{h}_{0}}=\mbox{e}^{-{\rm i}\pi\sqrt{2}\,\beta^{-1}\hat{a}_{0}}\ \hat{U}\equiv\widetilde{\Omega}^{\frac{1}{2}}

(9.81)

with
\hat{U}
from eq.(
)
(
{U}^{2}=+1
and
{U}^{2}=-1
in the Neveu-Schwarz and Ramond sector,
respectively). Consequently, one can construct the dual operator

\widetilde{{{{{\bm{a}}}}}}_{\pm}(\widetilde{\lambda})

from the vertices
\widetilde{V}_{\pm}
and
\widetilde{\Omega}^{\frac{1}{2}}

via the formulae, which are only notationally different to (
3.26
) and (
3.27
).
Notice that the transformation (
9.75
) flips the sign of
\varphi
and,
therefore, its zero mode momentum
\hat{a}_{0}
. As a result,
the operators
\widetilde{{{{{\bm{a}}}}}}_{\pm}

would be given by the trace over a representation
\widetilde{\rho}_{\pm}

of the
\widetilde{q}
- oscillator algebra,

[\widetilde{{\mathcal{H}}},\widetilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\pm}]=\pm 2\,\widetilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{\pm}\,,\qquad\qquad\widetilde{q}\,\widetilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{+}\widetilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{-}-\widetilde{q}^{\,-1}\,\widetilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{-}\widetilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{+}=\frac{1}{\widetilde{q}-{\widetilde{q}}^{\,-1}}\ ,

(9.82)

subject to the requirement

{{\rm Tr}}_{\widetilde{\rho}_{\pm}}\big[\mbox{e}^{\mp 2{\rm i}\pi\beta^{-1}P\widetilde{\cal H}}\big]\neq 0,\,\infty\qquad{\rm with}\qquad{\Im}m(P)<0\,.

(9.83)

A comparison with the similar condition (
3.25
) for the representation
{\rho}_{\pm}
,
appearing in the construction of
{{{{\bm{a}}}}}_{\pm}
,
suggests that under the duality transformation

{\rho}_{\pm}\mapsto\widetilde{\rho}_{\mp}
.

The operator
\widetilde{{{{{\bm{a}}}}}}_{\pm}(\widetilde{\lambda})
possesses
the formal power series expansion in the “dual” spectral parameter:

\log\widetilde{{{{{\bm{a}}}}}}_{\pm}(\widetilde{\lambda})=-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\widetilde{{\bf H}}_{n}^{(\pm)}\ \big(\,\widetilde{\lambda}^{2}\big)^{n}\ .

(9.84)

Based on the fact that the residue in the OPE

T_{2n}^{(a)}(u)\,\widetilde{V}_{\pm}(v)
is a total derivative,
it is possible to argue, following the lines of
Bazhanov:1998dq
, that
the operators
\widetilde{{\bf H}}_{n}^{(\pm)}
commute with the local IM.
They are referred to as the dual nonlocal IM.

Similar as in the case of the quantum KdV,
one expects the eigenvalues of the dual nonlocal IM
to appear in the large
\mu
asymptotics
of the connection coefficients.
For instance, the
formal series
X(\mu)
, which enters into the asymptotic formula (
8.11
)
for
D_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},+\mathfrak{m},+A}(\mu)
, coincides with
the eigenvalue of
\widetilde{{{{{{\bm{a}}}}}}}_{-}(\widetilde{\lambda})
,
provided that the expansion parameters
\mu
and
\widetilde{\lambda}
are properly related.
This way, formula (
8.11
) may be promoted to the operator relation:

{{{{{\bm{a}}}}}}_{+}({\lambda})\,\asymp\,{\bf R}\,\mu^{-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\beta}\,\hat{a}_{0}}\ \exp\Big(\mu^{\frac{1}{1-\beta^{2}}}\ q_{-1}\Big)\ {\bf B}(\mu)\,\widetilde{{{{{\bm{a}}}}}}_{-}(\widetilde{\lambda})\qquad\qquad\qquad(\mu\to+\infty)\,.

(9.85)

Here
{\bf R}
is the reflection operator (
9.15
), whose eigenvalues coincide with

R_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},A}
(
8.4
),
while
{\bf B}(\mu)
, with eigenvalue

B(\mu)
(
8.12
), involves only the local integrals of motion.
Recall that

\mu\propto\lambda^{2}
according to eq.(
7.17
) which, specialized
to the case at hand, reads as

\displaystyle\mu=-\lambda^{2}\ \Gamma^{2}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\,(1-\beta^{2})\big)\,\big(\tfrac{2}{1-\beta^{2}}\big)^{1-\beta^{2}}\ .

(9.86)

The relation between
\widetilde{\lambda}
and
\lambda
can be
established in the following way. Introduce
\widetilde{\mu}
through the formula

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}=-\widetilde{\lambda}^{2}\ \Gamma^{2}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\,(1-\beta^{-2})\big)\,\big(\tfrac{2}{1-\beta^{-2}}\big)^{1-\beta^{-2}}\ ,

(9.87)

which is just the “dual” version of (
9.86
).
On the other hand, the analysis of the ODE performed in sec.
8.3

suggests that
\widetilde{\mu}=\mu^{-\beta^{-2}}
(see (
8.33
)).
Combining this with the above equations one finds

\displaystyle\widetilde{\lambda}^{2}=\big(\beta^{2}\big)^{\beta^{-2}-1}\,\Big(\Gamma\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\,(1-\beta^{-2})\big)\Big)^{-2}\,\Big({\lambda}^{2}\,\Gamma^{2}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\,(1-\beta^{2})\big)\Big)^{-\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}}\ .

(9.88)

It should be pointed out
that the relation
\widetilde{\mu}=\mu^{-\beta^{-2}}
assumes
the convention (
8.1
) so that (
9.88
) is applicable for
z_{1}z_{2}=1
.
Otherwise it would require a simple modification.

If the eigenvalues of the coefficients in the Taylor series expansion

\log{{{{{\bm{a}}}}}}_{\pm}({\lambda})=-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,{\bf H}_{n}^{(\pm)}\ \big({\lambda}^{2}\big)^{n}

(9.89)

are known in analytical form, then the duality relation allows one to
determine the eigenvalues of the dual non-local IM
\widetilde{{\bf H}}_{n}^{(\mp)}
.
For instance, it follows from formula (
7.5
) that
the eigenvalue of
{\bf H}_{1}^{(+)}
for the singlet primary states

{\bm{e}}_{0,0}
,
{\bm{e}}_{1,-1}
and
{\bm{e}}_{1,+1}

are given by

H_{1}^{(+)}({\bm{e}}_{0,0})=H^{(1)}\big(\sqrt{2}\,\beta P,\beta^{2}\,|\,Z\big)\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ H_{1}^{(+)}({\bm{e}}_{1,\pm 1})=H^{(2)}\big(\sqrt{2}\,\beta P,\beta^{2}\,|\,Z^{\mp 1}\big)\ ,

(9.90)

where
Z=z_{1}=z_{2}^{-1}
and

\displaystyle H^{(1)}(p,g\,|\,Z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\,\frac{\pi\Gamma\big(-g\big)}{\cos(\frac{\pi g}{2})}\ \frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{g}{2}+p\big)}{\Gamma\big(\frac{1}{2}-\tfrac{g}{2}+p\big)}\ \big(Z+Z^{-1}\big)

(9.91)

\displaystyle H^{(2)}(p,g\,|\,Z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\,\frac{\pi\Gamma\big(-g\big)}{\cos(\frac{\pi g}{2})}\ \frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{g}{2}+p\big)}{\Gamma\big(1-\frac{g}{2}+p\big)}\ \Big(\,p\,\big(Z+Z^{-1}\big)+\tfrac{g}{2}\ \big(Z-Z^{-1}\big)\,\Big)\ .

In turn, the eigenvalues of the corresponding dual non-local IM read as

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{1}^{(-)}({\bm{e}}_{0,0})=H^{(1)}\big(\sqrt{2}\,\beta^{-1}P,\beta^{-2}\,|\,Z\big)\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \widetilde{H}_{1}^{(-)}({\bm{e}}_{1,\pm 1})=H^{(2)}\big(\sqrt{2}\,\beta^{-1}P,\beta^{-2}\,|\,Z^{\pm 1}\big)\ .

10

W
-algebra and local IM
{\bf I}_{1}^{(a)}
for
k_{a}=1

For further illustration, we describe the lowest spin local fields for the

W_{\bm{k}}^{(c,r)}
algebra with
\bm{k}=(1,1,\ldots,1)

and arbitrary
r
. In turn, explicit formulae are derived for the densities

T_{2}^{(a)}
corresponding to the local IM
{\bf I}_{1}^{(a)}
.

10.1
W
-currents of Lorentz spin 2 and 3

When all
k_{a}=1
, the vertex
V_{+}
defined by eqs. (
) and
(
6.1
) takes the form

\displaystyle V_{+}=\sum_{a=1}^{r}\mbox{e}^{2{\rm i}\phi_{a}}\ \mbox{e}^{\frac{2{\rm i}(\beta-1)}{\sqrt{r}}\varphi}\ .

(10.1)

Here
\{\phi_{a}\}_{a=1}^{r}
is a set of independent chiral Bose fields subject to the OPE

\displaystyle\phi_{a}(u)\phi_{b}(v)=-\tfrac{1}{2}\ \delta_{ab}\ \log(u-v)+O(1)\,,

(10.2)

while

\displaystyle\varphi=\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}\,\sum_{a=1}^{r}\phi_{a}\ .

(10.3)

In what follows
{\cal O}_{ab}
will stand for the exponential operators

\displaystyle{\cal O}_{ab}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\mbox{e}^{2{\rm i}(\phi_{a}-\phi_{b})}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ (a\not=b)\,,

(10.4)

which are local fields of Lorentz spin
2
.

Consider the
r
fields

\displaystyle X_{b}=\beta^{-1}\,R_{b}-(\beta^{-1}-\beta)\,\sum_{a=1\atop a\not=b}^{r}{\cal O}_{ab}\ \ \ \ \qquad\ \ \ \ (b=1,\ldots,r)

(10.5)

with

\displaystyle R_{b}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle r\,(\partial\phi_{b})^{2}-2\sqrt{r}\,(1-\beta)\ \partial\varphi\,\partial\phi_{b}+(1-\beta)^{2}\ (\partial\varphi)^{2}

(10.6)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle{\rm i}\,(1-\beta^{2})\,\partial^{2}\phi_{b}-\frac{{\rm i}}{\sqrt{r}}\ (1-\beta)^{2}(1+\beta)\,\partial^{2}\varphi\,,

as well as the
\frac{1}{2}\,(r-1)\,r
fields

\displaystyle Y_{ab}=Y_{ba}={\cal O}_{ab}+{\cal O}_{ba}-\big(\partial\phi_{a}-\partial\phi_{b}\big)^{2}\ \ \ \ \ \ (a\not=b)\ .

(10.7)

One can check that
they commute with the screening charge
\oint\mbox{d}u\,V_{+}(u)
.
In other words,
X_{a}
,
Y_{ab}
belong
to the space
{\cal W}^{(2)}
.
We argue that any spin two
W
-current can be expressed as a linear combination
of the fields
X_{a}
and
Y_{ab}
. This way they
form a basis in
{\cal W}^{(2)}
.

The last statement
is supported by the observation that the
OPE of
X_{a}
and
Y_{ab}
generates no spin 2
fields, which are linearly independent from the basic ones.
For example, a straightforward calculation yields the following result

X_{a}(u)X_{b}(v)=\frac{C_{ab}}{(u-v)^{4}}+\frac{2X_{ab}(v)}{(u-v)^{2}}+\frac{1}{u-v}\,\Big(\,\partial X_{ab}(v)+\big(\beta^{-1}-\beta\big)\,Z_{ab}(v)\,\Big)+O(1)

(10.8)

where

\displaystyle X_{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{r}{\beta}\ X_{a}\ \delta_{ab}+(\beta^{-1}-\beta)\ \tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(X_{a}+X_{b}\big)

(10.9)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle(\beta^{-1}-\beta)\big(\beta^{-1}r+2\beta^{-1}-2\beta\big)\ \tfrac{1}{2}\ Y_{ab}\ (1-\delta_{ab})

and
C_{ab}
are some constants whose explicit form is unessential.
This shows that the spin 2 fields generated in the OPE
X_{a}(u)X_{b}(v)

are linearly expressed in terms of the basic ones. The less singular term in (
10.8
) involves
the spin 3 local fields

\displaystyle Z_{ab}\ :\ \ \ \ \ \ \ Z_{ab}=-Z_{ba}\ \qquad\qquad(a\neq b)\,.

(10.10)

They also commute with
\oint\mbox{d}u\,V_{+}(u)
.
The explicit formula for
Z_{ab}
reads as

\displaystyle Z_{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\beta^{-1}-\beta)\,\Big[\,\tfrac{1}{2}\ \partial\,\Big(\displaystyle{\sum_{c=1\atop c\not=a}^{r}}{\cal O}_{ca}-\sum_{c=1\atop c\not=b}^{r}{\cal O}_{cb}\,\Big)+2{\rm i}\,\big(\partial\phi_{a}-\partial\phi_{b}\big)\,\Big(\displaystyle{\sum_{c=1\atop c\not=a}^{r}}{\cal O}_{ca}+\sum_{c=1\atop c\not=b}^{r}{\cal O}_{cb}\Big)\,\Big]

(10.11)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\,2{\rm i}r\beta^{-1}\ \big(\partial\phi_{a}{\cal O}_{ab}-\partial\phi_{b}{\cal O}_{ba}\big)-2{\rm i}\sqrt{r}\beta^{-1}\ (1-\beta)\ \partial\varphi\,\big({\cal O}_{ab}-{\cal O}_{ba}\big)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\,\tfrac{1}{2}\big(\beta^{-1}r+2\beta^{-1}-2\beta\big)\partial({\cal O}_{ab}-{\cal O}_{ba})+(\beta^{-1}-\beta)\big(\,\tfrac{4{\rm i}}{3}\,(\partial\phi_{a}-\partial\phi_{b})^{3}+\tfrac{{\rm i}}{6}\,(\partial^{3}\phi_{a}-\partial^{3}\phi_{b})\,\big)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle r\beta^{-1}\big(\partial^{2}\phi_{a}\partial\phi_{b}-\partial^{2}\phi_{b}\partial\phi_{a}\big)+\sqrt{r}\beta^{-1}(1-\beta)\big(\partial^{2}\varphi\ (\partial\phi_{a}-\partial\phi_{b})-(\partial^{2}\phi_{a}-\partial^{2}\phi_{b})\,\partial\varphi\big)\,.

The singular part of the OPE of
X_{a}
and
Y_{bc}
is given by

\displaystyle X_{a}(u)Y_{bc}(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\delta_{ab}+\delta_{ac}}{2(u-v)^{4}}\ \big(\beta^{-1}r+2\beta^{-1}-2\beta\big)+\frac{2X_{a|bc}(v)}{(u-v)^{2}}

(10.12)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{u-v}\,\Big(\,\partial X_{a|bc}(v)+\big(\delta_{ac}-\delta_{ab}\big)\ Z_{bc}(v)\,\Big)+O(1)\ \ \ \ \ \ (n\not=j)

with

\displaystyle 2X_{a|bc}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{ac}\,(X_{a}-X_{b})+\delta_{ab}\,(X_{a}-X_{c})

(10.13)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle(\beta^{-1}r+2\beta^{-1}-2\beta\big)\ (\delta_{ab}+\delta_{ac})\ Y_{bc}\ .

All the fields appearing in the singular parts of this OPE are linearly expressed in terms of the basic spin
2 fields
X_{a},\,Y_{ab}

as well as the spin 3 fields
Z_{ab}
.

Finally one should consider the OPE involving the
Y
-fields only.
Its singular part possesses the following structure

Y_{ab}(u)Y_{cd}(v)=\frac{Y_{ab|cd}^{(4)}}{(u-v)^{4}}+\frac{Y_{ab|cd}^{(2)}(v)}{(u-v)^{2}}+\frac{1}{u-v}\ \Big(\,\frac{1}{2}\ \partial Y_{ab|cd}^{(2)}(v)+Y_{ab|cd}^{(1)}(v)\,\Big)+O(1)

(10.14)

where
a\not=b,\ c\not=d
.
A calculation results in the explicit formulae

\displaystyle Y_{ab|cd}^{(4)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\ (\delta_{ac}+\delta_{ad}+\delta_{bc}+\delta_{bd}+6\,\delta_{ac}\delta_{bd}+6\,\delta_{ad}\delta_{bc})

(10.15)

\displaystyle Y^{(2)}_{ab|cd}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\delta_{ac}+\delta_{bd}+\delta_{bc}+\delta_{bd}+2\,\delta_{ac}\delta_{bd}+2\,\delta_{ad}\delta_{bc}\big)\ (Y_{ab}+Y_{cd})

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\big(1-\delta_{bc}\big)\,\delta_{ad}\ Y_{bc}-\big(1-\delta_{ad}\big)\,\delta_{bc}\ Y_{ad}-\big(1-\delta_{ac}\big)\,\delta_{bd}\ Y_{ac}-\big(1-\delta_{bd}\big)\,\delta_{ac}\ Y_{bd}\ ,

while

\displaystyle Y^{(1)}_{ab|cd}=\delta_{ac}\,{Z}_{bdc}+\delta_{ad}\,{Z}_{bcd}+\delta_{bc}\,{Z}_{adc}+\delta_{bd}\,{Z}_{acd}

(10.16)

with

\displaystyle{Z}_{bdc}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial{\cal O}_{cd}-\partial{\cal O}_{cb}+{\rm i}\,(2\,\partial\phi_{c}-\partial\phi_{b}-\partial\phi_{d})\,\big({\cal O}_{bd}-{\cal O}_{db}\big)

(10.17)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle{\rm i}\,\big(2\,\partial\phi_{d}-\partial\phi_{c}-\partial\phi_{b}\big)\,\big({\cal O}_{cb}+{\cal O}_{bc}\big)-{\rm i}\,\big(2\,\partial\phi_{b}-\partial\phi_{d}-\partial\phi_{c}\big)\,\big({\cal O}_{dc}+{\cal O}_{cd}\big)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(2\,\partial\phi_{c}-\partial\phi_{b}-\partial\phi_{d}\big)\big(\partial^{2}\phi_{b}-\partial^{2}\phi_{d}\big)-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(2\,\partial^{2}\phi_{c}-\partial^{2}\phi_{b}-\partial^{2}\phi_{d}\big)\big(\partial\phi_{b}-\partial\phi_{d}\big)\,.

Notice that

\displaystyle{Z}_{bdc}=-{Z}_{dbc}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ (b\not=d,\ \ d\not=c,\ c\not=b)

(10.18)

so that the number of independent components of
{Z}_{bdc}
is
equal to
\frac{1}{2}\,r(r-1)(r-2)
.

The above analysis suggests that
X_{a}
,
Y_{ab}
form
a basis in
{\cal W}^{(2)}
, while
any spin 3 field from
{\cal W}^{(3)}

may be represented as a linear combination of
Z_{ab}
,
{Z}_{abc}
as well as the derivatives

\partial X_{a}
and
\partial Y_{ab}
.
One can continue the process of generating the higher Lorentz spin
s=4,5,\ldots
fields,
which would belong to the
linear spaces
{\cal W}^{(s)}
.
An important property of the operator algebra
W_{\bm{1}}^{(c,r)}=\oplus_{s=2}^{\infty}{\cal W}^{(s)}
is that it contains the
spin 2 field

\displaystyle T_{2}=\frac{1}{\beta r}\,\bigg(\sum_{a=1}^{r}X_{a}+(\beta^{-1}-\beta)\,\sum_{b>a}Y_{ab}\bigg)\ ,

(10.19)

which forms the Virasoro subalgebra

\displaystyle T_{2}(u)T_{2}(v)=\frac{c}{2(u-v)^{4}}-\frac{T_{2}(u)+T_{2}(v)}{(u-v)^{2}}+O(1)

(10.20)

with central charge

\displaystyle c=r-\frac{6}{r}\ \big(\beta^{-1}-\beta\big)^{2}\ .

(10.21)

10.2
Local IM

The integrals of motion
{\bf I}_{1}^{(a)}
are built from the densities
belonging to the linear space
{\cal W}^{(2)}
. They can be expressed as
a linear combination of the basic fields with some numerical coefficients:

\displaystyle T^{(a)}_{2}=\sum_{b=1}^{r}A^{(a)}_{b}X_{b}+\sum_{b\not=c}B^{(a)}_{bc}\,Y_{bc}\ .

(10.22)

The coefficients may be fixed using the method discussed in sec.
9.1
, which is based on
the notion of the reflection operator.
Introduce
{\bf R}
, defined via its action on the

W
-currents

\displaystyle{\bf R}\,X_{a}\,{\bf R}^{-1}={\tilde{X}}_{a}\ ,\ \ \ \ \qquad{\bf R}\,Y_{ab}\,{\bf R}^{-1}={\tilde{Y}}_{ab}\ ,

(10.23)

where
{\tilde{X}}_{a}
and
{\tilde{Y}}_{ab}
are obtained from the fields
X_{a}
(
10.5
) and
Y_{ab}
(
10.7
)
by means of the formal substitution

\displaystyle\phi_{a}\,\mapsto\,-\phi_{a}-\tfrac{{\rm i}}{2}\,C_{a}\ .

(10.24)

The constants
C_{a}
are defined as the solution of the linear system,

\displaystyle\log(z_{a})=C_{a}+\frac{\beta-1}{r}\ \sum_{b=1}^{r}C_{b}\ ,

(10.25)

so that under the replacement (
10.24
),

\displaystyle V_{+}=\sum_{a=1}^{r}\mbox{e}^{2{\rm i}\phi_{a}}\ \mbox{e}^{\frac{2{\rm i}(\beta-1)}{\sqrt{r}}\varphi}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \mapsto\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ V_{-}=\sum_{a=1}^{r}z_{a}\ \mbox{e}^{-2{\rm i}\phi_{a}}\ \mbox{e}^{-\frac{2{\rm i}(\beta-1)}{\sqrt{r}}\varphi}\ .

(10.26)

Then the densities entering into the local IM are those, which are invariant under the reflection up to a total derivative:

\displaystyle{\bf R}\,T_{2}^{(a)}\,{\bf R}^{-1}=T_{2}^{(a)}+\partial{\cal O}_{1}\ .

(10.27)

The following
r
spin 2 local fields

\displaystyle T_{2}^{(a)}=-\frac{2}{\beta^{-1}-\beta}\ X_{a}+\sum_{b=1\atop b\neq a}^{r}\,\frac{2z_{b}}{z_{a}-z_{b}}\,Y_{ab}

(10.28)

obey the condition (
10.27
).
This becomes evident if
T^{(a)}_{2}
are rewritten using the differential polynomials
R_{a}
(
10.6
)
and the exponential fields

{\cal O}_{ab}=\mbox{e}^{2{\rm i}(\phi_{a}-\phi_{b})}
:

\displaystyle T^{(a)}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{1-\beta^{2}}\ \Big(\,r\,(\partial\phi_{a})^{2}-2\sqrt{r}\,(1-\beta)\ \partial\varphi\,\partial\phi_{a}+(1-\beta)^{2}\ (\partial\varphi)^{2}\,\Big)

(10.29)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\sum_{b=1\atop b\neq a}^{r}\,\frac{2z_{b}}{z_{a}-z_{b}}\,(\partial\phi_{a}-\partial\phi_{b})^{2}\,+\,\sum_{b=1\atop b\neq a}^{r}\frac{2}{z_{a}-z_{b}}\ \Big(\,z_{a}\,\mbox{e}^{2{\rm i}(\phi_{b}-\phi_{a})}+z_{b}\,\mbox{e}^{2{\rm i}(\phi_{a}-\phi_{b})}\,\Big)+\partial\big(\ldots\big)\ .

The prediction (
8.18
) from the ODE/IQFT correspondence concerns the eigenvalues
of the local IM, which a priori would be linearly expressed in terms of

\oint\frac{{\rm d}u}{2\pi}\,T_{2}^{(a)}
.
To establish the precise relation it is sufficient to consider the
eigenvalue for the simplest
W
-primary states (
7.3
).
Introduce the short cut notation

\displaystyle{\bm{e}}_{\mathfrak{m}}={\bm{e}}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},2{\mathfrak{J}},P}\ :\ \ \ \ \bm{\mathfrak{j}}=\big(\underbrace{\tfrac{1}{2},\ldots,\tfrac{1}{2}}_{\mathfrak{m}},\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{r-\mathfrak{m}}\big)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ (\mathfrak{m}=0,1,\ldots,r-1)\,.

(10.30)

As follows from (
8.18
) the corresponding eigenvalue is given by

\displaystyle I_{1}^{(a)}({\bm{e}}_{\mathfrak{m}})=\frac{1}{24}\ \sum_{b=1\atop b\neq a}^{r}\,\frac{z_{a}+z_{b}}{z_{a}-z_{b}}\,\Big(\,3\,\epsilon(\mathfrak{m}-a)\,\epsilon(\mathfrak{m}-b)-1\,\Big)

(10.31)

\displaystyle-\,\frac{\epsilon(\mathfrak{m}-a)}{\beta^{-1}-\beta}\ \Big(\sqrt{r}P+\tfrac{1}{8}\ \big(\beta+\beta^{-1}\big)\ (r-2\,\mathfrak{m})\,\Big)-\frac{2\beta}{\beta^{-1}-\beta}\Bigg[\,\frac{r}{48}+\bigg(P+\frac{r-2\,\mathfrak{m}}{4\beta\sqrt{r}}\bigg)^{2}\,\Bigg]\,,

where

\displaystyle\epsilon(n)=\begin{cases}+1\ \ \ \ &{\rm for}\ \ \ \ n\geq 0\\
-1\ \ \ \ &{\rm for}\ \ \ \ n<0\end{cases}\ .

(10.32)

On the other hand, for such primary states

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mbox{d}u\ \mbox{e}^{2{\rm i}(\phi_{a}-\phi_{b})}(u)\,{\bm{e}}_{\mathfrak{m}}=0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ (\forall a,b,\mathfrak{m})\ .

(10.33)

This makes the
calculation of the eigenvalues of
\oint\frac{{\rm d}u}{2\pi}\,T_{2}^{(a)}

with the density given by (
10.29
)
elementary and shows that they exactly coincide with
I_{1}^{(a)}({\bm{e}}_{\mathfrak{m}})

(
10.31
).

11
Hamiltonians for the GAGM

As we saw, the study
of the algebra

W_{\bm{k}}^{(c,r)}
and the subsequent
construction of the local IM is
rather involved.
Nevertheless using the
expression for the spectrum (
8.26
),
as well as the
k_{a}=1
result for the densities (
10.29
),
it is possible to deduce the form of
{\bf I}_{1}^{(a)}

for arbitrary values of the parameters. Let’s illustrate this
first in the isotropic limit.

11.1
The isotropic limit

The limit was already discussed in the
context of the ODE
in sec.(
7.4
).
As prescribed by
eq.(
7.74
)
one must first perform the substitution

\displaystyle z_{a}\mapsto z_{a}+\tfrac{\beta^{2}K}{2(1-\beta^{2})}

(11.1)

and then take
\beta\to 1^{-}
.
For the local IM
{\bf I}_{1}^{(a)}
, we define

{\bf I}_{1}^{(a,{\rm iso})}=\frac{1}{2K}\ \lim_{\beta\to 1^{-}}\big(\beta^{-2}-1\big)\,{\bf I}^{(a)}_{1}\,.

(11.2)

Consider the local densities (
10.29
).
Following the above procedure
with
K=r
in eqs.(
11.1
), (
11.2
),
one obtains

{\bf I}_{1}^{(a,{\rm iso})}=-\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\mbox{d}u}{2\pi}\,\bigg((\partial\phi_{a})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{b=1\atop b\neq a}^{r}\frac{1}{z_{a}-z_{b}}\ \Big(\,(\partial\phi_{a}-\partial\phi_{b})^{2}-\mbox{e}^{2{\rm i}(\phi_{b}-\phi_{a})}-\mbox{e}^{2{\rm i}(\phi_{a}-\phi_{b})}\Big)\bigg)\,.

(11.3)

Recall that these operators act invariantly in
{\cal H}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}},P}
(
).
Provided that

P=\frac{\mathfrak{m}}{2\sqrt{r}}\,,

(11.4)

the latter can be realized as a subspace of the tensor product of
r
integrable representations
of the Kac-Moody algebra at level one. It is easy to see that the
densities for the local integrals of motion
can be expressed in terms of the currents

J_{\pm}^{(a)}=\mbox{e}^{\pm 2{\rm i}\phi_{a}}
and
J_{0}^{(a)}=2\partial\phi_{a}
(see eq.(
3.2
))
so that

{\bf I}_{1}^{(a,{\rm iso})}=-\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\mbox{d}u}{2\pi}\,\bigg(G^{(a)}+\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{b=1\atop b\neq a}^{r}\frac{G^{(a)}+G^{(b)}-2\eta^{AB}J_{A}^{(a)}J_{B}^{(b)}}{z_{a}-z_{b}}\,\bigg)\ \ \ \ \ \ (k_{a}=1)\ ,

(11.5)

where

\displaystyle 2\eta^{AB}J_{A}^{(a)}J_{B}^{(b)}=\tfrac{1}{2}\ J_{0}^{(a)}J_{0}^{(b)}+J_{+}^{(a)}J_{-}^{(b)}+J_{-}^{(a)}J_{+}^{(b)}\ .

(11.6)

The notation
G^{(a)}=(\partial\phi_{a})^{2}
stands for the Virasoro field associated with
the
k_{a}=1
Kac-Moody algebra.

Formula (
11.5
) admits an immediate
generalization for arbitrary positive
integers
k_{a}
.
One assumes
that the local densities are expressed as a quadratic combination of the
Kac-Moody currents, subject to the OPE (
1.13
)
The local IM should be invariant w.r.t. the global
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
symmetry
that occurs in the isotropic limit.
Also, taking into account
the expression (
8.26
) for the eigenvalues of the IM,
specialized to
\xi\to+\infty
, one arrives at
the formula

\displaystyle(-k_{a})\,{\bf I}_{1}^{(a,{\rm iso})}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\mbox{d}u}{2\pi}\,\bigg(G^{(a)}+\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{b=1\atop b\neq a}^{r}\frac{k_{b}\,G^{(a)}+k_{a}\,G^{(b)}-2\eta^{AB}J_{A}^{(a)}J_{B}^{(b)}}{z_{a}-z_{b}}\,\bigg)\,,

(11.7)

where

\displaystyle G^{(a)}=\frac{\eta^{AB}J_{A}^{(a)}J_{B}^{(a)}}{k_{a}+2}\,.

(11.8)

Notice that the overall normalization of
{\bf I}_{1}^{(a,{\rm iso})}
is such that

\displaystyle\sum_{a=1}^{r}(-k_{a})\,{\bf I}_{1}^{(a,{\rm iso})}=\sum_{a=1}^{r}\,\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\mbox{d}u}{2\pi}\ G^{(a)}\ .

(11.9)

It is instructive to consider the limit
when all the
k_{a}
tend to infinity.
One writes
k_{a}=\nu_{a}\,K
and takes
K\to\infty
while keeping

\nu_{a}
fixed. The latter automatically satisfy

\sum_{a=1}^{r}\nu_{a}=1\ .

(11.10)

This can be interpreted as the classical limit, with
K
being identified with
the inverse Planck constant:

K=\hbar^{-1}\ .

(11.11)

The currents

j^{(a)}_{A}=\hbar\,J^{(a)}_{A}

(11.12)

become classical fields obeying
the equal time Poisson bracket relations

\big\{j^{(a)}_{A}(u),j^{(b)}_{B}(v)\big\}=\delta_{ab}\,\Big(\,-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\nu_{a}\,\eta_{AB}\,\delta^{\prime}(u-v)+{\rm i}\,{f_{AB}}^{C}\,j^{(a)}_{C}(u)\,\delta(u-v)\,\Big)\,.

(11.13)

In turn, for the integrals of motion (
11.7
),

\displaystyle\lim_{\hbar\to 0}\hbar^{2}\,(-{k_{a}})\,{\bf I}_{1}^{(a,{\rm iso})}=\frac{1}{2}\ \sum_{b=1\atop b\neq a}^{r}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\mbox{d}u}{2\pi}\ \eta^{AB}\ \frac{\big(\nu_{a}j^{(b)}_{A}-\nu_{b}j^{(a)}_{A}\big)\,\big(\nu_{a}j^{(b)}_{B}-\nu_{b}j^{(a)}_{B}\big)}{\nu_{a}\nu_{b}\,\big(z_{a}-z_{b}\big)}\ .

(11.14)

Notice that the term
G^{(a)}
outside the sum in (
11.7
)
is absent from the classical expression. It can be interpreted as an effect of renormalization (quantum counterterm)
which appears already at the first perturbative order.

11.2
The Gaudin limit

To take the Gaudin limit, following the discussion in sec.
7.4
,
the parameters
z_{a}
should be rescaled as
z_{a}=\delta\,y_{a}

with
\delta\to 0
. Defining the Gaudin Hamiltonians as

{\bf H}_{\rm\scriptscriptstyle G}^{(a)}=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\delta\,(-k_{a})\,{\bf I}_{1}^{(a,{\rm iso})}\,,

(11.15)

one obtains

{\bf H}_{\rm\scriptscriptstyle G}^{(a)}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\mbox{d}u}{2\pi}\ \sum_{b=1\atop b\neq a}^{r}\,\frac{k_{b}\,G^{(a)}+k_{a}\,G^{(b)}-2\eta^{AB}J_{A}^{(a)}J_{B}^{(b)}}{y_{a}-y_{b}}\ .

(11.16)

Note that the spectrum of the first few lowest integrals of motion for
the affine Gaudin model associated to

\mathfrak{sl}(N)
was studied in the work

Lacroix:2018itd
, while for the case of an arbitrary Lie algebra
some conjectures are formulated in ref.
Lacroix:2018fhf
.
Also, the classical limit of

{\bf H}_{\rm\scriptscriptstyle G}^{(a)}
results in a similar expression
as in the r.h.s. of (
11.14
) with
z_{a}
replaced by
y_{a}
.

11.3
General case

For arbitrary positive integer
k_{a}
and
\beta\in(0,1)

we propose the following formula for the
Hamiltonians of the GAGM,

{\bf{H}}^{(a)}_{\rm gen}\equiv(-\tfrac{k_{a}}{2})\,{\bf I}_{1}^{(a)}\,,

(11.17)

expressed in terms of the
set of parafermionic
\{\psi_{\pm}^{(a)}\}
and bosonic
\{\phi_{a}\}
fields:

\displaystyle{\bf{H}}^{(a)}_{\rm gen}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\mbox{d}u}{2\pi}\,\bigg[\,\frac{\beta^{2}K}{1-\beta^{2}}\,G^{(a)}+\frac{1-\beta}{1+\beta}\ \big(\,k_{a}\,(\partial\varphi)^{2}-\sqrt{Kk_{a}}\ \partial\phi_{a}\partial\varphi\,\big)

(11.18)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{b=1\atop b\neq a}^{r}\frac{1}{z_{a}-z_{b}}\ \Big(\,k_{a}z_{b}\,G^{(b)}+k_{b}z_{a}\,G^{(a)}\,\Big)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\sum_{b=1\atop b\neq a}^{r}\frac{\sqrt{k_{a}k_{b}}}{z_{a}-z_{b}}\,\Big(\,(z_{a}+z_{b})\,\partial\phi_{a}\partial\phi_{b}+z_{a}\,\psi^{(b)}_{+}\psi^{(a)}_{-}\mbox{e}^{2{\rm i}(\phi_{b}-\phi_{a})}+z_{b}\,\psi^{(a)}_{+}\psi^{(b)}_{-}\mbox{e}^{2{\rm i}(\phi_{a}-\phi_{b})}\Big)\,\bigg]\,,

where

\displaystyle G^{(a)}=(\partial\phi_{a})^{2}+W_{2}^{(a)}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \partial\varphi=\sum_{a=1}^{r}\sqrt{\frac{k_{a}}{K}}\ \partial\phi_{a}\ .

(11.19)

Recall that the spin 2 field
W^{(a)}_{2}
occurs
in the OPE of the fundamental parafermions
\psi^{(a)}_{\pm}
as in eq. (
3.9
).
Formula (
11.18
)
can be rewritten in terms of the Kac-Moody currents
J_{\pm}^{(a)}=\sqrt{k_{a}}\,\psi_{\pm}^{(a)}\,\mbox{e}^{\pm\frac{2{\rm i}\phi_{a}}{\sqrt{k}_{a}}}
,

J_{0}^{(a)}=2\sqrt{k_{a}}\,\partial\phi_{a}
. The result was already presented in the introduction,
see eq.(
1.16
).
Taking the limit
\beta\to 1^{-}
, the combination
\frac{1}{K}(\beta^{-2}-1)\,{\bf{H}}^{(a)}_{\rm gen}
coincides
with the r.h.s of (
11.7
). Another consistency check is that the eigenvalue of
the operators (
11.18
), computed on the primary states

{\bm{e}}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\pm 2{\mathfrak{J}},P}
(
7.3
), agrees with the
prediction (
8.26
) that comes from the ODE side. One can show by direct
computation that
{\bf H}_{\rm gen}^{(a)}

with
a=1,2,\ldots,r
mutually commute.

The generalized affine Gaudin model,
being a multiparametric integrable system, admits
various interesting limits. In the previous subsection the
isotropic limit was discussed as well as the classical one. Let us emphasize that
the latter was performed only after taking

\beta\to 1^{-}
. It turns out that a meaningful classical limit
exists for any fixed value of
\beta
and
z_{a}
.
Indeed, setting

k_{a}=\nu_{a}\,K

and
j^{(a)}_{A}=J^{(a)}_{A}/\hbar
in
eq. (
1.16
) and taking

K=\hbar^{-1}\to\infty
,
one arrives at

\displaystyle\lim_{\hbar\to 0}\hbar^{-2}\,{\bf H}_{\rm gen}^{(a)}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\mbox{d}u}{2\pi}\,\bigg[\,\frac{\beta^{2}}{1-\beta^{2}}\ \frac{g^{(a)}}{\nu_{a}}+\frac{1}{4}\ \frac{1-\beta}{1+\beta}\ \Big(\,\nu_{a}\,\big(j_{0}^{(\rm tot)}\big)^{2}-\,j_{0}^{(a)}j_{0}^{(\rm tot)}\,\Big)

(11.20)

\displaystyle-\sum_{b=1\atop b\neq a}^{r}\frac{1}{z_{a}-z_{b}}\,\Big(\,\frac{1}{4}\,(z_{a}+z_{b})\ j^{(a)}_{0}j^{(b)}_{0}+z_{a}\ j_{+}^{(b)}j_{-}^{(a)}+z_{b}\ j_{+}^{(a)}j_{-}^{(b)}-\frac{\nu_{a}\,z_{b}}{\nu_{b}}\ g^{(b)}-\frac{z_{a}\,\nu_{b}}{\nu_{a}}\ g^{(a)}\Big)\,\bigg]

with

g^{(a)}=\eta^{AB}j_{A}^{(a)}j_{B}^{(a)}\,,\qquad\qquad\qquad j_{0}^{({\rm tot})}=\sum_{a=1}^{r}j_{0}^{(a)}

(11.21)

and
\nu_{1}+\nu_{2}+\ldots+\nu_{r}=1
. Needless to say that these classical observables
mutually Poisson commute w.r.t. the Poisson bracket (
11.13
).

12
Baxter statistical systems in the scaling limit and
the GAGM

12.1
Baxter-type statistical systems

Behind the integrability of the generalized affine
Gaudin model are the algebraic structures, which are inherited from the
quasi-triangular Hopf algebra
U_{q}\big(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)\big)

and the universal
R
-matrix
{\cal R}
. The development of quantum groups,
that encompasses these notions,
was inspired by the works of Baxter on exactly soluble lattice
models. The
R
-matrix, encoding the Boltzmann weights of
the statistical system, comes from taking a particular realization
of
{\cal R}
. Namely, the finite dimensional matrix

\displaystyle{R}_{{\ell}_{1},{\ell}_{2}}(\lambda_{1}/\lambda_{2})=\big(\pi_{{\ell}_{1}}(\lambda_{1})\otimes\pi_{{\ell}_{2}}(\lambda_{2})\big)[{\cal R}]\,,

(12.1)

where
\pi_{\ell}
stands for the
2\ell+1
dimensional representation of
the
U_{q}\big(\mathfrak{sl}(2)\big)
algebra, satisfies
the Yang-Baxter equation. Let’s suppose that
\ell_{1}=\frac{1}{2}
.
Denoting

\displaystyle H^{(\ell)}=\pi_{\ell}({\tt h})\,,\ F^{(\ell)}=\pi_{\ell}({\tt e}_{-})\,,\ E^{(\ell)}=\pi_{\ell}({\tt e}_{+})\,\in\,{\rm End}\big(\mathbb{C}^{(2\ell+1)}\big)

(12.2)

the matrix (
12.1
)
is given explicitly by
Kulish

{R}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\frac{1}{2}},\ell}(\lambda)=r(\lambda)\ \Big(\,q^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{z}\otimes H^{(\ell)}}-\lambda^{2}\,q^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{z}\otimes H^{(\ell)}}+\lambda\,(q-q^{-1})\,(\sigma^{+}\otimes F^{(\ell)}+\sigma^{-}\otimes E^{(\ell)}\big)\,\Big)

(12.3)

with
some overall factor
r(\lambda)
, which cancels out in the Yang-Baxter equation and is not
essential for our purposes.
We set
r(\lambda)=1

and define

\displaystyle\bm{R}^{(\ell)}(q\zeta)=\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{z}\otimes 1}\ {R}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\frac{1}{2}},\ell}\big(q^{\frac{1}{2}}\lambda\big)\ \lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{z}\otimes 1}\ \ \ \ \ \ \qquad{\rm with}\ \ \ \qquad\zeta=-\lambda^{2}\ .

(12.4)

It is a
2\times 2
matrix,

\bm{R}^{(\ell)}(q\zeta)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}q^{\frac{1}{2}(1+H^{(\ell)})}+q^{\frac{1}{2}(1-H^{(\ell)})}\,\zeta&-(q-q^{-1})\,q\,\zeta\,F^{(\ell)}\\[5.69046pt]
(q-q^{-1})\,E^{(\ell)}&q^{\frac{1}{2}(1-H^{(\ell)})}+q^{\frac{1}{2}(1+H^{(\ell)})}\,\zeta\end{array}\right)\,,

whose elements act in a
2\ell+1
dimensional linear space.
The lattice transfer-matrix is constructed from such building blocks.
First introduce the monodromy matrix

{\bm{M}}\Big(\zeta\,\Big|\begin{array}[]{ll}\ell_{N},\ell_{N-1},\,\ldots,\,\ell_{1}\\
\eta_{N},\eta_{N-1},\,\ldots,\,\eta_{1}\end{array}\!\!\Big)=q^{-\frac{N}{2}}\ \bm{R}_{N}^{(\ell_{N})}\big(q\zeta/\eta_{N}\big)\,\bm{R}_{N-1}^{(\ell_{N-1})}\big(q\zeta/\eta_{N-1}\big)\cdots\bm{R}_{1}^{(\ell_{1})}\big(q\zeta/\eta_{1}\big)\,.

Its entries act in the “physical space”, which is the tensor product

\mathscr{V}_{N}=\mathbb{C}^{(2\ell_{N}+1)}_{N}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{(2\ell_{N-1}+1)}_{N-1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathbb{C}^{(2\ell_{1}+1)}_{1}\ .

(12.11)

Taking the trace over the two dimensional “auxiliary space”, one obtains the transfer-matrix

\mathbb{T}(\zeta)={\rm Tr}\big[\,\omega^{\sigma^{z}}\bm{M}(\zeta)\,\big]\,.

(12.12)

Here the parameter
\omega
can be arbitrarily chosen. It will be assumed to be a unimodular number,
so that

\omega^{2}=\mbox{e}^{2\pi{\rm i}{\tt k}}

(12.13)

with some real
{\tt k}
.
Note that the similarity transformation, which is performed in (
12.4
),
has no effect
on the trace in the definition (
12.12
). It
was done to make apparent that
\mathbb{T}(\zeta)
is a polynomial
in
\zeta=-\lambda^{2}
of order
\zeta^{N}
, normalized according to

\mathbb{T}(0)=\omega^{+1}\,q^{+\mathbb{S}^{z}}+\omega^{-1}\,q^{-\mathbb{S}^{z}}\,.

(12.14)

Also, as
\zeta\to\infty
one has

\displaystyle\lim_{\zeta\to\infty}\,\zeta^{-N}\,\mathbb{T}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\omega^{+1}\,q^{-\mathbb{S}^{z}}+\omega^{-1}\,q^{+\mathbb{S}^{z}}\big)\,\prod_{J=1}^{N}\,\eta_{J}^{-1}\ .

Here
\mathbb{S}^{z}
stands for the
z
projection of the total spin operator,

\mathbb{S}^{z}=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{m=1}^{N}H^{(\ell)}_{m}\ ,

(12.15)

which commutes with the transfer-matrix.

The Yang-Baxter equation guarantees that
\mathbb{T}(\zeta)
commutes with
itself for different values of the spectral parameter
\zeta
. The diagonalization problem can be
solved using whatever version of the Bethe ansatz approach (e.g., quantum inverse scattering method).
In the sector with given value of
\mathbb{S}^{z}
,
the Bethe ansatz equations read as
Baxter:1971cs
;
Sogo
;
Kirillov

\prod_{J=1}^{N}\frac{\eta_{J}+q^{+2\ell_{J}}\,\,\zeta_{m}}{\eta_{J}+q^{-2\ell_{J}}\,\zeta_{m}}=-\omega^{2}\,q^{2S^{z}}\,\prod_{j=1}^{M}\,\frac{\zeta_{j}-q^{+2}\,\zeta_{m}}{\zeta_{j}-q^{-2}\,\zeta_{m}}\,\qquad\qquad(\,m=1,2,\ldots,M\,)\,,

(12.16)

where
M=\sum_{J=1}^{N}\ell_{J}-S^{z}
.

12.2
Main conjecture concerning the scaling

Being a purely algebraic procedure, the
diagonalization of
\mathbb{T}(\zeta)
can be performed
for arbitrary values of the inhomogeneities
\eta_{J}
and the positive integers
2\ell_{J}
. However, to make
connection with the field theory where a fundamental symmetry is translational invariance,
we’ll focus on the lattice with

N
being divisible by
some integer
r
,

\displaystyle N=rL\,,

(12.17)

while the parameters
\eta_{J},\ell_{J}
satisfy the
r
- site periodicity conditions

\displaystyle\eta_{J+r}=\eta_{J}\,,\qquad\qquad\ell_{J+r}=\ell_{J}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \qquad\qquad\qquad(J=1,2,\ldots,N)\,.

(12.18)

The lattice model turns out to be critical when

q
is a unimodular number,
|q|=1
.
With a suitably defined scaling limit where
L\to\infty
,
the universal properties are described by a CFT. However,
different types of critical behaviour occur depending on the
domain of the parameter
q
. We conjecture that
with an appropriate definition of the scaling limit, and
a proper identification of the parameters,
the lattice transfer-matrix

\mathbb{T}(\zeta)

becomes the operator
{\bm{\tau}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\lambda)

(
2.6
) as
N\to\infty
.
The scaling limit of the Baxter
Q
-operator (whose
eigenvalues are polynomials in
\zeta
with zeroes being the
roots of the Bethe ansatz equations (
12.16
)) yields

{{{{{\bm{a}}}}}}_{+}(\lambda)
.
12
12

12

There are two Baxter
Q
-operators
{\mathbb{Q}}_{\pm}(\zeta)
. The zeroes of the eigenvalues of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{+}

satisfy (
12.16
), while those of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{-}
obey the system, which
is obtained from (
12.16
) via the substitutions

S^{z}\to-S^{z}
and
\omega\to\omega^{-1}

(for details see, e.g., ref.
Bazhanov:2020fbp
). In what follows we always assume that

S^{z}\geq 0
, and discuss the zeroes of the eigenvalues of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{+}
, rather than
\mathbb{Q}_{-}
.

The anisotropy parameter
q
should belong to the domain

\displaystyle\pi\big(1-\tfrac{1}{K}\big)<\arg(q)<\pi\ \ \ \qquad\ {\rm with}\ \qquad\ \ \ \ K=\sum_{a=1}^{r}2\ell_{a}

(12.19)

and can be written as

q=-\,\mbox{e}^{\frac{{\rm i}\pi}{K}(\beta^{2}-1)}\,,\ \ \ \ \ \qquad\ \ {\rm where}\ \ \ \qquad\ \ 0<\beta<1\ .

(12.20)

Also, the positive integers
k_{a}
from the GAGM are identified as

k_{a}=2\ell_{a}\,.

(12.21)

In taking the scaling limit, one should send
\zeta\to 0
such that the combination

\zeta\,N^{\frac{2}{K}(1-\beta^{2})}
remains fixed. Up to an overall constant, it coincides with the
spectral parameter

\lambda^{2}
entering into

{\bm{\tau}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\lambda)
and

{{{{{\bm{a}}}}}}_{\pm}(\lambda)
.
The
r
inhomogeneities
\eta_{a}
are related to the
r
parameters
z_{a}

though, in general, the relation is highly non-trivial.
13
13

13

It may also be that some restrictions on the domain of the inhomogeneities
\eta_{J}
need to
be imposed in order to make connection with the GAGM. This point requires
further investigations.

It is important to keep in mind that the scaling limit is defined not
for the full space of states
\mathscr{V}_{N}
(
12.11
), but only
a certain class of so-called low energy states.

A study of the scaling limit,
among other things, requires an analysis of the algebraic structures
underlying the lattice system.
In the case when all the
\ell_{a}=\frac{1}{2}
or, equivalently,

k_{a}=1
the matrix
\mathbb{T}(\zeta)
coincides with
the one-row transfer-matrix of the original Baxter inhomogeneous six-vertex model
Baxter:1971cs

subject to quasi-periodic boundary conditions. The recent paper
Bazhanov:2020fbp
contains
a summary of
the known results concerning the algebraic aspects of this model.
A comprehensive study of the critical behaviour of the lattice system is
beyond the scope of this work. Here
we would merely like to provide an
illustration of how the GAGM appears in the scaling limit.
As such
we’ll stick to the case
\ell_{a}=\frac{1}{2}
and rely on the results of
Bazhanov:2020fbp
.
For the reader’s convenience, we follow the notations of that work.

In the case of the homogeneous six-vertex model the transfer-matrix
commutes with the spin
\frac{1}{2}
Heisenberg
XXZ
Hamiltonian.
A similar important property holds true for the
model, where the parameters
\eta_{J}

satisfy the periodicity conditions (
12.18
) with any
r\geq 1
.
Namely, the commuting family
contains spin chain Hamiltonians
that are given by a sum of
terms, each of which is built out of local spin operators supported on

r+1
consecutive sites of the lattice.
There are
r
such Hamiltonians and, in terms of the one-row transfer-matrix, they are expressed as
(see eq. (6.11) in ref.
Bazhanov:2020fbp
)

\mathbb{H}^{(a)}=2{\rm i}\,\zeta\partial_{\zeta}\log\big(\mathbb{T}(-q^{-1}\zeta)\big)\big|_{\zeta=\eta_{a}}\ -2{\rm i}L\ \sum_{b=1}^{r}\big(1-q^{2}\,\eta_{b}/\eta_{a}\big)^{-1}\,\qquad\qquad(\,a=1,2,\ldots,r\,)\ .

(12.22)

For the definition of the scaling limit, a central rôle belongs to the sum

\mathbb{H}=\sum_{a=1}^{r}\mathbb{H}^{(a)}\,,

(12.23)

which is essentially the logarithmic derivative of a
r
row transfer-matrix.
The ground state of this Hamiltonian
serves as the reference state from which the energy of the
excited states is counted. In performing the scaling limit one takes
N\to\infty
but considers only the
class of states, whose excitation energy over the ground state energy is sufficiently low. Then as
N\to\infty

the low energy spectrum organizes into the conformal towers, which are classified w.r.t
to the algebra of extended conformal symmetry. In the case at hand, it is expected that
the latter coincides with
\overline{W}_{\bm{1}}^{(c,r)}\otimes{W}_{\bm{1}}^{(c,r)}
.
The two
W
-algebras in the tensor product are isomorphic and describe the left and right
chiralities of the underlying CFT.
In turn, for the low energy spectrum of

\mathbb{H}
(
12.23
) at large
N
, one would have

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}\,\asymp\,Ne_{\infty}{\bf 1}+\frac{2\pi r}{N}\,v_{\rm F}\,\big({\bf I}_{1}+\overline{{\bf I}}_{1}\big)+o(N^{-1})\,.

(12.24)

Here
{\bf I}_{1}
is the local IM in the generalized affine Gaudin model,
defined via
(
), (
). The operator
\overline{\bf I}_{1}
, corresponding to the
other chirality, is described by the similar equations.
The first term in the r.h.s. is proportional to the identity operator
and so does not depend on the particular low energy state.
It contains the constant
e_{\infty}
, which stands for the specific bulk energy.
The explicit
form of
e_{\infty}
is not significant in the context of the field theory.
The positive constant
v_{\rm F}
, usually referred
to as the Fermi velocity, depends on the overall normalization of the
Hamiltonian.

12.3
Example of the scaling limit with
r=2
and
\ell_{1}=\ell_{2}=\frac{1}{2}

The asymptotic formula (
12.24
) involves a contribution
from both the left and right chiralities. In order to
study the scaling limit of a low energy state,
it is useful to employ the relations which
involve each chirality separately.
Among these are the sum rules for the Bethe roots.
Let’s illustrate them on the example of the inhomogeneous
six-vertex model with anisotropy parameter
q=-\,\mbox{e}^{\frac{{\rm i}\pi}{2}(\beta^{2}-1)}
,
subject to the two site periodicity condition.
We focus on the case when
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}

are unimodular numbers and, without loss of
generality, set

\eta_{1}=\eta_{2}^{-1}=\mbox{e}^{{\rm i}\varpi}\,.

(12.25)

Also, it will be assumed that

\tfrac{\pi}{2}\,(1-\beta^{2})<\varpi<\tfrac{\pi}{2}\,(1+\beta^{2})\,.

(12.26)

The number of lattice sites
N
should be even.
Suppose for now that
N
is divisible by 4 and

S^{z}=0
.
Then the ground state of the Hamiltonian
\mathbb{H}
(
12.23
) is a singlet.
Together with
\zeta_{n}
it is convenient to use

\theta_{n}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\log(\zeta_{n})\,,

(12.27)

which are roots of the Bethe ansatz equations written in trigonometric form.
For the ground state, they are split into two groups

\{\theta^{(+)}_{n}\}_{n=1}^{N/4}
and
\{\theta^{(-)}_{n}\}_{n=1}^{N/4}

such that
\Im m(\theta_{n}^{(\pm)})\approx\pm\tfrac{1}{2}\,\varpi
(see top
left panel of fig.
2
).
We impose an ordering as

\Re e\big(\theta_{1}^{(\pm)}\big)\leq\Re e\big(\theta_{2}^{(\pm)}\big)\leq\ldots\leq\Re e\big(\theta_{N/4}^{(\pm)}\big)\,.

(12.28)

The roots
\theta^{(\pm)}_{n}
with
n\ll N
or
N/4-n\ll N
will be referred to as the left
and right edge roots, respectively, while the rest will be called the bulk roots.
The latter, as
N\to\infty
,
become densely distributed along the lines
\Im m(\theta)=\pm\tfrac{1}{2}\,\varpi
.
Namely, introducing

\rho_{N}^{(\pm)}\big(\theta_{n+\frac{1}{2}}^{(\pm)}\big)=\frac{2}{N\big(\theta_{n+1}^{(\pm)}-\theta_{n}^{(\pm)}\big)}\ \qquad\qquad{\rm with}\qquad\qquad\theta_{n+\frac{1}{2}}^{(\pm)}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(\theta_{n+1}^{(\pm)}+\theta_{n}^{(\pm)}\big)

(12.29)

one finds

\lim_{N\to\infty}\rho_{N}^{(\pm)}(\theta)=\rho_{\infty}(\theta)\equiv\frac{1}{2\pi(1-\beta^{2})\cosh(\frac{\theta}{1-\beta^{2}})}\ :\qquad\qquad\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mbox{d}\theta\,\rho_{\infty}(\theta)=\frac{1}{2}\ .

(12.30)

In contrast the edge roots develop a scaling behaviour.
It turns out that, keeping either
n
or
N/4-n
fixed as
N\to\infty
, the following limits exist

s_{n}^{(\pm)}=\lim_{N\to\infty}N^{1-\beta^{2}}\,\zeta_{N/4-n}^{(\pm)}\,,\qquad\qquad\qquad\bar{s}_{n}^{(\pm)}=\lim_{N\to\infty}N^{1-\beta^{2}}\,\big(\zeta_{n}^{(\pm)}\,\big)^{-1}\ ,

(12.31)

where
\zeta_{n}^{(\pm)}=\mbox{e}^{-2\theta_{n}^{(\pm)}}
.
Note that
s_{n}^{(\pm)}
and
\bar{s}_{n}^{(\pm)}
are complex numbers, such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\arg\big(s_{n}^{(\pm)}\big)=\pm\varpi\ ,\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\lim_{n\to\infty}\arg\big(\bar{s}_{n}^{(\pm)}\big)=\mp\varpi\ ,

(12.32)

while

\big|s_{n}^{(\pm)}\big|^{\frac{1}{1-\beta^{2}}}\asymp 2\pi n+O(1)\,,\qquad\quad\big|\bar{s}_{n}^{(\pm)}\big|^{\frac{1}{1-\beta^{2}}}\asymp 2\pi n+O(1)\qquad\quad(n\to\infty)\,.

(12.33)

A consequence of our general proposal is that,
up to an overall factor,

s_{n}^{(\pm)}
coincide with the zeroes of the spectral determinant

D_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}(\mu)
for the vacuum ODE (
7.7
) with

r=2\,,\qquad\qquad k_{1}=k_{2}=1\,,\qquad\qquad{\mathfrak{j}}_{1}=\mathfrak{j}_{2}=\mathfrak{m}=0\,,\qquad\qquad P=\frac{{\tt k}}{\sqrt{2}\beta}\,.

(12.34)

As usual,
\beta^{2}=\frac{\xi}{\xi+1}

and
{A}=\tfrac{{\rm i}}{\beta^{-1}-\beta}\ \big(\,\sqrt{K}\,P-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\beta\,\mathfrak{m}\,\big)

while, for the case under consideration,
K=k_{1}+k_{2}=2
.

N
N
N
\theta
\Im m(\theta)=+\tfrac{1}{2}\varpi
\Im m(\theta)=-\tfrac{1}{2}\varpi
h_{1}^{(N,{\rm reg})}
\bar{h}_{1}^{(N,{\rm reg})}
\tfrac{N}{4\pi v_{\rm F}}\ \big({\cal E}_{\rm NS}^{(\rm vac)}-N\,e_{\infty}\big)

Figure 2
:
Numerical data for the ground state of the Hamiltonian
\mathbb{H}

(
12.23
) for even
N/2
and
S^{z}=0
. The top left panel
depicts the pattern of Bethe roots in the complex
\theta
plane
with
\theta=-\frac{1}{2}\log(\zeta)
(
N=100
). The top right panel
concerns the ground state energy
{\cal E}_{{\rm NS}}^{({\rm vac})}
.
The black crosses were obtained through the solution of the Bethe ansatz equations for increasing
N
,
while the blue solid line represents the fit
-0.14341-0.43286/N^{2}
. The limiting value,
as predicted by eq. (
12.49
), is marked by the dashed black line. The
bottom two panels illustrate the sum rules (
12.42
) and (
12.44
). The superscript
“
\small{\rm reg}
” indicates that a subtraction and rescaling has been made,
which makes the limit
N\to\infty
well defined, e.g.,

h_{1}^{(N,{\rm reg})}=N^{-(1-\beta^{2})}\big(h_{1}^{(N)}-\frac{N}{2}\,\frac{\cos(\varpi)}{\sin(\pi\beta^{2}/2)}\,\big)
. Again, the crosses stand for the numerical values, which were found by solving
the Bethe ansatz equations, while the solid blue line is a fit,

-0.21820+0.11285/N^{2}
and
-0.12806+0.06623/N^{2}

for the bottom left and bottom right panels,
respectively. The dashed line represents the limiting value according to
eqs. (
12.42
) and (
12.44
).
For the eigenvalue of the
first non-local IM on the primary states, see (
9.90
) and (
9.91
).
The parameters were taken to be

\beta^{2}=0.43
,
{\tt k}=0.1
and
\varpi=1.3
.

The ODE involves the parameters
z_{1}
,
z_{2}
and we are free
to set
z_{1}z_{2}=1
. Provided the inhomgeneities are restricted as in
eqs. (
12.25
) and (
12.26
),
they turn out to be unimodular numbers and it is convenient to swap them for
x
,

z_{1}=z_{2}^{-1}\,:\ \ \ \ \ \qquad x=\tfrac{1}{2}\,(z_{1}+z_{2})\ ,\qquad\qquad-1<x<1\,.

(12.35)

With such reality conditions imposed on
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
, the
properties of the zeroes of the spectral determinant are described in
appendix
B
.
In particular, they are split into two groups
\mu_{n}^{(\pm)}
, such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\arg\big(\mu_{n}^{(\pm)}\big)\,=\,\pm\,\alpha_{0}(x)

(12.36)

with
\alpha_{0}(x)
from (
B.9
). Comparing the above with eq. (
12.32
)
one finds that

\alpha_{0}(x)=\varpi\,.

(12.37)

This allows one to relate the inhomogeneities of the lattice system
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
with

z_{1}
and
z_{2}
. Notice that the domains
\varpi\in\big(\frac{\pi}{2}\,(1-\beta^{2}),\frac{\pi}{2}\,\big]

and
\varpi\in\big[\,\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\,(1+\beta^{2})\big)
map to
x\in[\,0,1)
and
x\in(-1,0\,]
, respectively.
Moreover, a comparison of (
12.33
) with the asymptotic formula (
B.12
)
yields

s_{n}^{(\pm)}=f^{1-\beta^{2}}\,\mu_{n}^{(\pm)}\,.

(12.38)

Here
f=f(x)
, given by (
B
),
is a real and positive number.

Relation (
12.38
) can be checked using the sum rules.
For any given solution to the Bethe ansatz equations (
12.16
) consider
the finite sum

h^{(N)}_{1}=\sum_{n=1}^{M}(\zeta_{n})^{-1}\,.

(12.39)

For the class of low energy states
it turns out that the following limit exists

h^{(\infty)}_{1}=\lim_{N\to\infty}N^{-(1-\beta^{2})}\bigg(h_{1}^{(N)}-\frac{N}{2}\,\frac{\cos(\varpi)}{\sin(\frac{\pi\beta^{2}}{2})}\,\bigg)\,.

(12.40)

Without going into details, we just mention that the existence of the
limit comes from the Bethe ansatz equations.
Our main conjecture implies that for a low energy state,

h_{1}^{(\infty)}
appears in the first term of the Taylor expansion
of the corresponding spectral determinant. In view of (
9.89
),
as well as the
\mu-\lambda
relation specialized to
K=2
(see eq.(
9.86
)),
the latter can be expressed in terms
of the eigenvalues of the non-local integrals of motion
{\bf H}_{n}^{(+)}
:

\log\big(D_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}(\mu)\big)\,=\,\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{n-1}\,\big(\tfrac{2}{1-\beta^{2}}\big)^{-n(1-\beta^{2})}\ \Gamma^{-2n}\big(\tfrac{1-\beta^{2}}{2}\big)\ {H}_{n}^{(+)}\ \mu^{n}\,.

(12.41)

Combined with the relation (
12.38
), this yields

h_{1}^{({\infty})}=\bigg(\frac{1-\beta^{2}}{2f}\bigg)^{1-\beta^{2}}\frac{{H}_{1}^{(+)}}{\Gamma^{2}\big(\tfrac{1-\beta^{2}}{2}\big)}\ .

(12.42)

Though the sum in (
12.39
) goes over all the
\zeta_{n}
,
the contribution of the roots at the left edge of the distribution
becomes negligible as
N\to\infty
.
Thus the limit (
12.40
) encodes the scaling properties
of the right edge roots.
The similar characteristic can be introduced for the left edge roots:

\bar{h}_{1}^{(\infty)}=\lim_{N\to\infty}N^{-(1-\beta^{2})}\bigg(\sum_{n=1}^{M}\zeta_{n}-\frac{N}{2}\,\frac{\cos(\varpi)}{\sin(\frac{\pi\beta^{2}}{2})}\,\bigg)\,.

(12.43)

Then

\bar{h}_{1}^{({\infty})}=\bigg(\frac{1-\beta^{2}}{2f}\bigg)^{1-\beta^{2}}\frac{\bar{H}_{1}^{(+)}}{\Gamma^{2}\big(\tfrac{1-\beta^{2}}{2}\big)}\ ,

(12.44)

where
\bar{H}_{1}^{(+)}
is the eigenvalue of the non-local IM
corresponding to the second chirality.

The sum rules (
12.42
) and (
12.44
) can be applied to
the Bethe roots corresponding to the
ground state of the Hamiltonian

\mathbb{H}
in the sector
S^{z}=0
with even
N/2
. We found that
in the scaling limit it becomes the state

\displaystyle\bar{{\bf e}}_{0,0}\big(\bar{P}^{(\rm vac)}_{\rm NS}\big)\otimes{\bf e}_{0,0}\big({P}^{(\rm vac)}_{\rm NS}\big)

(12.45)

with

\displaystyle{P}^{(\rm vac)}_{\rm NS}=-\bar{P}^{(\rm vac)}_{\rm NS}=\frac{{\tt k}}{\sqrt{2}\beta}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \big(\tfrac{1}{2}\leq{\tt k}<\tfrac{1}{2}\big)\ .

(12.46)

The numerical data in support of the identification is presented in fig.
2
.

Consider the formula describing the scaling limit of the Hamiltonian (
12.24
) specialized to the ground state.
The extensive part of the energy is determined by the density of the bulk roots (
12.30
).
A standard computation results in the expression

e_{\infty}=-\frac{\cos\big(\frac{\pi\beta^{2}}{2}\big)}{\pi\big(1-\beta^{2}\big)}\ \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\mbox{d}\theta}{\cosh\big(\frac{\theta}{1-\beta^{2}}\big)}\bigg(\,\frac{1}{\cosh(\theta)-\sin\big(\frac{\pi\beta^{2}}{2}\big)}+\frac{1}{\cosh(\theta-2{\rm i}\varpi)-\sin\big(\frac{\pi\beta^{2}}{2}\big)}\,\bigg)\vphantom{\sum_{a\atop b}}

(12.47)

As was already mentioned, the Fermi velocity depends on the overall normalization of the Hamiltonian.
With the definition (
12.22
) and (
12.23
), it turns out that
v_{\rm F}
does
not depend on the inhomogeneities and is given by

v_{{\rm F}}=\frac{2}{1-\beta^{2}}\qquad\qquad\qquad(r=2)\ .

(12.48)

From eqs. (
12.46
) and (
12.24
), one has the prediction

N/2-{\rm even},\ S^{z}=0\ :\ \ \lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{N}{4\pi v_{\rm F}}\ \big({\cal E}_{\rm NS}^{(\rm vac)}-N\,e_{\infty}\big)=-\frac{1}{6}+\frac{{\tt k}^{2}}{\beta^{2}}\,\ \ \ \ \ \ \big(\tfrac{1}{2}\leq{\tt k}<\tfrac{1}{2}\big)

(12.49)

The latter has been confirmed via numerical work (see fig.
2
).

For odd
N/2
with
S^{z}=0
the ground state is doubly degenerate.
Again, using the sum rules, one finds that the states in the scaling limit can
be identified with (some details can be found in fig.
3
)

\displaystyle\bar{{\bf e}}^{(-)}_{2,0}\big(\bar{P}^{(\rm vac)}_{\rm NS}\big)\otimes{\bf e}_{0,0}\big({P}^{(\rm vac)}_{\rm NS}\big)\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \bar{{\bf e}}_{0,0}\big(\bar{P}^{(\rm vac)}_{\rm NS}\big)\otimes{\bf e}^{(-)}_{2,0}\big({P}^{(\rm vac)}_{\rm NS}\big)\ .

(12.50)

In this case,

N/2-{\rm odd},\ S^{z}=0\ :\ \ \lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{N}{4\pi v_{\rm F}}\ \big({\cal E}_{\rm NS}^{\rm(vac)}-N\,e_{\infty}\big)=+\frac{1}{3}+\frac{{\tt k}^{2}}{\beta^{2}}\,\ \ \ \ \ \quad\big(\,\tfrac{1}{2}\leq{\tt k}<\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big)

(12.51)

There is a doublet of first excited states, whose energy in the scaling limit
is described by the same formula. Clearly, they correspond to

\displaystyle\bar{{\bf e}}^{(+)}_{2,0}\big(\bar{P}^{(\rm vac)}_{\rm NS}\big)\otimes{\bf e}_{0,0}\big({P}^{(\rm vac)}_{\rm NS}\big)\ ,\qquad\ \ \ \ \ \ \bar{{\bf e}}_{0,0}\big(\bar{P}^{(\rm vac)}_{\rm NS}\big)\otimes{\bf e}^{(+)}_{2,0}\big({P}^{(\rm vac)}_{\rm NS}\big)\ .

(12.52)

In making the above identifications it was assumed that
0<x<1
.
In the domain
-1<x<0
, the picture is reversed. The ground states flow to (
12.52
),
while the first excited states become (
12.50
).

For even
N/2
and
S^{z}=0
the first excited state is also two-fold degenerate.
Applying the sum rules, one finds that in the scaling limit they become

\displaystyle\bar{{\bf e}}_{1,+1}\big(\bar{P}^{(\rm vac)}_{\rm R}\big)\otimes{\bf e}_{1,+1}\big({P}^{(\rm vac)}_{\rm R}\big)\,,\qquad\qquad\bar{{\bf e}}_{1,-1}\big(\bar{P}^{(\rm vac)}_{\rm R}\big)\otimes{\bf e}_{1,-1}\big({P}^{(\rm vac)}_{\rm R}\big)

(12.53)

for
-1<x<1
. Here

\displaystyle{P}^{(\rm vac)}_{\rm R}=-\bar{P}^{(\rm vac)}_{\rm R}=\frac{{\tt k}-\frac{1}{2}}{\sqrt{2}\beta}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \big(0\leq{\tt k}\leq 1\big)\ .

(12.54)

As for the energy, one has

N/2-{\rm even},\ S^{z}=0\ :\ \ \lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{N}{4\pi v_{\rm F}}\ \big({\cal E}_{\rm R}^{(\rm vac)}-N\,e_{\infty}\big)=+\frac{1}{12}+\frac{({\tt k}-\frac{1}{2})^{2}}{\beta^{2}}\,\ \ \ \ \ \quad(0\leq{\tt k}<1)\ .

(12.55)

When studying the scaling limit, it is apparent how to
assign an
N
dependence to the ground state and perhaps the first few excited
states in the disjoint sectors of the Hilbert space. These sectors, in the
case under consideration, are distinguished by the value of the quantum number

S^{z}
and the parity of
N/2
.
However,
for a general lattice system there are difficulties in introducing the
N
-dependence,
i.e., the Renormalization Group (RG) flow
for an individual stationary state. Of course,
since the Hilbert space is not isomorphic for different lattice sizes,
the problem only makes sense for the low energy part of the spectrum.
But even then, forming
individual RG flow trajectories is not a trivial task.
For integrable spin chains, there is a procedure for assigning an
N
dependence
to a Bethe state that
relies explicitly on the Bethe ansatz equations (for a discussion see, e.g., ref.
Bazhanov:2019xvyA
).

{h}_{1}^{(N,{\rm reg})}
\theta
\Im m(\theta)=+\tfrac{1}{2}\varpi
\Im m(\theta)=-\tfrac{1}{2}\varpi
N

Figure 3
:
The left panel shows the typical pattern of Bethe roots
in the complex
\theta
plane
\big(\theta=-\frac{1}{2}\log(\zeta)\big)
for
the ground state of
\mathbb{H}
with
N/2
odd and
S^{z}=0
, which flows
to

\bar{{\bf e}}_{0,0}\big(\bar{P}^{(\rm vac)}_{\rm NS}\big)\otimes{\bf e}^{(-)}_{2,0}\big({P}^{(\rm vac)}_{\rm NS}\big)
.
Note that the right most root, located far above the real axis,
has
\Im m(\theta)=\frac{\pi}{2}
.
The right panel
presents numerical data for

h_{1}^{(N,{\rm reg})}=N^{-(1-\beta^{2})}\big(h_{1}^{(N)}-\frac{N}{2}\,\frac{\cos(\varpi)}{\sin(\pi\beta^{2}/2)}\,\big)
. The crosses were obtained from the solution
of the Bethe ansatz equations,
while the solid blue line represents the fit
-1.3582-0.12659/N^{2}
.
Formula (
12.42
) relates the limiting value
\lim_{N\to\infty}h_{1}^{(N,{\rm reg})}

to the eigenvalue of the first non-local IM corresponding to the state

{\bf e}^{(-)}_{2,0}\big({P}^{(\rm vac)}_{\rm NS}\big)
. This eigenvalue is
not available in analytical form.
A numerical integration of the ODE (
7.7
) corresponding to

{\bf e}^{(-)}_{2,0}

leads to the prediction

h_{1}^{(\infty)}=-1.35817
, which is depicted by the black dashed line.
The parameters were set to be

\beta^{2}=0.43
,
{\tt k}=0.1
and
\varpi=1.3
.

In the general set-up with
S^{z}\geq 0
and both even or odd
N/2
,
the low energy
stationary states in the scaling limit
can be classified according to the irreps of the
\overline{W}_{\bm{1}}^{(c,2)}\otimes{W}_{\bm{1}}^{(c,2)}

algebra. They are splitted into
the Neveu-Schwarz and Ramond sectors.
The
Neveu-Schwarz states are those, which have the form

\bar{\bm{e}}_{\bar{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},{\mathfrak{m}},\bar{P}_{\rm NS}}(\bar{\bm{\gamma}};\bar{\bm{w}})\otimes{\bm{e}}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},{\mathfrak{m}},P_{\rm NS}}({\bm{\gamma}};\bm{w})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\rm with}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\bar{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}}},{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}}\in\big\{(0,0),\,(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})\big\}\,,\quad\mathfrak{m}=0\,.

(12.56)

The admissible values of
P_{\rm NS}
and
{\bar{P}}_{\rm NS}
are given by

\displaystyle 2P_{\rm NS}=\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{2}}\ \,S^{z}+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\beta}\ \big({\tt k}+{\tt w}\big)\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ 2\bar{P}_{\rm NS}=\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{2}}\ \,S^{z}-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\beta}\ \big({\tt k}+{\tt w}\big)\,,

(12.57)

where

\displaystyle-{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\leq{\tt k}<{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\ ;\ \ \qquad\ {\tt w}=0,\,\pm 1,\pm 2,\ldots\ .

(12.58)

In turn, the Ramond states are described as

\bar{\bm{e}}_{\bar{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},\bar{P}_{\rm R}}(\bar{\bm{\gamma}};\bar{\bm{w}})\otimes{\bm{e}}_{\bm{\mathfrak{j}},\mathfrak{m},P_{\rm R}}({\bm{\gamma}};\bm{w})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\rm with}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\bar{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}}},{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}}\in\big\{(\tfrac{1}{2},0),\,(0,\tfrac{1}{2})\big\}\,,\quad\mathfrak{m}=1\,.

(12.59)

Here

\displaystyle 2P_{\rm R}=\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{2}}\ \,S^{z}+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\beta}\ \big({\tt k}-\tfrac{1}{2}+{\tt w}\big)\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ 2\bar{P}_{\rm R}=\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{2}}\ \,S^{z}-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\beta}\ \big({\tt k}-\tfrac{1}{2}+{\tt w}\big)\,,

(12.60)

while

\displaystyle 0\leq{\tt k}<1\ ;\ \ \ \ {\tt w}=0,\,\pm 1,\pm 2,\ldots\ .

(12.61)

The following comment is in order here. The transfer-matrix and the Hamiltonian
essentially depend only on the unimodular parameter
\omega^{2}
(
12.13
).
Taking the logarithm results in

\log(\omega^{2})=2\pi{\rm i}\,({\tt k}+{\tt w})

(12.62)

with
{\tt w}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
The parameter

{\tt k}
can be brought to lie in any segment of unit length, which
is usually referred to as the “first Brillouin zone”.
Formulae (
12.58
) and (
12.61
) mean that for
the Neveu-Schwarz and Ramond sectors, the first Brillouin zone should be chosen
in a different way, to be

\big[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\big)

and
[\,0,1)
, respectively. The integer

{\tt w}
(the winding number), which labels the bands of the spectrum, is a
quantum number that appears only in the scaling limit.
It is worth reiterating that
for finite
N
this quantum number is not well defined.

Let
|\bm{\Psi}_{N}\rangle
be a trajectory describing the RG flow of some low energy Bethe
state. For fixed
N
it is an eigenstate of the matrices
\mathbb{H}^{(a)}
(
12.22
)
and we denote by
{\cal E}^{(a)}_{N}
the corresponding eigenvalues.
Formula (
12.24
) implies

\displaystyle\frac{N}{4\pi v_{\rm F}}\,\Big(\,{\cal E}^{(1)}_{N}+{\cal E}^{(2)}_{N}-e_{\infty}\,N\,\Big)\,\asymp\,I_{1}^{(e)}+\bar{I}_{1}^{(e)}+o(1)

(12.63)

with
v_{\rm F}=2\,(1+\xi)
,
e_{\infty}
as in (
12.47
) and
I_{1}^{(e)}

(
\bar{I}_{1}^{(e)}
) stands for the
eigenvalues of the right (left) local IM
(
9.18
). In general,

I_{1}^{(e)}=I_{1}^{(e,{\rm vac})}(P)+{\tt L}\,,\qquad\qquad\bar{I}_{1}^{(e)}=I_{1}^{(e,{\rm vac})}(\bar{P})+\bar{{\tt L}}\,,

(12.64)

where the vacuum eigenvalues
I_{1}^{(e,{\rm vac})}(P)
correspond to the

{W}_{\bm{1}}^{(c,2)}
primary states and
are listed in the fourth column of tab.
1
, while
{\tt L}
,
\bar{\tt L}
are non-negative
integers.
The admissible values of
P
,
\bar{P}
are given by eqs. (
12.57
) and (
12.60
)
in the Neveu-Schwarz and Ramond sectors, respectively. Notice that the relation (
12.63
)
does not depend on the inhomogeneity parameters
\eta_{a}
.
The inhomogeneous six-vertex model with
r=2
and
\eta_{1}=-\eta_{2}={\rm i}

possesses an additional global
{\cal Z}_{2}
symmetry.
This model was studied in the work
IJS2
. Among other things,
based on the modular invariance of the CFT
partition function (for
{\tt k}=0
),
the authors discuss
the gluing conditions for the right and left chiralities.
Their results are also applicable to the more general case with the inhomogeneities as in
eqs. (
12.25
) and (
12.26
).

Contrary to the eigenvalues of the operator
\mathbb{H}
(
12.23
), i.e.,

{\cal E}^{(1)}_{N}+{\cal E}^{(2)}_{N}
the scaling part of the eigenvalues of
each
\mathbb{H}^{(a)}
individually
contain an explicit dependence on the inhomogeneities.
One can obtain the asymptotic formula

\displaystyle\frac{N}{4\pi v_{\rm F}}\,\big(\,{\cal E}^{(1)}_{N}-{\cal E}^{(2)}_{N}\,\big)

\displaystyle\asymp

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm i}\,\xi(1+\xi)}{2\pi}\ f\partial_{x}f\,\big(\,I_{1}^{(e)}-\bar{I}_{1}^{(e)}\,\big)

(12.65)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{\xi(1+\xi)^{2}f^{2}}{2\pi\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}\ \sqrt{2}\beta\,\big(\,I_{1}^{(o)}-\bar{I}_{1}^{(o)}\,\big)+o(1)\,,

where
\xi=\frac{\beta^{2}}{1-\beta^{2}}
and
f=f(x)
is given by (
B
). Note that

\displaystyle f(0)=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{1+\xi}\ \frac{\Gamma(\frac{\xi}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{1+\xi}{2})}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \qquad\ \partial_{x}f(0)=0\ .

(12.66)

An illustration for the Bethe states, which flow
to the tensor product of the Ramond primary states (
12.53
), is provided
in fig.
4
.

\theta
\Im m(\theta)=+\tfrac{1}{2}\varpi
\Im m(\theta)=-\tfrac{1}{2}\varpi
N
\tfrac{N}{4\pi v_{\rm F}}\big({\cal E}^{(1)}_{N}-{\cal E}^{(2)}_{N}\big)

Figure 4
:
The first excited states of the Hamiltonian
\mathbb{H}
with
N/2
even and
S^{z}=0

form a doublet. The Bethe roots for the states in the doublet are related to each other
via complex conjugation. Depicted in the left panel of the figure is the typical pattern of Bethe
roots in the complex
\theta
plane
for the Bethe state, which flows to
\bar{{\bf e}}_{1,+1}\big(\bar{P}^{(\rm vac)}_{\rm R}\big)\otimes{\bf e}_{1,+1}\big({P}^{(\rm vac)}_{\rm R}\big)
. The right panel compares the numerical data
for the difference
{\cal E}^{(1)}_{N}-{\cal E}^{(2)}_{N}
with the prediction (
12.65
). The crosses
were computed via the Bethe ansatz, while the solid blue line is the fit

0.72858-2.9919/N^{2}
. The dashed black line represents the r.h.s. of eq. (
