).
Note that, in the case at hand,
I_{1}^{(e)}-\bar{I}_{1}^{(e)}
is zero, while the eigenvalue of

{\bf I}_{1}^{(o)}
on the Ramond primary states follows from eq. (
9.1
)
and the last column of tab.
1
. The parameters were set to be

\beta^{2}=0.43
,
{\tt k}=0.1
and
\varpi=1.8
, so that

P_{\rm R}^{{\rm vac})}=-\bar{P}_{\rm R}^{({\rm vac})}=-0.43133
.

To determine the scaling limit of
|\bm{\Psi}_{N}\rangle
one could employ formulae
(
12.63
) and (
12.65
).
However, unlike the sum rules, they contain the contribution of
the eigenvalues of both right and left IM
{\bf I}_{1}^{(e,o)}
and
\bar{{\bf I}}_{1}^{(e,o)}
.
Nevertheless, their computation is a much
simpler task than that of the eigenvalues of the non-local IM.
Perhaps the most convenient way of identifying the scaling limit
of an RG trajectory involves the reflection operator.
Its eigenvalues admit a closed expression (
8.4
),
while the construction of the eigenstates is a straightforward algebraic procedure.
On the other hand, the eigenvalues of the left and right reflection operators appear
separately in the large
N
limit of certain products over the Bethe roots.
Some related discussion can be found in sec. 11 of ref.
Bazhanov:2019xvyA
.
14
14

14

To avoid confusion, let us emphasize that the work

Bazhanov:2019xvyA
considered the inhomogeneous six-vertex model
for
r=2
,
where
\eta_{1}=-\eta_{2}={\rm i}
, but in the domain of the anisotropy parameter

{\rm arg}(q)\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
. This is complementary to (
12.19
)
with
K=2
, i.e.,
{\rm arg}(q)\in(\frac{\pi}{2},\pi)
.

13
Outlook

Among the lessons we took away from carrying out this project
is that the generalized affine Gaudin model fits within the standard framework
of Yang-Baxter integrability. As a result it can be studied using
conventional techniques such as the quantum inverse scattering method
and its variants. This has exciting implications, since the model
shares the same integrability hierarchy as
a variety of physically interesting systems.
The latter includes a large class of classically
integrable Non-Linear Sigma Models (NLSM). For example, in refs.
Vicedo:2017cge
;
Delduc:2018hty
;
Delduc:2019bcl

the classical affine Gaudin model (
11.14
)
and its generalizations to Lie algebras of higher rank are used in
assembling integrable NLSM.
The results of our work provide an avenue for the systematic
quantization of such theories within the ODE/IQFT correspondence,
in the spirit of ref.
Bazhanov:2017nzh
.
Another application lies in the domain of Condensed Matter Physics.
The GAGM may be understood as an
integrable multiparametric generalization of the
Kondo model.
Finally, we hope that the output of this work would be useful in the context
of the representation theory of infinite dimensional algebras.

Appendix A
Appendix: leading asymptotic of

D_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}
for
\mu\to+\infty

A few technical assumptions are made
in order to simplify the calculation.
Namely, that
(i)
both
\mu
and
\kappa=\mu^{\frac{1}{2}(1+\xi)K}

are real and positive,
(ii)
all
z_{a}\not\in(0,+\infty)
and
(iii)

0<(1+\xi)K<2
. Later we’ll discuss how the third assumption can be relaxed.

Suppose that
\mu
or, equivalently,
\kappa

is small.
As long as the term

\kappa^{2}{\cal P}

can be neglected, the ODE (
7.48
) takes the form
\big(-\partial^{2}_{z}+t_{\tt L}(z)\big)\Psi=0
and the solution

\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\leftarrow)}}_{A}
is approximated as

\displaystyle\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\leftarrow)}}_{A}(z)\approx C\ z^{\frac{1}{2}-{\rm i}A}\ \frac{\prod_{m=1}^{{\tt M}_{+}}(z-x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}_{m})}{\prod_{a=1}^{r}(z-z_{a})^{\mathfrak{j}_{a}}\prod_{\alpha=1}^{\tt L}(w-w_{\alpha})}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \big(\,|\kappa^{2}{\cal P}|\ll 1\,\big)\,.

(A.1)

To ensure the asymptotic condition
\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\leftarrow)}}_{A}(z)\to z^{\frac{1}{2}-{\rm i}A}
for
z\to 0
, the constant
C

should be set to be

\displaystyle C=\frac{\prod_{a=1}^{r}(-z_{a})^{\mathfrak{j}_{a}}\prod_{\alpha=1}^{\tt L}(-w_{\alpha})}{\prod_{m=1}^{{\tt M}_{+}}(-x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}_{m})}\ .

(A.2)

For
\kappa\ll 1
,
there is a region with
|z|
large, where
the condition
|\kappa^{2}{\cal P}|\ll 1
is still satisfied. In that domain (
A.1
) becomes

\displaystyle\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\leftarrow)}}_{A}(z)\approx C\ z^{\frac{1}{2}-{\rm i}A-\frac{1}{2}\,{\mathfrak{m}}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ (1\ll|z|\ll\mu^{-1})\ .

(A.3)

Here we use

\mathfrak{m}=2({\mathfrak{J}}+{\tt N}-{\tt M}_{+})
(see eqs. (
7.57
) and (
7.69
)).
On the other hand, taking into account (
7.53
), for large
z
one has

\displaystyle t_{\tt L}(z)=\frac{1}{4z^{2}}\ \big(2{\rm i}A+\mathfrak{m})^{2}-1\big)+O\big(z^{-3}\big)

(A.4)

so that the ODE (
7.48
) can be replaced by

\displaystyle\Big(-\partial^{2}_{z}+\frac{1}{4z^{2}}\ \big((2{\rm i}A+\mathfrak{m})^{2}-1\big)+\kappa^{2}\ z^{-2+(1+\xi)K}\Big)\ \Psi=0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ (|z|\gg 1)\ .

(A.5)

The latter is a variant of the Bessel equation. The solution
(
A.3
) can be continued to the whole domain
|z|\gg 1
:

\displaystyle\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\leftarrow)}}_{A}(z)\approx\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{(1+\xi)K}}\ \,W_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}(0)\ \,\mu^{{\rm i}A+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{m}}\ z^{\frac{1}{2}}\ I_{\nu}\Big(\tfrac{2\kappa}{(1+\xi)K}\ z^{\frac{1}{2}(1+\xi)K}\Big)\ ,

(A.6)

where we use the notation

\displaystyle W_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}(0)\equiv C\ \sqrt{\frac{(1+\xi)K}{\pi}}\ \ \Gamma\big(1+\nu)\ \big((1+\xi)K\big)^{\nu}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \nu=-\frac{2{\rm i}A+\mathfrak{m}}{(1+\xi)K}\ .

(A.7)

Keeping in mind the asymptotic behaviour of the modified Bessel function at large argument,

I_{\nu}(x)\asymp\mbox{e}^{x}\big/\sqrt{2\pi x}
, one finds

\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\leftarrow)}}_{A}(z)\asymp\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\kappa}}\ W_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}(0)\,\mu^{{\rm i}A+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{m}}\ \ z^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{4}K(1+\xi)}\ \exp\Big(+\tfrac{2\kappa}{(1+\xi)K}\ z^{\frac{1}{2}(1+\xi)K}\Big)\ \ \ \ {\rm as}\ \ \ z\to+\infty\,.

(A.8)

Recall that the other solution
\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\rightarrow)}}(z)
was defined through the condition
15
15

15

The assumption
0<(1+\xi)K<2
is being used here.

\displaystyle\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\rightarrow)}}(z)\asymp\frac{1}{\sqrt{\kappa}}\ z^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{4}(1+\xi)K}\ \exp\Big(-\tfrac{2\kappa}{(1+\xi)K}\ z^{\frac{1}{2}(1+\xi)K}\Big)\ \ \ \ \ {\rm as}\ \ \ z\to+\infty\,.

(A.9)

Calculating the Wronskian of these two solutions at
z\to+\infty
one arrives at the relation

\displaystyle\lim_{\mu\to 0^{+}}\,\mu^{-{\rm i}A-\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{M}}\ {\rm Wron}\big[\,\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\rightarrow)}},\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\leftarrow)}}_{A}\,\big]=W_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}(0)

(A.10)

with

W_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}(0)=\frac{(-1)^{\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{m}}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\ \big((1+\xi)K\big)^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2{\rm i}A+\mathfrak{m}}{(1+\xi)K}}\,\Gamma\big(1-\tfrac{2{\rm i}A+\mathfrak{m}}{(1+\xi)K})\ \frac{\prod_{a=1}^{r}(z_{a})^{\mathfrak{j}_{a}}\prod_{\alpha=1}^{\tt L}w_{\alpha}}{\prod_{m=1}^{{\tt M}_{+}}x^{\scriptscriptstyle{(+)}}_{m}}\ .

(A.11)

A similar formula can be obtained for
W_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},-\mathfrak{m},-A}(0)
.

Let’s turn to the large
\mu
behaviour of

{\rm Wron}\big[\,\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\rightarrow)}},\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\leftarrow)}}_{A}\,\big]
.
With the Langer correction taken into account,
the WKB approximation for
\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\rightarrow)}}
reads as

\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\rightarrow)}}\asymp\frac{1}{\sqrt{\kappa}}\ \big({\cal S}(z)\big)^{-\frac{1}{4}}\exp\bigg[-\frac{2\kappa}{(1+\xi)K}\ z^{\frac{1}{2}(1+\xi)K}+\kappa\int_{z}^{+\infty}\mbox{d}z\ \Big(\,\sqrt{{\cal S}(z)}-z^{\frac{1}{2}(1+\xi)K-1}\,\Big)\bigg]\,,\vphantom{\sum_{a\atop b}}

(A.12)

where

\displaystyle{\cal S}(z)={\cal P}(z)+\kappa^{-2}\ \Big(t_{\tt L}(z)+\frac{1}{4z^{2}}\Big)\,.

(A.13)

The other solution
\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\leftarrow)}}_{A}
is approximated by

\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\leftarrow)}}_{A}(z)\approx\ \frac{\tilde{C}}{\sqrt{\kappa}}\ z^{-{\rm i}A}\,\big({\cal S}(z)\big)^{-\frac{1}{4}}\exp\bigg[\int_{0}^{z}\mbox{d}z\ \Big(\,\kappa\sqrt{{\cal S}(z)}-\,\frac{{\rm i}A}{z}\Big)\bigg]

(A.14)

with some constant
\tilde{C}
.
Computing the Wronskian of
the solutions
\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\rightarrow)}}
and
\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\leftarrow)}}_{A}

at
z\to 0
, yields

\displaystyle\kappa^{-1}\log\bigg(\frac{{\rm Wron}[\,\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\rightarrow)}},\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\leftarrow)}}_{A}\,]}{2\tilde{C}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\asymp

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\bigg[-\frac{{\rm i}A}{\kappa}\,\log(\varepsilon)

(A.15)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{\varepsilon}^{+\infty}\mbox{d}z\ \Big(\,\sqrt{{\cal S}(z)}-z^{\frac{1}{2}(1+\xi)K-1}\,\Big)\bigg]+O(\kappa^{-2})

In order to determine
\tilde{C}
we note that for large
\mu
and

|z|\ll 1
, the function
\kappa^{2}{\cal P}(z)
can be replaced by

\kappa^{2}\,z^{-2+\xi K}
(here we use the convention
\prod_{a=1}^{r}(-z_{a})^{k_{a}}=1
,
see (
8.1
)). In turn, the
ODE (
7.48
) becomes

\displaystyle\Big(-\partial^{2}_{z}-\frac{1}{4z^{2}}\ \big(4A^{2}+1\big)+\kappa^{2}\ z^{-2+\xi K}\Big)\ \Psi=0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ (\,|z|\ll 1\,)\ .

(A.16)

Suppose that
\Im m(A)>0
. Then

\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\leftarrow)}}_{A}(z)\approx\Gamma(1+\rho)\ \big(\xi K/\kappa\big)^{\rho}\ \ z^{\frac{1}{2}}\ I_{\rho}\big(\,\tfrac{2\kappa}{\xi K}\ z^{\frac{1}{2}\xi K}\,\big)\qquad\qquad{\rm with}\qquad\rho=-\tfrac{2{\rm i}A}{\xi K}\ .

(A.17)

In the domain where the argument of the Bessel function is large

\Psi^{\scriptscriptstyle{(\leftarrow)}}_{A}(z)\approx\frac{\Gamma(1-\tfrac{2{\rm i}A}{\xi K})}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\,\bigg(\frac{\kappa}{\xi K}\bigg)^{\frac{2{\rm i}A}{\xi K}-\frac{1}{2}}\,z^{\frac{1}{4}(2-\xi K)}\,\exp\bigg(\frac{2\kappa}{\xi K}\,z^{\frac{1}{2}\xi K}\bigg)\,\qquad\big(\mu^{-\frac{1+\xi}{\xi}}\ll z\ll 1\big)\,.

(A.18)

Comparing the above equation with
the WKB formula (
A.14
), one finds

\tilde{C}\,=\,\frac{\mu^{{\rm i}\frac{\xi+1}{\xi}A}}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\ \big(\xi K\big)^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2{\rm i}A}{\xi K}}\ \Gamma\big(1-\tfrac{2{\rm i}A}{\xi K}\big)\,.

(A.19)

The relations (
A.15
), (
A.10
), (
A.11
),
combined
with the definition of
D_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}(\mu)
(
7.13
), yields the
large
\mu
asymptotic formula

D_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}\asymp R_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}\ \mu^{\frac{{\rm i}A}{\xi}-\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{m}}\ \ \exp\Big(\mu^{\frac{1}{2}(1+\xi)K}\ q_{-1}+o(1)\Big)\ \ \ \ \ \ \qquad\ (\mu\to+\infty)\ ,

(A.20)

where

\displaystyle q_{-1}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mbox{d}z\,\Big(\,\sqrt{{\cal P}(z)}-z^{\frac{1}{2}(1+\xi)K-1}\,\Big)

(A.21)

and

R_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}
is given by (
8.4
).

The asymptotic coefficient
q_{-1}
is expressed in terms of an
integral, which converges only for
0<(1+\xi)K<2
. Keeping in mind that

K
is a positive integer,
it can be literally applied to the case
K=1
and
0<\xi<1
.
Nevertheless, the
integral for
q_{-1}
can be analytically continued from the domain of convergency. This is done
by replacing the integration over the positive real semiaxis by that over a certain contour integral.
To illustrate the procedure, consider the case
r=1
when

\displaystyle{\cal P}(z)=z^{-2+\xi K}(1+z)^{K}\ .

(A.22)

Then for
\xi K>0,\ \xi(K+1)<2
the integral (
A.21
) converges and coincides with the
Euler beta function

q_{-1}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mbox{d}z\,\Big(\,\sqrt{{\cal P}(z)}-z^{\frac{1}{2}(1+\xi)K-1}\,\Big)=\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{\xi K}{2}\big)\,\Gamma\big(-\frac{(1+\xi)K}{2}\big)}{\Gamma\big(-\frac{K}{2}\big)}\ \ \ \ \qquad(r=1)\ .

(A.23)

The latter admits a well known integral representation,

\displaystyle\oint_{C_{\zeta}}\mbox{d}\zeta\ \zeta^{\alpha-1}(1-\zeta)^{\beta-1}=\big(1-\mbox{e}^{2\pi{\rm i}\alpha}\big)\,\big(1-\mbox{e}^{2\pi{\rm i}\beta}\big)\ \frac{\Gamma(\alpha)\Gamma(\beta)}{\Gamma(\alpha+\beta)}\ ,

(A.24)

where
the integration contour
C_{\zeta}

is the Pochhammer loop shown in fig.
5
.

Figure 5
:
The Pochhammer loop
C_{\zeta}
appearing in (
A.24
).

The branch of the integrand in (
A.24
) should be chosen in such a way that it is real and positive
within the segment
\zeta\in(0,1)
.
Performing the change of variables,

\displaystyle\zeta\mapsto z=\frac{\zeta}{1-\zeta}\ ,

(A.25)

the coefficient
q_{-1}
in (
A.23
) may be represented as

\displaystyle q_{-1}=\frac{1}{(1-\mbox{e}^{2\pi{\rm i}\nu_{\infty}})\ (1-\mbox{e}^{2\pi{\rm i}\nu_{0}})}\ \oint_{{\cal C}}\mbox{d}z\,\sqrt{{\cal P}(z)}

(A.26)

with

\displaystyle\nu_{0}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\xi K-1\ ,\ \ \ \ \qquad\ \nu_{\infty}=1-\tfrac{1}{2}\,(1+\xi)K\ .

(A.27)

The integration contour
{\cal C}
is the image of
C_{\zeta}
under the
conformal map (
A.25
).
Alternatively it can be described as follows.
Consider the Riemann sphere under stereographic projection
such that the south and north poles are mapped to

z=0
and
z=\infty
, respectively.
Then
{\cal C}

is the image of
a Pochhammer loop on the sphere
that
winds around the two poles.
The homotopy class of that loop is schematically depicted in fig.
1
.
Notice that

\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{\infty}
are the exponents, which
appear in the leading behaviour of
\sqrt{{\cal P}(z)}
at
z=0
and
z=\infty
:

\displaystyle\sqrt{{\cal P}(z)}\big|_{z\to 0}\sim z^{\nu_{0}}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \qquad\sqrt{{\cal P}(z)}\big|_{z\to\infty}\sim z^{-\nu_{\infty}}\,.

(A.28)

For the general case with
r>1
, the analytic continuation of
q_{-1}
is given
by equations (
A.26
) and (
A.27
). However
one should take care that the integration contour
{\cal C}
does not
wind around the points
z=z_{a}
.
This can be achieved by taking
{\cal C}
to lie inside the domain
{\cal D}
, which is the
union of
|z|>\varepsilon^{-1}
,
|z|<\varepsilon

and the wedge
|\arg(z)|<\delta
such that
z_{a}\not\in{\cal D}
.
Also in the case
r>1
the
branch of
\sqrt{{\cal P}(z)}
can not be chosen to be real for positive real
z
. Instead it
is sufficient to require that
\sqrt{{\cal P}(z)}
asymptotically approaches to positive real values as
z\to+\infty
.
Under the above conditions formulae (
A.26
), (
A.27
) provide an
analytical continuation of the asymptotic coefficient
q_{-1}
.
In turn, (
A.20
) becomes applicable for
\xi>0
and any
positive integers
k_{a}
, provided that

\displaystyle\xi K,\ (\xi+1)K\not\in 2\mathbb{Z}\ \qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\Big(K=\sum_{a=1}^{r}k_{a}\Big)\ .

(A.29)

Appendix B
Appendix: large
|\mu|
asymptotic for
r=2,\,k_{1}=k_{2}=1

Leading asymptotic

The asymptotic behaviour of the connection
coefficient, as described by (
A.20
),
assumes that
\mu
is a large, positive real number.
A full description of the large
|\mu|
asymptotic in the complex plane for generic values
of the complex parameters
z_{a}
is more difficult to obtain.
Assuming certain reality conditions imposed on
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
,
we describe it here for
the case
r=2,\,k_{1}=k_{2}=1
.

Adopting the conventions (
8.1
), so that
z_{1}z_{2}=1
,
introduce

\displaystyle x=\tfrac{1}{2}\,(z_{1}+z_{2})\ .

(B.1)

Then

\displaystyle{\cal P}(z)=z^{2(\xi-1)}\ \big(z^{2}-2xz+1\big)\ .

(B.2)

The asymptotic coefficient
q_{-1}
, being considered as a function
of
x
, can be expressed in terms of the Gauss
hypergeometric function (in fact the Legendre function
P^{-1}_{-1-\xi}
)

q_{-1}(x)=\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi\xi)}\ (1+x)\ {}_{2}F_{1}\big(1+\xi,-\xi,2,\tfrac{1}{2}\,(1+x)\big)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ (\xi\not=1,2,\ldots)\ .

(B.3)

A breakdown of
the leading asymptotic formula for large complex
\mu

is related to the lines along which the zeroes of the entire function

D_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}(\mu)
accumulate asymptotically.
In the case under consideration the positions of the Stokes lines significantly depend on the
domain of
x
.
Below we take
x^{2}
to be a real number.

For
-1<x<1
(equivalently
|z_{1,2}|=1
),
the zeroes of
D_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}(\mu)

asymptotically approach the two rays

\displaystyle\arg(\mu)=\pm\alpha_{0}\ ,

(B.4)

where
\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{0}(x)
is some monotonically
decreasing function of
x
such that

\frac{\pi}{2(1+\xi)}<\alpha_{0}(x)\leq\frac{\pi}{2}
for
0\leq x<1
, while

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}(-x)=\pi-\alpha_{0}(x)\,.

(B.5)

The rays (
B.4
) divide the complex
\mu
plane
into two wedges (see left panel of fig.
6
).

0<x<1
x>1
\mu
\mu

Figure 6
:
The typical pattern of zeroes of
D_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}(\mu)

in the complex
\mu
plane for the two
cases
0<x<1
and
x>1
. The numerical data was obtained
for
\mathfrak{j}_{1}=\mathfrak{j}_{2}=\mathfrak{m}=0
,
A=\tfrac{{\rm i}}{2}
and
\xi=1
,
when the connection coefficient is expressed in terms of the parabolic cylinder function.
The dashed lines mark the rays
(
B.4
) and (
B.13
) in the left and right panels, respectively.

The leading asymptotic behaviour of the connection coefficient
D_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}(\mu)

in these domains can be described through the formula:

D_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}(\mu)\asymp R_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}\ (|\mu|\,\mbox{e}^{{\rm i}\phi_{\alpha}})^{\frac{{\rm i}A}{\xi}-\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{m}}\ \exp\Big((|\mu|\mbox{e}^{{\rm i}\phi_{\alpha}})^{1+\xi}\ q_{-1}(\sigma_{\alpha}x)+o(1)\Big)\qquad\big(|\mu|\to+\infty\big)\ .

(B.6)

Here we use the notations

\sigma_{\alpha}=\begin{cases}+1\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ &{\rm for}\ \ \ |\alpha|<\alpha_{0}\\
-1\ \ \ \ \ \ \ &{\rm otherwise}\end{cases}\ ,\ \ \ \ \phi_{\alpha}=\begin{cases}\alpha\ \ \ \ \ &{\rm for}\ \ \ \ \ \ |\alpha|<\alpha_{0}\\
\alpha-\pi\ \ \ \ &{\rm for}\ \ \ \ \ \ \alpha_{0}<\alpha\leq\pi\\
\alpha+\pi\ \ \ \ &{\rm for}\ \ \ \ \ \ -\pi<\alpha<-\alpha_{0}\end{cases}

(B.7)

where
\alpha\in(-\pi,\pi]
stands for the argument of the complex number
\mu
, i.e.,
\mu=|\mu|\,\mbox{e}^{{\rm i}\alpha}
.

The position of the Stokes line, i.e., the value of the angle
\alpha_{0}
, is defined through the condition

\displaystyle\Re e\Big[\mbox{e}^{{\rm i}(1+\xi)\alpha_{0}}\ q_{-1}(x)-\mbox{e}^{{\rm i}(1+\xi)(\alpha_{0}-\pi)}\ q_{-1}(-x)\Big]=0\,,

(B.8)

which yields

\alpha_{0}(x)=\frac{\pi}{2}+\frac{1}{2{\rm i}(1+\xi)}\ \log\bigg[\frac{\mbox{e}^{-\frac{{\rm i}\pi}{2}\xi}\,q_{-1}(x)+\mbox{e}^{+\frac{{\rm i}\pi}{2}\xi}\,q_{-1}(-x)}{\mbox{e}^{+\frac{{\rm i}\pi}{2}\xi}\,q_{-1}(x)+\mbox{e}^{-\frac{{\rm i}\pi}{2}\xi}\,q_{-1}(-x)}\bigg]\ \ \ \ \ \qquad\ \ (0\leq x<1)\,.

(B.9)

Along the Stokes line
\arg(\mu)=\alpha_{0}
the large
\mu
asymptotic behaviour of the connection coefficient
reads as

\displaystyle D_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}(\mu)

\displaystyle\asymp

\displaystyle R_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}\ \big(|\mu|\,\mbox{e}^{{\rm i}(\alpha_{0}-\frac{\pi}{2})}\big)^{-\sqrt{\frac{2(1+\xi)}{\xi}}\,P}\ \exp\Big(\,\frac{g}{2f}\,|\mu|^{1+\xi}+o(1)\Big)

(B.10)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle 2\cos\Big(\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,f\ |\mu|^{1+\xi}-\pi\sqrt{\tfrac{1+\xi}{2\xi}}\ P+o(1)\Big)\qquad\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \qquad\qquad\big(|\mu|\to+\infty\big)\,.

Here

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{2}_{-1}(x)-q^{2}_{-1}(-x)-2\sin(\pi\xi)\,q_{-1}(x)q_{-1}(-x)

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sqrt{q^{2}_{-1}(x)+q^{2}_{-1}(-x)+2\cos(\pi\xi)\,q_{-1}(x)q_{-1}(-x)}

(B.11)

and instead of
A
, we use
P
such that
\frac{{\rm i}A}{\xi}-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathfrak{m}=-\sqrt{\frac{2(1+\xi)}{\xi}}\ P
,
which is assumed to be a real number. The
above asymptotic formula implies that
the position of the zeroes
\{\mu^{(\pm)}_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
with

\lim_{n\to\infty}\arg\big(\mu^{(\pm)}_{n}\big)=\pm\alpha_{0}
is described at large
n
by

\displaystyle\big|\,\mu^{(\pm)}_{n}\,\big|^{1+\xi}\asymp\frac{2\pi}{f(x)}\ \Big(n-\tfrac{1}{2}+\sqrt{\tfrac{1+\xi}{2\xi}}\ P+o(1)\,\Big)\ \ \ \ \ \ \qquad\qquad(n\gg 1)\,.

(B.12)

When
x>1

(z_{1,2}>0)

there are three rays along which the zeroes of
D_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}(\mu)
accumulate,

\displaystyle\arg(\mu)=0,\,\pm\,\tfrac{\pi}{2(1+\xi)}

(B.13)

(see right panel of fig.
6
).
The asymptotics of the connection coefficient with
\mu\to\infty
and
|\arg(\mu)|<\frac{\pi}{2(1+\xi)}
reads as

D_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}(\mu)\asymp R_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}\ (|\mu|\mbox{e}^{{\rm i}\alpha})^{\frac{{\rm i}A}{\xi}-\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{m}}\ \exp\Big((|\mu|\mbox{e}^{{\rm i}\alpha})^{1+\xi}\ q_{-1}\big(x+{\rm i}\,{\rm sgn}(\alpha)\,\varepsilon\big)+o(1)\Big)\Big|_{\varepsilon\to+0}

(B.14)

(the branch of the hypergeometric function in (
B.3
) is taken in such a way that

q_{-1}(x)
has a discontinuity for
x\in(1,+\infty)
).
In the wedge
\frac{\pi}{2(1+\xi)}<|\arg(\mu)|\leq\pi
, formula (
B.6
)
remains applicable provided
\alpha_{0}
is taken to be
\frac{\pi}{2(1+\xi)}
.

For
x<-1

(z_{1,2}<0)
the zeroes of
D_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}(\mu)

accumulate along the rays

\displaystyle\arg(\mu)=\pi,\,\pm\,\big(\pi-\tfrac{\pi}{2(1+\xi)}\big)\ .

(B.15)

In this case the large
\mu
asymptotic behaviour can be obtained from that corresponding to

x>1
by simultaneously reflecting
\mu\to-\mu
and
x\to-x
.

Finally we note that
for pure imaginary
x
, the zeroes of
D_{{\bm{\mathfrak{j}}},\mathfrak{m},A}(\mu)
accumulate along
the imaginary axis
\Im m(\mu)=0
.
In this case
(
B.6
), (
B.7
) remain valid upon setting

\alpha_{0}=\frac{\pi}{2}
therein.

Asymptotic coefficient
q_{1}

The coefficient
q_{1}
enters into the
subleading term in the large
\mu
asymptotic expansion (
8.12
).
When
r=2
and
k_{1}=k_{2}=1
it is a
linear combination of the eigenvalues of the local IM
{\bf I}_{1}^{(1)}
and
{\bf I}_{1}^{(2)}
,
weighted by factors, which are
themselves expressed
in terms of the Gauss hypergeometric function similar to (
B.3
).
It follows
from eqs.(
8.13
) and (
8.17
) that

\displaystyle q_{1}=\big(\,I_{1}^{(1)}\,z_{1}\partial_{z_{1}}+I_{1}^{(2)}\,z_{2}\partial_{z_{2}}\big)\,f_{1}\ ,

(B.16)

where

\displaystyle f_{1}=\big[\,\big(1-\mbox{e}^{2\pi{\rm i}\xi}\,\big)\ \big(1-\mbox{e}^{-2\pi{\rm i}\xi}\,\big)\,\big]^{-1}\oint_{\cal C}\frac{\mbox{d}z}{\sqrt{{\cal P}}}\ \frac{1}{z^{2}}

(B.17)

and the integration contour
{\cal C}
is the Pochhammer loop that appears in eq.(
8.3
).
A straightforward computation gives

f_{1}=\big(\sqrt{z_{1}z_{2}}\,\big)^{-1-\xi}\ \Phi\big(\tfrac{z_{1}+z_{2}}{2\sqrt{z_{1}z_{2}}}\big)

(B.18)

with

\displaystyle\Phi(x)=-\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi\xi)}\ {}_{2}F_{1}\big(-\xi,\ 1+\xi,1,\tfrac{1}{2}\,(1+x)\big)\ .

(B.19)

The convention
z_{1}z_{2}=1
, which was imposed before, is not being assumed here.
The coefficient
q_{1}
(
B.16
) is obtained
from
f_{1}
via differentiation w.r.t.
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
, which must be
treated as independent variables.
The final result can be brought to the form

q_{1}=\big(\sqrt{z_{1}z_{2}}\big)^{-1-\xi}\,\Big(\,-\tfrac{1}{2}\,(1+\xi)\ \Phi\big(\tfrac{z_{1}+z_{2}}{2\sqrt{z_{1}z_{2}}}\big)\,\big(I^{(1)}_{1}+I^{(2)}_{1}\big)+\Phi^{\prime}\big(\tfrac{z_{1}+z_{2}}{4\sqrt{z_{1}z_{2}}}\big)\ \tfrac{z_{1}-z_{2}}{4\sqrt{z_{1}z_{2}}}\ \big(I^{(1)}_{1}-I^{(2)}_{1}\big)\,\Big)\vphantom{\bigg)}

(B.20)

where together with
\Phi(x)
we use its derivative:

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime}(x)=\frac{\xi(1+\xi)}{1-x^{2}}\ q_{-1}(x)

(B.21)

with
q_{-1}
from (
B.3
).
At this point one can set
z_{1}z_{2}=1
. However
the ambiguity in the sign of the square root
\sqrt{z_{1}z_{2}}
requires special attention.
For instance,
in the case of the coefficient
q_{-1}
,
the sign
\sqrt{z_{1}z_{2}}=\pm 1
enters
explicitly into the asymptotic formulae for large complex
\mu
(e.g. the factor
\sigma_{\alpha}
in
(
B.6
)).

The local IM, whose eigenvalues coincide with the
linear combinations
I_{1}^{(e)}=-\frac{1}{4\xi}\,(I_{1}^{(1)}+I_{1}^{(2)})
and
I_{1}^{(o)}=\frac{1}{4\sqrt{2\xi(1+\xi)}}\,(I_{1}^{(1)}-I_{1}^{(2)})
, have been presented in eq. (
9.18
).
Further developing the asymptotic series one would come up against
q_{3}
(
8.13
),
which is a certain linear combination of the eigenvalues of
{\bf I}^{(e)}_{3}
and
{\bf I}^{(o)}_{3}
.
These IM are given in eq. (
9.1
) and their eigenvalues for
the primary states are listed in tab.
2
. Below, for completeness,
we quote the local fields appearing in (
9.1
)
in terms of the chiral Bose and fermion fields.

Spin 4 local fields

The local densities for the integrals of motion
{\bf I}_{2n-1}^{(a)}

are linear combinations of fields belonging to the space that was denoted by

{\cal W}^{(2n)}\subset W^{(c,2)}_{\bm{1}}
.
The space
{\cal W}^{(4)}

contains five spin 4 local fields,

\partial SS
,
\chi_{2}GS
,
G^{2}
,
\partial^{2}\chi_{2}S
and
{G}_{{\chi_{2}}}^{2}
,
which are not total derivatives.
To describe them, introduce the notation

\displaystyle G_{\pm\varphi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\partial\varphi)^{2}\pm{\rm i}\rho\ \partial^{2}\varphi\ ,\ \ \

(B.22)

\displaystyle G^{2}_{\varphi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\partial\varphi)^{4}+(1-\rho^{2})\ (\partial^{2}\varphi)^{2}+\tfrac{1}{3}\,\partial\Big(2{\rm i}\rho\,(\partial\varphi)^{3}-3\,\partial^{2}\varphi\,\partial\varphi-{\rm i}\rho\,\partial^{3}\varphi\Big)

and

\displaystyle G_{\chi_{a}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{{\rm i}}{2}\,\chi_{a}\partial\chi_{a}\ ,\ \ \ \qquad\qquad G_{\chi_{a}}^{2}=-\tfrac{7{\rm i}}{12}\ \chi_{a}\partial^{3}\chi_{a}+\tfrac{5{\rm i}}{8}\ \partial\big(\chi_{a}\partial^{2}\chi_{a}\big)\,.

(B.23)

Notice that the combination
G_{+\varphi}+G_{\chi_{1}}

is the same field as
G
from eq. (
9.6
).
The regular terms in the OPE (
9.1
) are given by

\displaystyle\partial SS

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm i}\ (G_{+\varphi}-3G_{-\varphi})\ G_{\chi_{1}}-\tfrac{{\rm i}}{2}\ \big(\partial^{2}\varphi\big)^{2}+\tfrac{{\rm i}}{7}\ \big(12\rho^{2}-1\big)\,G_{\chi_{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle{\rm i}\,\partial\big(-2{\rm i}\rho\ \partial\varphi\,G_{\chi_{1}}-\tfrac{1}{28}\ (9+4\rho^{2})\ \partial G_{\chi_{1}}-\tfrac{7}{24}\,\partial G_{+\varphi}+\tfrac{1}{24}\,\partial G_{-\varphi}\,\big)

\displaystyle G^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G_{+\varphi}^{2}+G_{\chi_{1}}^{2}+2G_{+\varphi}G_{\chi_{1}}

(B.24)

\displaystyle GS

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-{\rm i}\ (\partial\varphi)^{3}\ \chi_{1}+\rho\ \big((\partial\varphi)^{2}\ \partial\chi_{1}+\partial^{2}\varphi\partial\varphi\chi_{1}\big)+{\rm i}\rho^{2}\ \partial^{2}\varphi\partial\chi_{1}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\tfrac{{\rm i}}{4}\,\big(2\partial^{3}\varphi\chi_{1}+3\,\partial\varphi\partial^{2}\chi_{1}\big)-\tfrac{5}{12}\,\rho\ \partial^{3}\chi_{1}\ .

For computations, it is useful to express the field
\chi_{2}GS
in the form

\displaystyle\chi_{2}GS

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm i}\,\big(-(\partial\varphi)^{3}+\tfrac{1}{2}\ \partial^{3}\varphi\big)\,\chi_{2}\chi_{1}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\rho\,(\partial\varphi)^{2}\,\big(\chi_{2}\partial\chi_{1}+\chi_{1}\partial\chi_{2}\big)

(B.25)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\tfrac{3{\rm i}}{4}\ \partial\varphi\,\partial\chi_{2}\partial\chi_{1}-\tfrac{{\rm i}}{8}\,(3-4\rho^{2})\,\partial^{2}\varphi\,\partial(\chi_{2}\chi_{1})+\tfrac{5}{24}\,\rho\,\big(\partial\chi_{2}\partial^{2}\chi_{1}+\partial\chi_{1}\partial^{2}\chi_{2}\big)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\tfrac{{\rm i}}{8}\,(3-4\rho^{2})\,\partial^{2}\varphi\,\big(\chi_{1}\partial\chi_{2}+\chi_{2}\partial\chi_{1}\big)+\,\partial{\cal O}_{3}

with

\displaystyle{\cal O}_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\,\rho\,(\partial\varphi)^{2}\,\chi_{2}\chi_{1}+{\rm i}\rho^{2}\ \partial\varphi\,\chi_{2}\partial\chi_{1}+\tfrac{{\rm i}}{4}\,(3-4\rho^{2})\,\partial\varphi\,\chi_{2}\partial\chi_{1}

(B.26)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\tfrac{5}{24}\,\rho\,\partial\chi_{2}\partial\chi_{1}-\tfrac{5}{12}\,\rho\,\chi_{2}\partial^{2}\chi_{1}\ .

Also, it is convenient to re-write
\partial^{2}\chi_{2}S=\rho\partial^{2}\chi_{2}\partial\chi_{1}-{\rm i}\partial\varphi\,\partial^{2}\chi_{2}\chi_{1}
as

\displaystyle\partial^{2}\chi_{2}S

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm i}\,\partial\varphi\,\partial\chi_{2}\partial\chi_{1}+\tfrac{{\rm i}}{2}\,\partial^{2}\varphi\,\partial(\chi_{2}\chi_{1})-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\rho\,\big(\partial\chi_{2}\partial^{2}\chi_{1}+\partial\chi_{1}\partial^{2}\chi_{2}\big)+

(B.27)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\tfrac{{\rm i}}{2}\,\partial^{2}\varphi\,\big(\chi_{1}\partial\chi_{2}+\chi_{2}\partial\chi_{1}\big)+\partial\,\big(\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,\rho\,\partial\chi_{2}\partial\chi_{1}-{\rm i}\,\partial\varphi\,\partial\chi_{2}\chi_{1}\big)\ .

(6.12)
